### 2. `codex tcp-tunnel` (the hidden HTTP/3 CONNECT tunnel, #45900)

Rust added `codex-rs/tcp-tunnel` plus the hidden `codex tcp-tunnel` subcommand:
a loopback TCP listener forwarded through an HTTP/3 (MASQUE-style) CONNECT
proxy, with the bearer and optional extension headers supplied only on stdin,
`LISTENING <addr>` readiness on stdout, `AUTH_UPDATED` acknowledgements, and a
listener that survives transport loss without replaying TCP streams.

Go now implements the same surface in the new `tcptunnel` package:

- `args.go`: clap-shaped argument parsing and the policy surface -
  `ParseTrustedOrigins` (exact HTTPS origins only) and `ParseProxyTarget`
  (trusted origin, credential-free authority, nonzero port), mirroring Rust's
  `parse_trusted_origins` / `ProxyTarget::parse` rules and error texts.
- `control.go`: the bounded stdin protocol (`ReadConnectHeaders` /
  `ReadAuthToken`) with Rust's limits (64 KiB tokens, 16 KiB metadata, 16
  headers, 4 KiB values), the `x-` non-forwarding header rule, duplicate
  rejection, and failures that never echo a secret.
- `tunnel.go`: the transport - a cached QUIC connection to the proxy, one
  authenticated CONNECT per local connection, `LISTENING`/`AUTH_UPDATED`
  readiness, the draining/reconnect loop with Rust's 1s->15s backoff while
  dropping (never queueing) local connections, and stream bridging with
  half-close on local EOF.
- `cli`/`app` wiring: `codex tcp-tunnel ...` reaches `tcptunnel.Run`.

Tests: the policy/admission matrix and the clap requirement matrix mirror
Rust's `proxy_policy_and_target_admission`; the control-input tests mirror
`control_input_acknowledges_bearer_renewal_with_or_without_metadata`,
`closed_parent_pipe_finishes_even_before_proxy_readiness` and
`invalid_control_input_stops_credentials_without_exposing_secrets`; and two
end-to-end tests run against a local HTTP/3 CONNECT proxy built with
`quic-go` + `http3` in the test itself - one drives the full transport
(authenticated CONNECT, bearer renewal, transport loss, reconnect, no replay)
and one drives `Run`'s whole command surface (stdin control, `LISTENING`,
`AUTH_UPDATED`, per-connection CONNECT headers).

This is the first new third-party dependency in a while
(`github.com/quic-go/quic-go` + `qpack`); it is pure Go, so the cgo-free release
path is unaffected (`go build ./...` and the existing cross-build script).

### 3. `codex exec --json` web-search actions and results (#46319)

Rust now maps web-search actions explicitly and forwards the structured results
in the exec JSON item.

- `protocol.ThreadItem` gained `Results *[]any` (`results`), keeping Rust's
  absent-vs-empty distinction, and `WebSearchItemWithResults` is the new
  constructor (the old `WebSearchItem` delegates with no results).
- `exec`: the tool-output path forwards `web_search_results` and derives the
  human-readable detail exactly like Rust's
  `codex_core::web_search::web_search_action_detail` (page actions report the
  URL; find-in-page reports `'pattern' in url`; multi-query searches report
  `first ...`), for both the agent-item and tool-output sources.
- `turn/web_search.go` preserves a present-but-empty results array instead of
  collapsing it to JSON `null`.

Evidence: `exec.TestExecWebSearchPageActionsAndResultsSurviveJSONOutput` pins
the serialized `item.completed` event for open_page and find_in_page with
absent/empty/success/error results, mirroring Rust's
`web_search_page_actions_and_results_survive_json_output`.

### 4. Experimental `rollout/compress` RPC (#46020)

Rust added `rollout/compress`: no parameters, requires the `experimentalApi`
capability, rejects non-local thread stores, immediately acknowledges the
trigger with `{}`, and schedules a best-effort background pass that compresses
cold local rollouts even when `features.local_thread_store_compression` is off.

- `rollout/compression.go`: `SpawnRolloutCompressionWorker` /
  `RunRolloutCompression` mirror Rust's worker - the process-scoped rollout
  maintenance lock (skip when another maintenance job holds it), the
  `rollout-compression.lock` run marker under `.tmp` with its 6-hour stale
  window, the 7-day cold-file age gate, the archived-then-active scan order, the
  sibling temp file + zstd encode + decode verification, the source-state
  recheck before publishing, the no-clobber publish that keeps the source's
  mtime, and removal of the plain file only after the compressed copy is
  published.
- `appserver`: `MethodRolloutCompress` is a top-level experimental method
  (dispatch + result type + schema signature), and the runtime router forwards
  it to the thread router's handler, so the capability gate
  (`rollout/compress requires experimentalApi capability`) and the response
  shape match.
- `appserver/rollout_migration_startup.go` +
  `RuntimeRouter` startup: `MaybeSpawnRolloutCompressionOnStartup` starts the
  startup-triggered pass when `local_thread_store_compression` is enabled,
  mirroring `thread_manager.rs`.

Tests: `rollout.TestRolloutCompressionWorker*` (cold compression + lossless
readback + mtime preservation, run-marker skip, already-compressed no-clobber),
`appserver.TestRuntimeRouterRolloutCompressSchedulesBackgroundPass` (the RPC
acknowledges and the background pass compresses a cold rollout), and
`appserver.TestRolloutCompressRequiresExperimentalCapability`.

Recorded nuance: Go has no per-rollout writer lock
(`WriterLockCoordinator`), so the worker's "skipped busy" branch has no Go
caller; the maintenance lock still excludes compression from migration and
other compression runs. Go also has no non-local thread store, so Rust's
`unsupported_thread_store_operation("rollout/compress")` rejection has no Go
counterpart (`experimental_thread_store` is not modelled at all).

### 5. Personality retirement (#45809, the flag retired in the registry above)

Rust removed the feature gate from `personality = "none"` and dropped the
`personality_enabled` field from `ModelsManagerConfig`, and pinned that an
explicit empty base-instructions override stays empty.

- `model.ModelsManagerConfig.PersonalityEnabled` is gone; the
  personality-section strip now runs whenever the resolved personality is
  `none`, exactly like Rust's `with_config_overrides`.
- `ModelsManagerConfig.BaseInstructions` became `*string`, so a present-but-empty
  override is distinguishable from an absent one (Rust's `Option<String>`).
- `appserver.modelConfigForAppTurn` no longer reads the retired key.

Tests: `model.TestExplicitEmptyBaseInstructionsStayEmptyWithPersonalityNone`,
`model.TestBakedPersonalitySectionIsPreservedWithoutExplicitNone`, plus the
updated strip/override tests; `appserver`
`TestRuntimeRouterTurnStartPersonalityNoneStripsBakedSection` passes again
without the flag.

### 6. Bio-policy errors are a distinct non-retryable classification (#46306)

Rust added `CodexErrorDetails::BioPolicy` (streaming `bio_policy`, HTTP 400
`bio_policy`, wrapped WebSocket bodies), kept the server message with a
biological-risk fallback, treated it as non-retryable, and mapped it to the
app-server's `other`.

- `codexapi.ErrorBioPolicy` is a new error kind with its own message form.
- `model/responses_stream.go` classifies the streaming code (no longer a
  generic invalid request) with the fallback message, and
  `isRetryableResponsesStreamError` treats it as non-retryable.
- `model/responses_agent.go` classifies HTTP 400 policy bodies for both
  `bio_policy` and `cyber_policy` (the latter was previously only classified on
  the streaming path), preserving the message and applying the matching
  fallback, for both the plain and wrapped (`type`/`status`) shapes.
- `appserver.turnAnalyticsErrorFieldsFromAPIError` maps `ErrorBioPolicy` to the
  `other` turn error with the `bio_policy` analytics kind, mirroring Rust's
  `CoreCodexErrorInfo::BioPolicy => CodexErrorInfo::Other`.

Tests: `model.TestResponseFailedErrorClassifiesPolicyCodesLikeRust`,
`model.TestBioPolicyErrorsAreTypedAcrossStreamAndHTTPLikeRust`, and the new
`bio policy` case in
`appserver.TestTurnAnalyticsErrorFieldsFromAPIErrorSerializesDataVariantsLikeRust`.
Rust's guardian retry guardrail is N/A: Go has no `should_retry_guardian_review`
classifier to extend.

## sync39 continuation (same upstream head: 7498521d288b)

The protocol sweep from the queued lanes above started in the same pass. Two
more Rust features are landed and verified against the unchanged checkout
(`git log`: `81fee58f`).

### `thread/start.daybreakEnabled` (#45513)

The initial Daybreak choice for a persistent thread is now accepted by
`thread/start` (`ThreadStartParams.DaybreakEnabled`), staged on the created
record's metadata, rejected for ephemeral threads on both the runtime and base
thread-start paths ("daybreakEnabled is not supported for ephemeral threads"),
and reported by the start response, `thread/started`, reads, and reads after
persistence because `BuildThread` already projects the metadata field. Evidence:
`appserver.TestRuntimeRouterThreadStartCarriesInitialDaybreakChoice` (true /
false / unset across response, read, and a reload of the store) and
`appserver.TestRuntimeRouterThreadStartRejectsDaybreakForEphemeralThreads`.

### Effective collaboration mode on resume (#45519)

Rust resumed a thread with `Default`, losing a saved Plan mode, and had no
server-reported mode for reconnecting clients. Go now:

- records the collaboration mode on the persisted thread-settings snapshot
  (`rollout.AppendThreadSettingsAppliedWithSnapshot` writes
  `thread_settings.collaboration_mode`, and the runtime router passes the
  thread's saved mode when it persists a settings update),
- restores it the way Rust does: the latest thread-owned snapshot first
  (fork-copied snapshots owned by another thread are skipped), then the legacy
  `TurnContext.collaboration_mode`, then Go's compact world-state section
  (`rollout.LatestPersistedCollaborationMode`,
  `Router.latestPersistedCollaborationMode`),
- reports it as `thread/resume.collaborationMode` (absent when nothing was
  saved), and
- seeds the resumed session's settings from the restored mode so the first turn
  after a cold resume keeps using the saved Plan mode
  (`RuntimeRouter.applyThreadResumeCollaborationMode`).

Evidence: `rollout.TestLatestPersistedCollaborationModeRestoresSavedMode` (owner
matching plus the fork-copy skip),
`rollout.TestLatestPersistedCollaborationModeFallsBackToTurnContext`,
`appserver.TestThreadResumeReportsLiveCollaborationMode` (including the
serialized `collaborationMode` document),
`appserver.TestThreadResumeRestoresPersistedCollaborationMode`, and
`appserver.TestLatestPersistedCollaborationModeReadsWorldState`.

### Re-checked in the same pass (no change needed)

- `allowedLoginMethods`: Go's `config.ConfigRequirements.AllowedLoginMethods`
  and the `allowed_login_methods` requirements-file parsing already mirror
  Rust's #45495 surface, including the local-only layer rule.
- `allowed_windows_sandbox_implementations`: Rust split the app-server protocol
  enum into `WindowsSandboxImplementation` (`elevated`/`unelevated`/`mxc`) while
  its requirements TOML still accepts only elevated/unelevated, and the
  app-server maps the config list into the protocol enum. Go's values for the
  two realizable variants already match the wire (`elevated`/`unelevated`); the
  `mxc` variant is produced only by the native MXC launcher, which is the
  platform-bound lane already recorded above.
- `supportsPersonality` (#45809) was already always false in Go.

- `go build ./...` green; `gofmt -l` clean on every file touched here.
- `go test ./appserver/ ./rollout/ ./parity/ ./model/ ./exec/ ./turn/ ./cli/
  ./tcptunnel/ -count=1` green, and `go test ./... -count=1` -> 96 packages
  `ok` with no `FAIL`.
- Pushed to `origin/main` (`81fee58f`).

## sync39 continuation 2 (same upstream head: 7498521d288b)

Upstream was re-checked (`git pull origin main` -> already up to date) and the
next queued protocol item was landed (`6c9b27a5`).

### Image references by file ID in user inputs (#45543/#45794)

Rust replaced the image URL fields with a shared `ImageReference` type: an image
is either an inline URL or an uploaded-file reference, the app-server wire form
adds `fileId` next to `url`, and the reference reaches the Responses API as
`file_id` instead of being resolved locally. Go now carries it end to end for
the app-server input path:

- `turn.TurnUserInput.FileID` (`fileId`) and `session.ContentPart.FileID`
  (durable `file_id`) mirror Rust's `ImageReference::File`.
- `appserver.inputContentFromTurnUserInputs` emits
  `{"type":"input_image","file_id":...}` for the Responses request (new
  `inputFileContentBlock`), leaving the inline URL form untouched.
- `appserver.threadUserInputContent` reports `{"type":"image","fileId":...}` for
  the in-flight user item, and `sessionContentFromTurnUserInputs` stores the
  reference on the session item so it survives a turn and a rollout round trip.
- The session-part <-> v2-content converters
  (`threadItemContentFromSession`, `threadItemUserInputContent`,
  `sessionContentPartsFromResponseContent`) handle both directions, and
  `ThreadItemContent` gained the `fileId` field.
- `countTurnUserInputImages` counts an uploaded file toward the image budget and
  resize-notice numbering, and the remote-URL guard leaves it alone because it
  has no URL.

Evidence: `appserver.TestUserInputImageReferencesKeepStableWireShapes` (the v2,
Responses, and durable shapes side by side, plus the inbound history both key
spellings), `appserver.TestSessionPartFileReferenceRendersAsFileID`, and
`appserver.TestTurnUserInputFileReferenceCountsAsImage`.

### The image lane's remainder, closed (commit `5691c456`)

- Image-edit selection (`turn/image_generation.go`) mirrors Rust's
  `recent_images`: the window still counts a file-backed image (so numbering
  matches), a window shorter than requested reports how many were available, and
  a window containing a file reference is rejected with Rust's text
  ("...but that window includes a file-backed image that cannot be used for
  editing") instead of silently editing an older inline image.
- Tool-output content items carry the uploaded-file form, so a file reference
  produced by a dynamic tool reaches the model as `file_id` and the v2 client as
  `fileId`, and truncation keeps such items verbatim. Evidence:
  `turn.TestRecentImageURLsRejectsFileBackedWindowsLikeRust`,
  `turn.TestImageGenerationEditRejectsFileBackedRecentWindow`,
  `turn.TestDynamicToolFileImageOutputKeepsTheFileReference`, and
  `utils.TestTruncateFunctionOutputItemsRetainsFileImages`.

Still open in this lane (recorded): the TUI/Guardian omission rules (omit file
references from unsupported TUI display and from Guardian image context), which
belong with the TUI lane and the guardian-context lane respectively.

### Findings that re-scope two queued items

- **MCP app UI metadata (#45805)**: Go's `mcpToolCall` item could render
  `mcpAppResourceUri`, `appContext` and `appName`, but it only *read* them from
  item data — the descriptor-to-item-metadata producer had no Go counterpart.
  The item was therefore re-scoped into a single "MCP app metadata capture"
  lane, which is **done** below.
- **Code-mode overhead timing (#46288)**: Go's code-mode exec output is the JS
  runtime's own text (`text(...)`) with no Rust-style
  `"{status}\nWall time N seconds\nOutput:\n"` response header, so the
  `features.code_mode.experimental_show_cell_overhead` option has no landing
  site until the code-mode response header format itself is ported (which also
  brings the handler/host timing measurements Rust reuses).

## sync39 continuation 3 (same upstream head: 7498521d288b)

### Per-connector tool exposure configuration (#46035, commit `083142da`)

Rust added `apps.<connector_id>.omit_tools_from` (code_mode, deferred, direct)
and combined a connector's omissions with its server's in the tool exposure
policy, so an app can opt out of deferred discovery without changing exposure
for other connectors. Go now parses the setting into the apps configuration
either spelling, drops unknown surfaces, keeps an explicit empty list so it can
clear lower-priority omissions, reports it on the config read surface like the
protocol shape (null when unset), and applies it in the exposure policy:
`turn.mcpOmitToolsFromForTool` joins the server's configured omissions with the
invoking connector's, and the app-server and exec paths pass the effective
`[apps]` config into the registry options. Evidence:
`apps.TestAppsConfigParsesConnectorToolExposureOmissions`,
`turn.TestMCPConnectorOmitToolsFromCombinesWithServerRestrictions`,
`turn.TestMCPConnectorOmitToolsFromPreservesServerRestrictions`, and the config
read assertion in `config.TestServiceReadToolsAndAppsOriginsMatchRustConfigRPC`.

### The code-mode response header and the overhead option (#46288, commit `6406c4e8`)

Rust's code-mode responses carry a header before the script output: the status
line, the wall time (one decimal; three with the overhead breakdown), and an
`Output:` separator. Go previously emitted only the yielded status prefix and no
header at all for completed cells, and `features.code_mode.
experimental_show_cell_overhead` was rejected by strict config.

Go now emits Rust's header on every code-mode response path (direct exec,
settled and yielded cells, wait, and the remote host), inserting it after
truncation the way Rust does so the truncation warning and token accounting
never count the header; the option is accepted in `features.code_mode` and is
wired through the registry options into the shared code-mode runtime. Evidence:
`tool.TestCodeModeResponseHeaderMatchesRustFormat` (both formats, the negative
rounding difference, and absent host timing), the header-aware assertions
updated across `tool`, `codemode` and `appserver`, and
`config.TestCodeModeExperimentalShowCellOverheadLikeRust`.

### The code-mode lane's remainder, closed (`a3a00f8d` and `f2bea6f5`)

- **Script failures report Rust's response.** Rust's failed code-mode response
  is not a tool error: the script's partial output stays in the content, a
  `Script error:\n<message>` block is appended, the header reads "Script
  failed", and the response is marked unsuccessful. Go returned a model-visible
  execution error (`JavaScript execution failed: ...`) instead, so the failure
  text and content shape differed and the TUI's `Script error:` rendering never
  saw a block. The exec, wait, nested-timer, uncaught nested-tool failure and
  remote host error paths now produce Rust's response, with the status derived
  from the response's success flag. Evidence:
  `tool.TestCodeModeScriptFailureShapeMatchesRust` plus the updated exception
  matrix, nested-failure, pending-limit and remote-error tests.
- **The host duration feeds the overhead breakdown.** Rust's
  `code_mode_host_duration_ns` (elapsed time from receiving an Execute/Wait/
  Terminate request to having its outcome ready, always supplied, zero valid)
  now flows from Go's code-mode host (measured per request) through the framing
  protocol - shared by the stdio and gRPC transports - and the client's remote
  response into the response header. Parsing mirrors Rust's contract: an absent
  measurement is rejected while zero is accepted. Evidence:
  `codemode.TestRuntimeResponseHostDurationRoundTrips`, the host-response wire
  assertion in `codemode.TestSessionRuntimeExecuteWaitTerminateAndHost`, and
  `tool.TestCodeModeRemoteHostDurationFeedsTheOverheadHeader`.

Checked while closing the lane: the vendored `codemode/grpc/
codex.code_mode.v1.proto` predates Rust's typed-message additions
(`traceparent = 10`), but Go's gRPC transport carries the generic JSON framing
rather than those typed messages, and its payload already carries the W3C trace
context (`CodeModeTraceContext`) and the host duration, so no functional gap
remains there.

### MCP app UI metadata captured for tool calls and history (#45805, commit `0e1acb08`)

Rust added `mcpToolCall.mcpAppUi` (the invoked descriptor's `resourceUri` plus
`preferredModelDisplayMode`), carried it through tool-call events and saved
history, and kept the legacy resource-URI/app-context fields. Go rendered those
fields but nothing produced them, so this lands the producer end to end:

- `mcp/app_ui.go`: `McpAppUI` / `McpAppDisplayMode` with Rust's derivation —
  resource URI from `ui.resourceUri`, then `ui/resourceUri`, then
  `openai/outputTemplate`; display mode `fullscreen` only for an explicit
  `openai/ui.preferredModelDisplayMode` (an unsupported value stays `inline`);
  no declared widget means nil, so clients keep using catalog discovery. Plus
  `MCPToolCallLinkID`, and `ApplyMCPAppOutputMetadata`, which attaches the
  presentation for any server whose descriptor declares a widget and the
  connector/link/app/action identity only for the trusted codex_apps server.
- `mcp/tool_executor.go` captures that metadata on every call's output, and
  `appserver.sessionItemForAppToolCall` copies it onto the tool-call item
  (`mcpAppUi`, the legacy `mcpAppResourceUri`, and the `appContext` document).
- The v2 item renders `mcpAppUi` (camelCase) beside the legacy field, and the
  rollout core/public projections carry it, so a resumed session keeps the
  presentation.

Evidence: `mcp.TestMCPAppUIFromToolMetaCoversDescriptorDeclarations`,
`mcp.TestApplyMCPAppOutputMetadataMirrorsTrustedCapture`,
`appserver.TestMCPAppUIMetadataReachesTheToolCallItemAndWire`,
`appserver.TestMCPAppUIStaysNullWithoutDescriptorWidgets`, and
`rollout.TestMCPAppUISurvivesTheRolloutRoundTrip`.

### The owning plugin id on tool-call items (commit `cb82118d`)

The residual recorded above is closed. Rust's `mcp_tool_metadata` reports the
owning plugin id for every server, so the id now flows from the server status
through `RuntimeToolInfo.PluginID` into `ToolExecutorOptions.PluginID`, is
written on the call's output metadata (`plugin_id`), and reaches the item data
and the v2 `pluginId` field instead of only arriving from imported history.
Evidence: the plugin-id assertions in
`mcp.TestApplyMCPAppOutputMetadataMirrorsTrustedCapture` (including that a
non-Codex-Apps server still reports its plugin) and
`appserver.TestMCPAppUIMetadataReachesTheToolCallItemAndWire`.

## sync39 continuation 4 (same upstream head: 7498521d288b)

### MCP user interaction stays on the root thread (#46066)

The queued MCP-lane item is landed. Rust rejects every MCP request that needs
human input from a non-root agent - interactive elicitations (including browser
sign-in, which can arrive with an empty form schema) and tool approval prompts -
with the handoff guidance, while automatic approvals and review decisions keep
working, and the connector-auth path keeps its diagnostic next to the guidance.

- `mcp.MCPElicitationHandoffMessage` is Rust's `MCP_ELICITATION_HANDOFF_MESSAGE`
  verbatim, and `mcp.MCPToolApprovalOutcome` carries a handler's decision plus
  the optional model-visible message Rust's `ReviewDecision::denied(message)`
  holds; the executor prefers that message over the default rejection text.
- `appserver.mcpElicitationAuthority.AllowUserInteraction` mirrors
  `ElicitationAuthority::allow_user_interaction`. The runtime publishes it from
  the owning thread (`!r.turnThreadIsSubagent(threadID)`), so a subagent's
  authority disallows interaction, and a root thread's (and every
  background/system flow, which Rust creates with `allow_user_interaction: true`)
  allows it.
- `appserver.mcpElicitationRequiresUserInput` is Rust's guard predicate: an
  explicit `codex_requires_user_input` marker or the `browser_auth` approval kind
  marks a request interactive even with an empty schema, a URL elicitation and a
  user-verification challenge always need the user, and a form needs the user as
  soon as it declares any property. `HandleMCPElicitation` runs the guard before
  any approval-policy handling, automatic approval, or review, exactly where
  Rust's closure does.
- `appserverMCPToolApprovalHandler` denies a non-interactive authority with the
  handoff message. It sits after the permission-request hooks and the Guardian
  review, which is Rust's placement: `request_mcp_tool_user_approval` is reached
  from `request_approval` only after hooks and Guardian routing.
- `mcp.AuthElicitationFailureResult` mirrors Rust's error arm in
  `maybe_request_codex_apps_auth_elicitation`: the connector's preferred
  structured content is flattened into a text item (and cleared), the original
  content is kept, and `Authentication for <connector> could not be completed.
  <error>` is inserted first, so the diagnostic and the handoff guidance both
  survive the call's normal tool-output limit.

Evidence: `appserver.TestRuntimeMCPElicitationAuthorityTracksTheOwningThreadLikeRust`
(the thread-store seam), `appserver.TestAppserverMCPElicitationRejectsSubagentUserInputLikeRust`
(form, empty-schema browser auth, `codex_requires_user_input` over a strict
auto-review request, URL, user verification, and the OpenAI form mode all get
the guidance with no prompt and no review),
`appserver.TestAppserverMCPElicitationKeepsSubagentAutomaticDecisionsLikeRust`
(the empty-schema approval request is still Guardian-reviewed and a full-access
empty form is still auto-accepted),
`appserver.TestAppserverMCPToolApprovalHandsSubagentRequestsToTheParentLikeRust`
(denied, no prompt, nothing remembered),
`mcp.TestToolExecutorSurfacesHandlerMessagesLikeRust`, and
`mcp.TestToolExecutorPreservesAuthDiagnosticsOnHandoffLikeRust` (guidance first,
diagnostic and structured text preserved, `structuredContent` cleared and the
original result left intact).

Go-shape notes: Go resolves the authority per elicitation request from the
owning thread instead of threading `allow_user_interaction` through the MCP
runtime input, which gives the same result for a reused connection and leaves no
cached flag to invalidate; and Go's existing real-MCP-server path already maps a
handler error to a decline carrying `meta.message`, so a subagent's server
elicitation reaches the server as a decline whose message is the handoff
guidance rather than a transport error.

## sync39 continuation 5 (same upstream head: 7498521d288b)

### The Code Mode Guardian scope is retired (#45915)

Rust #45915 removed `code_mode` from `GuardianModelPolicy` and its approval
scopes so a Code Mode wrapper is transparent to a model policy while its nested
tools keep following their own categories. Go's `model` package carried the
same scope, so `GuardianModelPolicy.CodeMode`, `GuardianScopeCodeMode`, and the
`ReviewMode` arm are gone, the field docs now state that the scope keys are
`computer_use`, `shell`, `file_changes`, `mcp`, `network`, and `permissions`,
and the policy comment records Rust's rule that Code Mode wrappers have no
approval scope of their own. Evidence:
`model.TestModelInfoGuardianReviewPolicy` (the omitted-scope assertions now use
`file_changes`/`permissions`, and a wire policy carrying a legacy `code_mode`
key parses with that key ignored while `permissions` still resolves).

Recorded nuance: the other half of the Rust commit - skipping scoring and cache
invalidation for a direct Code Mode `exec` wrapper - has no Go site because Go
does not implement Rust's Guardian v2 async scorer or its cached security-risk
scores, which is the already-recorded N/A for that lane.

### An accepted exec-server replacement retries the attach (found while running the suite)

Running `go test ./...` twice surfaced a real, load-sensitive failure that is
not part of the upstream range but is a Go/Rust behaviour gap:
`execserver.TestConnectAcceptedClientInitializesAndReplacesLikeRust` failed with
`accepted exec-server replacement failed: exec-server initialize failed
(-32010): session <id> is already attached to another connection` (reproduced
on demand by running six concurrent copies of the package under load).

Rust classifies `SESSION_ALREADY_ATTACHED_ERROR_CODE` as a *retryable* recovery
error (`exec-server/src/client_recovery.rs::is_retryable_recovery_error`)
because retiring a transport is asynchronous on the server, and its accepted
replacement returns after handing the socket to the recovery task, which
re-dials/re-handshakes. Go's `ReplaceAcceptedConnection` closed the old transport
and then ran a single initialize, so the handshake could race the server's
detach and fail the host's handoff (losing the host-supplied socket).

Go now mirrors the classification: `execserver.rpcServerError` carries the
JSON-RPC code (text unchanged), `isSessionAlreadyAttachedError` matches -32010,
and `attachAcceptedReplacement` retries the handshake on the same socket for
that code only, bounded by a 2 s window with 10 ms steps, while every other
failure still releases the socket. Evidence:
`execserver.TestAcceptedReplacementRetriesAlreadyAttachedLikeRust` (a scripted
first attach rejected with -32010, then a successful resume, socket kept) and
`execserver.TestAcceptedReplacementSurfacesNonRetryableAttachErrorsLikeRust`
(another code still fails the handoff and closes), plus 330 clean runs of the
previously failing test under the six-job stress harness that reproduced it.

## sync39 continuation 6 (same upstream head: 7498521d288b)

### The read-only MCP tool policy (#46042)

The MCP-lane item is landed. Rust threads `requires_read_only_mcp_tools`
through `McpConfig` into every server's effective config, marks tool requests
with `openai/readOnly`, and keeps read-only connections and catalogs away from
unrestricted ones. Go now does the same:

- `mcp.RuntimeConfig.RequiresReadOnlyMCPTools` mirrors `McpConfig`'s field and
  stays disabled by default (Rust's core configuration hardcodes `false`, so no
  Go production path enables it either); `NewMCPService` publishes it on every
  server's `ServerConfig.RequiresReadOnlyTools`, which is Rust's
  `McpConnectionSet` applying `with_read_only_mcp_tools(config.
  requires_read_only_mcp_tools)` to each enabled server.
- `applyMCPReadOnlyToolsMeta` mirrors `RmcpClient::apply_read_only_tools_meta`:
  it forces `openai/readOnly: true`, overriding a caller-supplied `false`, while
  preserving other metadata (the caller's object is cloned, never mutated).
  `mcpListParamsForCursorWithConfig` is the `tools/list` builder (pagination
  preserved) and `mcpToolCallParams` is the shared `tools/call` builder used by
  both transports, so the policy cannot diverge between HTTP and stdio.
  `resources/list` and `resources/templates/list` keep the plain builder,
  matching Rust, which marks only `tools/list` and `tools/call`.
- `mcpConnectionCacheKey` carries `readOnlyTools`, mirroring
  `McpServerConnectionIdentity`'s field, so a read-only connection never reuses
  an unrestricted one (including through `ApplyRuntimeConfig`, which drops the
  cached client when the policy turns on), and `mcpToolCatalogGraceKey` reports
  a read-only server ineligible for the shared tool catalog.

Evidence: `mcp.TestMCPReadOnlyToolsRequestMetadataLikeRust` (legacy and
2026-07-28 modes x read-only on/off: the first `tools/list` page carries no
cursor, the second keeps `cursor: next`, both carry the marker only when the
policy is on, `resources/list` never does, and `tools/call` keeps `callId` and
`arguments` while overriding a caller-supplied `false`),
`mcp.TestMCPReadOnlyToolsIsolateConnectionsAndCatalogsLikeRust` (identity,
catalog eligibility, the per-server publication, the default-off rule, and the
connection replaced when the policy turns on), and
`mcp.TestMCPReadOnlyToolsKeepsUnrestrictedRequestsUnmarked` (no `_meta` at all
for an unrestricted request, and the caller's metadata object is not mutated).

Recorded nuance: Rust's other cache guard - "bypass the shared Codex Apps tools
cache for read-only connections" - has no Go site because Go has no shared
executable Codex Apps tools cache; Go re-lists the Apps catalog per turn and its
only shared catalog identity is the one excluded above.

## sync39 lazy pass 7 (same upstream head: 7498521d288b) - reconnaissance recorded

### The synchronous-review reasoning effort (#46292) needs a real port, not a gate

Read while looking for the next core item; recorded so the next pass starts from
evidence instead of re-deriving it.

Rust #46292 has two halves:

1. `ModelClient::reasoning_effort_override_enabled` is `feature &&
   !guardian::is_basic_session_source(session_source)`, and the same decision
   now drives request filtering, effort pinning, and update injection, so a
   Guardian reviewer session that inherited the parent's `configuration_update`
   effort items ignores them and keeps its own selected effort.
2. That reviewer effort is the one `guardian-reviewer::select_review_model`
   picks: `low` when the review model's preset supports it, else the preset's
   default effort, or - in the parent-fallback branch - `low` when the parent
   model supports it, else the parent's current/default effort.

Go's half of (1) is structurally absent rather than missing: Go's Guardian
reviewer is `modelGuardianReviewer` running a stateless `model.AgentRequest`
through `guardianSessionRunner`, which chains reviews with `PreviousResponseID`
and never replays parent history or `configuration_update` items, so there is no
Guardian session source, no inherited effort update to filter, and no
`reasoningoverride` pin applied to a review (`appserver/reasoning_effort_override.go`
is only reached from the reviewed thread's turn paths).

Half (2) is a genuine gap: `modelGuardianReviewer.review` builds its request with
`Prompt`, `InputItems`, `Model`, `TaskKind`, `ThreadID`, `TurnID`, `Originator`,
`OutputSchema`, `ClientMetadata`, and the permission profile, but never sets
`model.AgentRequest.ReasoningEffort`, and `RuntimeRouter.guardianReviewModelForTurn`
resolves only `RunConfig.AutoReviewModelOverride` (the model id) with no effort.
So a Guardian review request omits `reasoning.effort` entirely where Rust sends
the selected review effort.

Next step: port `select_review_model` into Go (the pieces exist -
`model.ModelPreset`, `ModelInfo.DefaultReasoningLevel`, and
`ModelInfo.SupportedReasoningLevels`), wire its effort into the review request,
and pin it with a test that the review request carries `low` for a
low-supporting review model, the preset default otherwise, and the parent's
effort in the fallback branch.

## sync39 continuation 7 (same upstream head: 7498521d288b)

### The Guardian reviewer selection and its reasoning effort (#46292)

The step recorded above is landed. Go now resolves the reviewer the way Rust's
`resolve_review_model`/`select_review_model` does and samples the review with the
selected model and its request-level reasoning effort:

- `model.SelectApprovalReviewModel` mirrors the Rust function: the parent
  model's `auto_review_model_override` wins, else the provider's preferred
  review model (`RuntimeProvider::approval_review_preferred_model`) when the
  catalog lists it, else the parent model; the reviewer prefers `low` whenever
  the selected model supports it, otherwise it keeps that model's default effort
  or - in the fallback branch - the parent's selected effort. It also reports
  Rust's `catalog_contains_auto_review`, `model_overridden`, `model_override`,
  and `default_review_model_id`.
- `appserver.RuntimeRouter.guardianReviewSelectionForTurn` resolves those inputs
  per review (parent model info, the turn's selected effort, the preferred
  review model, and `ModelService.Presets(RefreshOffline)`, the new accessor that
  mirrors `ModelsManager::list_models`), and `modelGuardianReviewer` now sends
  `AgentRequest.Model`/`ReasoningEffort` from that selection instead of only the
  catalog override.
- The Go half of the Rust commit that has no Go material stays as recorded: Go's
  reviewer is a stateless direct request, so there is no Guardian session source
  to exempt from `reasoning_effort_override`, no inherited
  `configuration_update` item to filter, and no `reasoningoverride` pin to skip.

Evidence: `model.TestSelectApprovalReviewModelMatchesRust` (override listed by
the catalog, override without `low`, preferred review model with and without
`low`, and the three fallback-effort cases, plus the flags),
`model.TestSelectApprovalReviewModelWithoutCatalogMatchesRust`, and
`appserver.TestGuardianReviewRequestUsesSelectedModelAndEffortLikeRust` (a live
review through `ensureGuardianReviewer`: `codex-auto-review` with `low`
supported, the same model keeping its `high` default, and the parent-model
fallback preferring `low` over the parent's selected `high`).

## sync39 continuation 8 (same upstream head: 7498521d288b)

### The Guardian reviewer's base instructions and `guardian_model_info`

Rust's `build_guardian_review_session_config` gives every review session base
instructions rendered from `model_messages.auto_review()`: the resolved policy
substituted into the resolved policy template, terminated by
`guardian_output_contract_prompt()`. Go's review request set no instructions at
all, so a review sampled with the generic Codex base instructions and no
security policy. Go now matches Rust end to end:

- `model.ApprovalReviewModel`'s two flags drive Rust's `guardian_model_info`
  rule: `RuntimeRouter.guardianReviewPlanForTurn` reads the reviewer's messages
  from the review model's catalog entry when the catalog lists the preferred
  review model or the parent model overrode it, and from the parent's entry
  otherwise.
- The bundled reviewer text is vendored and pinned:
  `state/templates/guardian/policy.md` and `policy_template.md` are copied from
  `codex-rs/prompts/templates/guardian/` and embedded, `state.GuardianPolicy` /
  `GuardianPolicyTemplate` expose them, and
  `parity.TestRustGuardianPolicyPromptMatchesGo` diffs both Go copies against
  `git show HEAD:codex-rs/prompts/templates/guardian/...` (the same vendoring and
  differential convention as the persistent-mode prompt).
- `state.RenderGuardianPolicyInstructions` mirrors
  `GuardianPolicyInstructions::body` (template trimmed at the end, every
  `{{ tenant_policy_config }}` replaced with the trimmed policy, then the output
  contract and a trailing newline), and `state.GuardianOutputContractPrompt`
  carries Rust's `guardian_output_contract_prompt()` text verbatim.
- `config.Config.GuardianPolicyConfig` mirrors Rust's resolution order - the
  managed `guardian_policy_config` requirement wins over `[auto_review] policy`,
  and an empty-after-trim value stays unset so the lower layer applies - and
  `Config.GuardianPolicyTemplate` reads
  `[auto_review] experimental_policy_template`. The requirement is parsed from
  the requirements file and kept off the app-server wire like the other internal
  requirement fields.
- `RuntimeRouter.guardianReviewPlanForTurn` now returns the plan
  (`Selection`, `AutoReview`, `Instructions`) that `modelGuardianReviewer` uses
  for the request's model, effort, and instructions and for the timeout and
  rejection text; the old parent-only `autoReviewMessages` callback is gone.

Evidence: `state.TestRenderGuardianPolicyInstructionsMatchesRust` (Rust's three
substitution cases), `state.TestBundledGuardianTextIsVendored`,
`parity.TestRustGuardianPolicyPromptMatchesGo`,
`config.TestGuardianPolicyConfigLikeRust` (managed beats `[auto_review] policy`,
empty normalizes to unset, template from config) and
`config.TestRequirementsFileParsesGuardianPolicyConfig`,
`appserver.TestGuardianReviewRequestCarriesThePolicyInstructionsLikeRust` (a
live review uses the review model's policy and template, and the parent's when
the catalog lacks the preferred review model), and
`appserver.TestGuardianReviewInstructionsPrecedenceLikeRust` (bundled vs catalog
vs managed/config policy and template, contract always terminal).

Recorded remainder: Rust's rejection/timeout text also falls back to bundled
constants (`REJECTION_INSTRUCTIONS` / `TIMEOUT_INSTRUCTIONS` in
`prompts/src/model_messages/guardian.rs`) when the catalog omits them, while Go
still falls back to its own `state.GuardianTimeoutMessage()` / bare rationale
text in that case. The catalog supplies both in the bundled catalog, so the
difference only shows for a catalog that omits them; it is the next Guardian
step together with the remaining behaviour-lane items.

### Finding for the next Guardian step: the planned-action prompt framing

Read while closing the instructions above, and material (the reviewer sees
different framing and extra text):

- Rust's current framing lives in
  `guardian-context/src/action.rs::PlannedAction::render`: for a sync full
  review it emits `The Codex agent has requested the following action:\n`,
  `>>> APPROVAL REQUEST START\n`, an optional `Retry reason:\n{reason}\n\n`,
  `Assess the exact planned action below. Use read-only tool checks when local
  state matters.\n`, `Planned action JSON:\n`, the JSON, and `>>> APPROVAL
  REQUEST END\n`; network actions and terminal input have their own scopes, and
  the async presentation uses `Planned action JSON:` under the request markers.
- Go's `state.BuildPromptWithOptions` still emits the older
  `Review the planned action and decide whether to allow it.\n\n` plus
  `Action:\n`, and for a node-REPL-eligible action a Go-authored block
  (`Below is JavaScript proposed for Node REPL...`, `Distinguish preparation
  ...`, `Node REPL action JSON:`).
- Rust asserts the opposite of that block in

## sync39 continuation 13 (same upstream head: 7498521d288b)

### Escalated terminal input is reviewed, not rejected

Rust reviews input to an existing unified-exec terminal
(`core/src/unified_exec/stdin_approval.rs`): `ProcessEntry::stdin_approval`
compares the terminal's retained launch permissions with the current policy,
rejects a NUL byte, and otherwise routes the write through
`Session::request_approval` (PermissionRequest hooks, then the Guardian review,
then a command approval request whose command line names the terminal session).
Go had a coarse stand-in: an escalated write was rejected unless the thread
already held a session command approval, so the user could never approve one.

Go now follows Rust's flow:

- `tool.UnifiedExecManager` records each terminal's launch facts (sandbox
  permissions, additional permissions, resolved permission profile, environment
  id, managed-network flag) and offers **every** non-empty write for review
  (except a TTY-less interrupt) through a `WriteStdinApprovalRequest` that
  mirrors Rust's `ApprovalAction::WriteStdin` (process id, the write and launch
  call ids, environment, chars, cwd, tty, retained permissions).
- `appserver.RuntimeRouter.writeStdinApproval` keeps the
  `write_stdin_approval` feature gate and the NUL-byte rejection, then applies
  Rust's `TerminalPermissions::review_requirement` to the subset Go models: a
  terminal whose launch profile still matches the current one writes without a
  prompt (unless the turn requires automatic review), and a bypassed or drifted
  terminal is reviewed. The review runs the hooks with Rust's `write_stdin`
  payload, the Guardian review for auto-review turns, then a command approval
  request (`write_stdin --session-id <pid>`) carrying Rust's
  `TerminalPermissions::approval_reason`, and the `state.Action` it reviews is
  Rust's `WriteStdin` projection (landed in continuation 10).

Evidence: `tool.TestUnifiedExecWriteStdinApprovalGateEscalatedLikeRust` (the
review request carries the launch facts, the write and launch call ids, and the
rejection maps to `write_stdin rejected: ...`),
`appserver.TestWriteStdinApprovalReviewsEscalatedInputLikeRust` (the user's
command approval request and a decline rejection),
`appserver.TestWriteStdinApprovalSkipsMatchingTerminalsLikeRust` (a matching
terminal writes without prompting),
`appserver.TestWriteStdinApprovalRunsHooksAndGuardianLikeRust` (hook allow/deny
and Guardian approve/deny precedence),
`appserver.TestWriteStdinApprovalDisabledFeatureDoesNotGateLikeRust`, and
`appserver.TestWriteStdinApprovalRejectsNULByteLikeRust`. Verification:
`go test ./... -count=1` exit 0 (the `./tool` and `./appserver` packages green
individually).

Recorded remainders of this lane:

1. Go compares resolved `sandbox.PermissionProfile` values where Rust compares
   full file-system sandbox contexts (including the Windows sandbox selection
   and the environment's network policy), and Go has no internal-grant concept,
   so the reason text omits Rust's "It also has an internal filesystem grant.".
2. Rust's `strict_auto_review` input is approximated by the turn's approvals
   reviewer routing to automatic review, and the user-decline message is Go's
   own text rather than Rust's denial message.

The two drift errors are landed in continuation 14.

## sync39 continuation 14 (same upstream head: 7498521d288b)

### Fail-closed drift for terminal input

`terminalWriteDriftError` now implements Rust's two fail-closed cases from
`TerminalPermissions::review_requirement`, which continuation 13 had skipped:
when the current environment enforces an environment-owned network policy that
the terminal's launch policy does not match (or the terminal launched bypassing
the sandbox), and when the current permission profile denies reads while the
terminal's launch profile differs, the write is rejected before any prompt with
Rust's messages ("this terminal cannot enforce the current environment-owned
network restrictions; start a new terminal" / the denied-read counterpart).
The terminal's launch now also records whether it enforced the managed network.

Evidence: `appserver.TestWriteStdinApprovalFailsClosedOnPolicyDriftLikeRust`
(the managed-network case through `writeStdinApproval` with the proxy enabled in
config, and the denied-read case through the rule itself), with no prompt
reaching the client. Verification: `go test ./... -count=1` exit 0.

source, and the section composition order item stands.

## sync39 continuation 16 (same upstream head: 7498521d288b)

### Projectless directories are never implicitly trusted (#46328)

The queued project-trust item is landed. Rust's loader records
`ConfigLayerStack::is_projectless` when a completed discovery found no
project-root marker, no Git checkout root and no project-local `.codex`
directory, and `thread/start` refuses the implicit trust write for those
directories so configuration added later is neither loaded nor preapproved.
Go persisted trust for any cwd whose effective profile allowed it.

- `config.Config` gained the discovery classification
  (`projectDiscoveryIsProjectless` / `projectRootMarkerFound`), computed in
  `LoadWithOptions` whenever project discovery ran (`CWD` set and
  `IgnoreProjectConfig` clear), independent of trust and of the project-config
  merge (which Go still gates on trust). `Config.IsProjectless()` reports it and
  stays false when discovery was skipped, matching Rust's
  `skipped_discovery_does_not_claim_projectless`. CODEX_HOME itself never counts
  as a project layer (Rust's `user_codex_home_is_not_a_project_layer`).
- `appserver` now requires `!cfg.IsProjectless()` beside
  `threadStartEffectivePermissionsTrustProject` before calling
  `trustThreadStartProject`, which is Rust's placement in
  `thread_processor.rs`.

Evidence: `config.TestProjectlessClassificationMatchesRust` (an unmarked
directory is projectless even with a saved `trusted` or `untrusted` decision,
and neither a missing cwd nor `IgnoreProjectConfig` claims projectless),
`config.TestProjectMarkersAndLocalLayersPreventProjectlessMatchesRust` (Rust's
seven-case marker table adapted to Go's `.gcode` directory),
`config.TestUserCodexHomeIsNotAProjectLayerMatchesRust`,
`config.TestManagedRootMarkersControlProjectlessMatchesRust` (managed
`project_root_markers` decide the classification), and
`appserver.TestRuntimeRouterThreadStartProjectlessDoesNotPersistProjectTrustLikeRust`
(a `danger-full-access` start in a projectless directory leaves `config.toml`
byte-identical, and project configuration added afterwards is neither loaded nor
trusted by a later read-only start). Verification: `go test ./... -count=1`
exit 0.

Fixture note: `appserver.TestRuntimeRouterThreadStartProjectTrustWriteGuards`
created its git fixture as an empty `.git` directory, which
`gitMetadataPathExists` (Rust #39629, already ported) does not count as
repository metadata, so the workspace had silently become projectless. The
fixture now writes `.git/HEAD` exactly like Rust's
`thread_start_with_nested_git_cwd_respects_effective_permissions_for_project_trust`,
which is what the test means to exercise; this is upstream-consistent fixture
maintenance, not a weakening of the assertion.

Recorded finding (not landed): Go's `projectRootMarkersFromValues` conflates an
absent `project_root_markers` with an explicit empty array (`[]`), so
`projectRootMarkerExistsWithMarkers` falls back to the built-in markers where
Rust treats `[]` as "root detection disabled". None of the projectless cases
above change their verdict because of it, but it is a latent divergence in
project-root discovery that should be fixed with its own test pass.

## sync39 continuation 17 (same upstream head: 7498521d288b)

### The turn-start active plugin inventory (#46323)

The queued analytics item is landed. Rust adds
`active_plugin_ids_at_turn_start` to the turn event: it combines the turn's
active host plugin identities with the plugin package keys selected for the
thread, prefers a valid remote id over the package key, sorts and deduplicates,
and reports `null` for an unknown inventory, an invalid identity, an id longer
than 128 bytes, or more than 512 distinct ids (`[]` stays an observed empty
inventory). Go's turn event had no such field.

- `plugin.IsValidRemotePluginID` mirrors
  `codex_core_plugins::remote::is_valid_remote_plugin_id` (non-empty ASCII
  letters, digits, `_`, `-`, `~`; not trimmed).
- `plugin.validatePluginSegment` now ports Rust's
  `plugin::plugin_id::validate_plugin_segment`: a **plugin name** may use `.` as
  a separator between non-empty segments (rejecting `.`/`..` traversal, leading
  or trailing dots, and `..`), while a marketplace name may not. This was a
  pre-existing divergence (Rust #36544, before the sync39 range) that the
  inventory's package-key validation depends on: `my.tool@123` is a valid
  fallback key while `bad..name@1` is not. `ParsePluginId` no longer trims its
  input, matching Rust's `PluginId::parse`.
- `appserver.activePluginTelemetryIDs` carries the identity selection and the
  complete-inventory bounds, `activePluginIDsForTurnAnalytics` assembles the
  identities (active host capabilities plus
  `executorSkillRootPluginIDs`, Go's selected-plugin snapshot), and
  `turnAnalyticsPluginInventory` memoizes the first observation on the active
  turn so the post-compaction run-config rebuild cannot re-sample it (Rust's
  first-received-inventory rule).
- `telemetry.CodexTurnEventParams` gained
  `active_plugin_ids_at_turn_start` (`*[]string`: nil is JSON `null`, an empty
  slice is `[]`), mapped from the new `CodexTurnEventInput` field and filled
  from `appTurnRunConfig.ActivePluginIDsAtTurnStart`.

Evidence: `plugin.TestIsValidRemotePluginIDMatchesRust` (including the
untrimmed `" plugin_box "` and the `box@local` package-key cases),
`plugin.TestNewPluginIdValidates` / `TestParsePluginId` /
`TestValidatePluginSegment` (dotted plugin names valid; traversal, leading,
trailing and doubled dots invalid; dotted marketplace names invalid),
`appserver.TestActivePluginTelemetryIDsSelectAndValidateBySource` (Rust's nine
identity cases including remote-wins-over-invalid-fallback, remote-looking
selected roots staying fallbacks, and exact deduplication of aliases and
distinct remote objects),
`appserver.TestActivePluginTelemetryIDsEnforceBounds` (Rust's 128/129-byte and
512/513-id bounds, for host and selected inputs),
`appserver.TestRuntimeRouterTurnAnalyticsPluginInventoryLikeRust` (a completed
turn reports the remote id, the package key, or null without a plugin
service), `appserver.TestTurnAnalyticsPluginInventoryKeepsTheFirstObservation`
(a mid-turn plugin reconciliation does not change the admitted turn's
inventory, while a new turn sees it), and
`telemetry.TestCodexTurnEventSerializesExpectedRustShape` plus
`TestCodexTurnEventPluginInventoryNullabilityMatchesRust` (the exact event JSON,
`null` vs `[]` vs a populated array). Verification: `go test ./... -count=1`
exit 0.

Recorded nuances (not landed):

1. Go's `CapabilitySummary.RemotePluginID` is a plain string, so a
   present-but-empty remote id is indistinguishable from an absent one; Rust's
   `Option<String>` case ("blank present remote" makes the inventory unknown)
   is exercised only at the identity layer in Go.
2. Rust samples the inventory from the turn context captured before pre-turn
   compaction; Go memoizes the first observation on the active turn, which
   yields the same "first received" inventory without modelling the step-context
   split.

  developer instructions retained).
- `RuntimeRouter.applyThreadResumeCollaborationMode` now takes the resumed
  session's effective config (hoisted from the cold-resume block, so the
  resolution is not repeated), overlays model and effort for a cold resume,
  reports the overlaid mode plus the effective `reasoningEffort`, restores it
  into the thread's live settings, and appends the thread-owned
  `ThreadSettingsApplied` snapshot the next resume restores from. A live thread
  still reports its own effective mode, and a resume with no persisted mode
  keeps the previous fallback to the live selection.

Evidence:
`appserver.TestRuntimeRouterThreadResumeRestoresPersistedModeLikeRust` (a real
`thread/resume` over a rollout whose owned snapshot saves
`{plan, gpt-5-plan, high, "plan first"}` with `model: "gpt-5.4"` and root
`model_reasoning_effort = "high"`: the response carries
`{plan, gpt-5.4, high, "plan first"}` and `reasoningEffort: high`, and the
rollout's latest owned snapshot carries the overlaid mode),
`appserver.TestThreadResumeOverlaysEffectiveModelAndEffortLikeRust` (the
overlay, the retained instructions, the reported effort, the restored live
settings, and the settings snapshot the resume persists),
`appserver.TestThreadResumeClearsSavedEffortWhenConfigHasNoneLikeRust` (Rust's
`Some(None)` arm clears the saved effort), plus the updated
`TestThreadResumeReportsLiveCollaborationMode` /
`TestThreadResumeRestoresPersistedCollaborationMode`. Verification:
`go test ./... -count=1` exit 0.

Recorded nuance: Go's resume response previously never reported
`reasoningEffort`; it now reports the resumed session's effective effort, which
is the value Rust reports from `session_configured.reasoning_effort`. The
overlay and the reported effort therefore always agree.

### The effective login methods in `configRequirements/read` (#45495)

The queued protocol item is landed. Rust's `configRequirements/read` reports
`allowedLoginMethods` from the *running* authentication policy - the locally
managed allowlist, the forced login method, and the forced/allowlisted ChatGPT
workspace intersection - and returns a requirements object even when no managed
requirements exist but that policy restricts login methods (an empty list
permits no method; the unrestricted default keeps `requirements: null`). Go
surfaced only the requirements file's own `allowed_login_methods` and returned
`requirements: null` whenever there was no requirements file.

- `config.ManagedAuthPolicy.EffectiveAllowedLoginMethods` mirrors
  `ManagedAuthPolicy::allowed_login_methods` (API then ChatGPT, filtered by the
  forced method, the managed allowlist, and the empty-workspace rule), and
  `Config.AllowedLoginMethods` mirrors `AuthManager::allowed_login_methods`.
  The method is named `EffectiveAllowedLoginMethods` because Go cannot give a
  method and the `AllowedLoginMethods` allowlist field the same name.
- `config.ProjectRequirementsWithAllowedLoginMethods` mirrors Rust's
  `map_requirements_to_api`: absent requirements return `nil` only for the
  unrestricted `[api, chatgpt]` default, otherwise a requirements object carries
  the effective list (a non-nil empty slice, so it serializes as `[]` rather
  than `null`).
- `appserver.configRequirementsRead` combines the stored requirements with the
  effective config's forced login method and workspaces, and the
  `configRequirements/read` dispatch now uses it.

Evidence: `config.TestConfigAllowedLoginMethodsFollowsForcedWorkspacesLikeRust`
(Rust's
`allowed_login_methods_follow_current_forced_workspaces` cases: a forced
workspace inside the allowlist keeps both methods, one outside leaves API only,
and no forced workspaces is unrestricted),
`config.TestConfigAllowedLoginMethodsCombinesForcedMethodAndAllowlistLikeRust`
(forced method and allowlists, including `[]` permitting nothing),
`config.TestProjectRequirementsWithAllowedLoginMethodsMatchesRust` (nil for the
unrestricted default, a requirements object for a restricted policy without
managed requirements, preserved managed fields, and the `[]` JSON shape), and
`appserver.TestRuntimeRouterConfigRequirementsReadReportsAllowedLoginMethodsLikeRust`
(unrestricted stays null; forced-API, managed allowlist, and workspace
intersection cases over a real `configRequirements/read` request).
Verification: `go test ./... -count=1` exit 0.

Recorded finding (not landed, same class as the `project_root_markers = []` one
from continuation 16): Go's `stringListFromConfigValue` drops an explicit empty
array, so `forced_chatgpt_workspace_id = []` is indistinguishable from an absent
key. Rust keeps `Some(vec![])`, which restricts ChatGPT to an empty workspace
set and therefore removes it from the allowed methods (and from login gating).
Fixing that means changing `ForcedChatGPTWorkspaceIDs` to preserve
presence/absence, switching `EffectiveChatGPTWorkspaces` and
`auth.EnsureWorkspaceAccountAllowed` to presence semantics, and re-running the
auth-gating tests, so it is scoped as its own pass in the auth lane.

## sync40 (upstream head `dbf478850f`, range `7498521d288b..dbf478850f`)

Upstream moved for the first time in this thread: two commits
(`3d5b66c655 Handle unknown error classifications and configure gateway OAuth
(#46482)`, `dbf478850f Make TUI async question replies compatible with desktop
(#46486)`). The Rust checkout was fast-forwarded to `dbf478850f`, and the
static parity layer again produced the objective triage signal: the fixture-root
counts, the TUI snapshot manifest, and the error-code surface all failed against
the new checkout before any code changed.

### 1. Gateway OAuth provider configuration (#46482)

Rust added an optional `gateway_oauth` block to a model provider: secondary OAuth
credentials delivered alongside the provider's primary authentication, with
strict validation (HTTPS or loopback HTTP endpoints without userinfo or
fragments, an AWS-auth conflict, a non-empty client id with a nonzero redirect
port, a header or cookie delivery whose name/scheme is a valid token and not a
reserved authentication/routing/internal header, and no collision with a
configured provider header).

- `model.GatewayOAuthConfig` / `GatewayOAuthDelivery` mirror Rust's types
  (`kind`, `name`, `scheme` with the "Bearer" default, plus
  `EffectiveHeaderName`), `ProviderInfo.GatewayOAuth` carries them, and
  `ProviderInfo.Validate` runs the gateway validation first, as Rust does.
- `model.gatewayOAuthConfigFromConfig` parses the block from a provider
  configuration table, and the URL/token/reserved-header rules live in
  `validateGatewayOAuthURL` / `isGatewayOAuthToken` / `isGatewayOAuthHeaderName`.
  Error text never echoes the offending URL.

Evidence: `model.TestGatewayOAuthParsesHeaderAndCookieDelivery` (Rust's
`parses_header_and_cookie_delivery`, including the default and explicit
schemes), `model.TestGatewayOAuthRejectsUnsafeEndpointsAndDeliveryCollisions`
(Rust's `rejects_unsafe_endpoints_and_delivery_collisions`: four unsafe base
URLs, the same four as token URLs with no URL echoed, six reserved or malformed
header names, a static-header collision, and an env-header collision), and
`model.TestGatewayOAuthRejectsAWSAndIncompleteConfigs` (the AWS combination,
missing base URL, blank client id, zero redirect port, a bad scheme, and a bad
cookie name).

Recorded dispositions for the rest of the Rust change: the
`thread_config/remote.rs` hunks drop `gateway_oauth` when a provider round-trips
through the remote thread-config proto, which Go does not model (Go's
cloud-config loader applies raw config layers rather than provider protos), so
they are N/A; `config.schema.json` gained nested `GatewayOAuthConfig` /
`GatewayOAuthDelivery` definitions only, so the parity schema surface (which
pins top-level keys) is unchanged; the remaining files are Rust tests or
telemetry internals.

### 2. Unknown error classifications stay readable (#46482)

Rust's core `CodexErrorInfo` now deserializes through a wire enum: known
classifications are preserved (including the legacy-only `threadRollbackFailed`),
an unknown one becomes `other` so saved sessions stay readable, and a known
classification whose payload is invalid is rejected. Go's rollout reader stored
whatever JSON it found, so a resumed thread surfaced a raw unknown classification
to clients instead of `other`.

- `rollout.normalizeCodexErrorInfoClassification` recognizes Rust's core
  snake_case spellings and the app-server camelCase spellings Go already stores,
  maps an unknown bare string or unknown single-key object to `other`, validates
  the status-code and `turn_kind` payloads (Rust's `NonSteerableTurnKind` accepts
  only review/compact), and rejects a known tag whose payload is invalid.
- `rolloutEventError` applies the normalization, and the two replay handlers now
  treat an unreadable classification like Rust's failed line decode (the record
  is not applied).

Evidence: `rollout.TestRecordFromPathToleratesUnknownCodexErrorClassifications`
(Rust's `rollout_line_decoder_preserves_terminal_records_with_unknown_errors`:
an unknown bare string and an unknown tagged payload both read back as `other`,
a known classification is preserved, and the malformed known payload leaves the
turn unresolved). Closing this also removed the last entry of
`parity.rustErrorCodeEmissionGaps`, so the parity enum surface now covers every
documented error code with no exemptions.

Recorded nuance: Go's rollout loader is raw-JSON based (payloads stay
`json.RawMessage`), so the rejection is expressed at the replay boundary rather
than by failing the whole line during `Load`, which is where Rust performs it.

### 3. Desktop async-question replies (#46486)

The TUI commit makes async-question answers use the desktop's existing reply
envelope with a stable per-question identity, resolves questions from committed
messages and replayed history, and renders replies as readable Q&A text.

- `context.AnsweredQuestion` now carries `(question id, question, answer)`,
  emits the `send_user_message_question_reply` envelope (markers plus the
  JSON payload), and falls back to the previous unmarked plain-text framing when
  the identity exceeds 512 bytes; the model-authored question stays bounded and
  line-break flattened.
- `tui.ParseAsyncQuestionReplies` / `AsyncQuestionReplyDisplayText` /
  `ParseAsyncQuestionReplyInput` mirror Rust's `async_question_reply` module,
  including the IDE-context prefix, single and batched envelopes, unknown-field
  tolerance, and rejection of embedded or malformed payloads.
- `bottompane.AsyncQuestions` retains each question's stable identity
  (`json(["request_user_input_async", message id, index])`), skips questions
  already answered by identity or by a legacy message-scoped reply, and resolves
  answers with `ResolveAsyncQuestionAnswers` (cursor and expanded state
  preserved, answered identities retained so replay cannot reopen them).
  `BuildAsyncQuestionAnswer` renders the reply envelope and enforces the
  user-input limit on the rendered reply, so JSON escaping counts.
- The TUI consumes replies end to end: live and buffered user-message items
  resolve their questions, buffered replay and transcript rendering show the
  readable Q&A text, and the chatwidget restore/preview helpers convert stored
  envelopes (Composer/queue previews and restored history).

Evidence: `context.TestAnsweredQuestionFramingIsBoundedAndKeepsUnicodeBoundaries`,
`TestAnsweredQuestionFlattensLineBreaks`, and
`TestAnsweredQuestionFallsBackToPlainTextForOversizedIdentity`;
`tui.TestAsyncQuestionReplyAcceptssSingleAndBatchedEnvelopes` (Rust's own
fixtures, including the unknown `extra` field and the batched rendering),
`TestAsyncQuestionReplyRejectsMalformedOrEmbeddedEnvelopes` (all five Rust cases
plus a missing-field case), `TestAsyncQuestionReplyFollowsIdeContextPrefix`, and
`TestParseAsyncQuestionReplyInputRequiresOneTextItem`;
`bottompane.TestAsyncQuestionsResolveAnswersLikeRust` and
`TestOversizedQuestionIDsKeepQuestionsAnswerableLikeRust` (Rust's two new state
tests), plus the updated submission tests;
`tea.TestAsyncQuestionsAnsweredOnAnotherClientDismissesLikeRust` (identical
titles, identity-scoped dismissal, no envelope leaking into the view),
`TestAsyncQuestionsAnsweredOnAnotherClientKeepsOtherDraftsLikeRust` (an unstored
draft on another question survives), and
`TestBufferedUserMessageRendersReplyEnvelopeAsTextLikeRust`;
`chatwidget.TestUserMessageReplyEnvelopeRendersAsTextLikeRust` (display,
restore, preview, override precedence, and the plain-message passthrough).

Recorded nuance (Go structural gap, not introduced here): Rust's chatwidget
renders a committed user message from another client through
`on_committed_user_message`; Go's tea model renders only locally submitted and
replayed messages, so a live remote `user_message` item resolves its questions
and is converted where it is rendered but does not itself enter the tea
transcript. Closing that is a TUI wiring change beyond this commit's contract.

### Landed: the environment-network drift rejection is gone (#46499)

Rust removed the fail-closed path that rejected terminal input when the
environment owns a network policy the terminal's launch bypassed: an approved
escalation is now allowed, is offered for escalation review, and the reviewer
weighs the bypass ("This terminal was launched outside the sandbox, bypassing
any managed network proxy."). Go still rejected it with
"this terminal cannot enforce the current environment-owned network restrictions;
start a new terminal".

- `RuntimeRouter.terminalWriteDriftError` now keeps only the denied-read
  fail-closed case, matching Rust's `TerminalPermissions::review_requirement`
  after the change (the environment-network branch is deleted, with the reason
  recorded in the doc comment).

Evidence: `appserver.TestWriteStdinApprovalFailsClosedOnPolicyDriftLikeRust` now
drives a terminal whose launch bypassed the sandbox while the environment
enforces a managed network: the write is approved through the normal
command-approval prompt (captured and accepted) with the bypass authority text
in the reason, instead of failing closed; the denied-read case still fails closed
with its own message. Verification: `go test ./... -count=1` exit 0.

Recorded remainder of #46499 (with the Rust targets):

1. **[landed in sync43]** An explicit full escalation must bypass the controller
   and attachment-owned proxies for the attempt (`tools/runtimes/unified_exec.rs`
   returns no network spec when the attempt's sandbox permissions require
   escalation). `ShellExecutor.Execute` now resolves the attempt's permissions
   with `sandbox.SandboxPermissionsPreservingDeniedReads(args.SandboxPermissions,
   validation.PermissionProfile)` before the managed-network resolver block and
   only applies the resolved env/proxy/decider when the result is not
   `require_escalated`. Evidence:
   `tool.TestShellExecutorEscalationBypassesManagedNetworkLikeRust` (an escalated
   launch keeps no managed network; a denied-read profile downgrades the
   escalation to `use_default` and keeps the proxy, Rust's ESCALATED_DENY_READ
   guard). Verification: `go test ./tool/ ./appserver/ -count=1` exit 0.
2. The launch baseline still has to record that a bypassed launch bypassed the
   environment's network restrictions (`tools/orchestrator.rs` computes
   `managed_network_active` from the owner policy and the effective network
   sandbox policy even when escalation skips the execution proxy), which is what
   makes the retained-terminal review above trigger. Go's
   `EnforceManagedNetwork` currently means "a proxy was attached".

### Finding: the sync39 triage missed the gateway OAuth credential manager

`a129392ebb Add OAuth credential management for model provider gateways
(#46318)` is inside the sync39 range (`5b1d656018..7498521d288b`) but is not
mentioned anywhere in the sync39 triage, and `8f73cdee45 Centralize OAuth login
and refresh handling with safer diagnostics (#46300)` is its sibling. The
gateway-OAuth work I landed in sync40 (the provider `gateway_oauth`
configuration) is therefore accepted by Go but **inert**: nothing resolves the
gateway credential, and sync41's #46490 composes exactly that manager with the
primary provider auth.

The next core step is therefore the #46318 + #46490 chain, whose Rust surface is
now fully mapped: `login/src/oauth/{pkce,authorization,client,error}.rs` (PKCE,
authorization URL, form/JSON token grants, rejection parsing with a bounded
error body and secret redaction), `login/src/gateway_auth{,_callback,_storage,
_token}.rs` (credential id derivation, cross-process lock, encrypted namespaced
storage, refresh/reuse policy, browser authorize), and
`model-provider/src/combined_auth.rs` (header/cookie composition, conflict and
sensitive-header rules) plus the models-endpoint, shared-manager and catalog
cache-identity wiring.

Go has no `secrets` package and no gateway-auth runtime; the reusable pieces are
`auth/oauth.go` (PKCE and the loopback browser-login server) and
`keyring.Store`/`auth.KeyringStore`. Go also has no `usage_tags` document on the
sampling span (#46501) and no tracing-derived feedback tags.
