  commands and the cell renders Rust's `Exploring`/`Explored` layout with
  `Read`/`List`/`Search` labels. Evidence:
  `tui/tea.TestModelRendersExploringCommandActionsLikeRust` plus the existing
  `TestModelRendersCommandExecutionLifecycle` (non-exploring commands keep the
  generic cell).

### Landed: live exploring-group accumulation (closes the #46565 exploration half)

- The tea Model now keeps one active exploring `ExecCell`
  (`activeExecCell`/`activeExecCellMessageIndex`, mirroring
  `computerActivityGroup`/`computerActivityMessageIndex`):
  - `renderCommandExecutionItem` routes a started call through
    `ExecCell.WithAddedCall` when an exploring group is open and opens a new
    cell otherwise; a completed call the group tracks finishes in place through
    `CompleteCall` (Rust `handle_command_execution_begin` /
    `handle_command_execution_completed_now`).
  - The Rust end-target distinctions are preserved: a replayed completion with
    no matching start folds into a still-open (non-running) exploring group,
    and a completion for an untracked call while the group still has running
    calls renders as an orphan history cell without disturbing the group.
  - The group closes on a non-exploring command, on any other item kind (except
    transcript-only reasoning, which attaches), and on turn completion /
    interruption (`MarkFailed` for running calls).
  - `appendReasoningToActiveActivityGroup` now also attaches to the active
    exploring group, so intervening reasoning stays in transcript order between
    the calls it followed (Rust `ActivityGroup`).
- Evidence: `tui/tea.TestExploringCommandsGroupIntoOneCellLikeRust`,
  `TestExploringGroupKeepsReasoningInOrderLikeRust`,
  `TestExploringGroupEndsOnNonExploringCommandLikeRust`,
  `TestExploringGroupClosesOnCompletionLikeRust`, plus the existing
  `tui/exec_cell` grouping tests now exercised by live input.

## sync52: workspace routing discovery for `account/read` (#45529/#45812/#46281)

The app-server `GetAccountResponse.workspaceRouting` field and its discovery were
the last unlanded protocol-sweep item. Go now mirrors Rust:

- `chatgptapi.GetAccountsCheck` fetches `accounts/check` (Codex path
  `/api/codex/accounts/check`, ChatGPT path `{base}/wham/accounts/check`) and
  decodes `accounts` as either a list or an id-keyed map ordered by
  `account_ordering` (Rust `RawAccountsCheckResponse`).
- `auth.WorkspaceRouting` + `AccountRoutingOverride` (NO_CONSTRAINT/us/us_cr) are
  exposed on `auth.GetAccountResponse.WorkspaceRouting` as `workspaceRouting`
  (null when absent), matching Rust's v2 account protocol.
- `appserver.workspaceRoutingForAccountRead` performs the discovery for every
  authenticated ChatGPT credential (ChatGPT, ChatGPT auth tokens, and personal
  access tokens, Rust `is_chatgpt_auth`), keyed by credential generation +
  workspace + backend scope so a refresh invalidates the cache and concurrent
  waiters share one fetch. `resolveWorkspaceRouting` mirrors Rust
  `resolve_routing`: the `NO_CONSTRAINT` backend sentinel, https-origin
  validation, required-backend reconciliation (`BackendConflict`), and the
  effective-base-url fallback. account/read surfaces routing failures as
  internal errors with Rust's messages (MissingWorkspace/DuplicateWorkspace/
  InvalidOverride/DiscoveryUnauthorized/...).

Evidence: `chatgptapi.TestAccountsCheckResponseDecodesListAndMapLikeRust` and
`TestCloudClientGetAccountsCheckPathAndHeaders`;
`appserver.TestRuntimeRouterGetAccountExposesWorkspaceRoutingLikeRust` (list
form, auth headers, cached single fetch),
`TestRuntimeRouterGetAccountWorkspaceRoutingErrorsLikeRust` (missing/duplicate/
invalid/missing-origin/401 -> the exact Rust messages), and
`TestResolveWorkspaceRoutingLikeRust` (sentinel, required match, conflict,
insecure origin). Two pre-existing account/read tests now stub `accounts/check`,
matching Rust's own auth fixtures.

### Landed: applying the discovered routing to model requests

- `model.ApplyWorkspaceRouting` mirrors Rust `apply_workspace_routing`: only the
  origin of the provider base URL changes (scheme/host/port), the path and query
  are preserved, and `x-openai-account-routing-override` is set for `us`/`us_cr`
  or removed for `NO_CONSTRAINT`. Invalid backends/origins/overrides are
  rejected with Rust's messages.
- `appserver.applyWorkspaceRoutingToAgent` resolves routing for a turn's
  Responses provider when the provider may use the dedicated Codex backend
  routes (`ProviderInfo.SupportsCodexBackendRoutes`) and is the first-party
  OpenAI provider, then rewrites the runner's provider and sets
  `RejectRedirects`.
- `ResponsesAgentRunner.RejectRedirects` mirrors Rust
  `ClientRedirectPolicy::Reject`: a routed request never follows a redirect, so
  the account-routing credential cannot be forwarded to another origin.

Evidence: `model.TestApplyWorkspaceRoutingMatchesRust`,
`model.TestApplyWorkspaceRoutingRejectsInvalidInputsLikeRust`,
`model.TestResponsesAgentRunnerRejectsRedirectsWhenRouted`, and the
`appserver.TestResolveWorkspaceRoutingLikeRust` "responses agent applies
routing" subtest.

### Landed: shared discovery core

- The discovery/resolution core moved into `model` (`DiscoverWorkspaceRouting`,
  `ResolveWorkspaceRouting`, the `ErrWorkspaceRouting*` values) so it is not
  app-server-private; the app-server keeps only its per-credential-generation
  cache and its account/read + turn wiring.

Evidence: `model.TestDiscoverWorkspaceRoutingMatchesRust` (workspace selection,
missing/duplicate/401), plus the existing app-server tests now exercising
`model.ResolveWorkspaceRouting`.

### Corrected: the `exec` CLI path must NOT apply routing

An earlier pass wired `applyExecWorkspaceRouting` into `exec.Runner.agentForRun`
and added `exec/workspace_routing_test.go`. That was **wrong and has been
removed**: Rust installs the routing resolver only from the app-server
(`account_processor.rs::set_workspace_routing_resolver`), and
`AuthManager::workspace_routing` documents that "CLI callers without a discovery
owner retain their existing routing behavior" by returning `Ok(None)`. So the
`codex exec` path leaves the provider untouched, and only app-server-hosted
turns (which install the resolver) rewrite it. The app-server
`responsesAgentForTurn` wiring is the correct site.

### Landed: connection-initialized routing notification

- `RuntimeRouter.notifyWorkspaceRoutingToConnection` mirrors Rust
  `AccountRequestProcessor::notify_workspace_routing_to_connection`: once a
  connection initializes, discovery runs off the request path (spawned like
  Rust's `tokio::spawn`), and when it resolved and the credential owner
  generation is unchanged, the connection receives `account/updated`. Both
  transports call it where they already send the initialize notifications
  (`stdio` and `websocket`), since Go drops the client `initialized`
  notification before it reaches the router.

Evidence: the `appserver.TestResolveWorkspaceRoutingLikeRust`
"connection initialize notifies account updated" and "no chatgpt credential
stays silent" subtests.

### Landed: discovery consistency checks

- `workspaceRoutingConfigIdentity` captures the routing scope (effective
  `chatgpt_base_url`, `model_provider`, and the managed `chatgpt_base_url`
  requirement) before discovery. After discovery the resolver re-reads it and
  refuses the result when the credential owner generation moved or the resolved
  routing names another account (`ErrWorkspaceRoutingAccountChanged`), when the
  reload fails (`ErrWorkspaceRoutingRequirementsReload`), or when the scope
  changed (`ErrWorkspaceRoutingConfigurationChanged`) — mirroring Rust
  `AuthManager::workspace_routing` plus `read_account`'s reload guard.

Evidence: `appserver.TestRuntimeRouterWorkspaceRoutingRejectsCredentialChangeLikeRust`
(an owner switch during the request surfaces the exact Rust message) and
`appserver.TestRuntimeRouterWorkspaceRoutingRejectsConfigChangeLikeRust` (a
`model_provider` change during discovery refuses the routing).

### Landed: 401 recovery-and-retry for discovery

- `discoverWorkspaceRouting` mirrors Rust `read_account`'s recovery loop: a 401
  from accounts/check refreshes the credential owner
  (`refreshManagedAuthForStatus`) and retries discovery with the refreshed
  credential, bounded to the recovery plan's steps (3) so a persistently
  rejecting backend cannot spin; a failed refresh maps to
  `DiscoveryUnauthorized`.
- The owner-generation baseline is seeded with the resolved credential
  (`AuthChangeTracker.SeedLastAuth`), matching Rust's manager (which records its
  initial credential before observing changes), so a same-owner refresh is not
  misread as an ownership change.

Evidence: `appserver.TestRuntimeRouterWorkspaceRoutingRecoversFromUnauthorizedLikeRust`
(the stub accounts/check rejects the first credential, the token endpoint
rotates it, and discovery then succeeds with exactly one unauthorized attempt).

### Remaining nuances in this lane

Still missing versus Rust: the per-session
`WorkspaceRoutingContext.previously_routed` / session-backend bound checks
(`SessionBackendChanged`) — Go applies routing once per turn provider rather
than per request, so there is no per-session latch to consult. Rust's
`ResponsesConnectionKey` socket-reuse key is effectively satisfied in Go by
construction: the provider (origin plus routing header) is fixed for a runner,
and the websocket session cache is keyed per thread+turn, so a pooled socket
can never serve a different route.

## sync58: application network policy primitives (#45503)

The sync49 triage marked #45503 N/A; re-checking the commit showed it adds an
exported `http-client` API surface (`NetworkPolicy`, `NetworkPolicyController`,
`NetworkPolicyRevision`, `NetworkPermit`, `DestinationPolicy`,
`NetworkPolicyDenied`) with **no production caller** in Rust yet, so Go was
missing the surface rather than an integrated behaviour. Go now mirrors it in
`network/network_policy.go`:

- `DestinationPolicy` (unrestricted or restricted) permits only `https`/`wss`
  requests to exact normalized hosts, ignoring one trailing dot
  (`DestinationPolicy::allows`).
- `NetworkPolicyController` publishes an already-composed policy for the current
  revision, rejects stale publications, clears the policy on a failed load
  (`Unavailable`), and revokes permits the new policy no longer allows.
- `NetworkPolicy` is the read side transports bind to: `ForCurrentAccount` (an
  account-bound handle cannot regain access after an invalidation),
  `Changes`/`Revision`, `Invalidate`, `Acquire`, and
  `AcquireForUnsupportedSDK` (denied once any endpoint scoping or restriction
  applies).
- `NetworkPermit` retains authorization for the whole operation with a latched
  `Check`/`Revoked` and `RunWithNetworkPermit` (revocation wins over the running
  operation).

Go adaptation recorded in the code: Go has no weak references, so a transport
that abandons a permit calls `Release()` to keep the controller's tracking list
bounded (Rust relies on `Drop`); the observable revocation behaviour is
unchanged.

Evidence: `network.TestRestrictedDestinationPolicyMatchesExactSecureHosts`,
`TestNetworkPolicyInvalidationRevokesPermitsAndRejectsStalePublication`,
`TestNetworkPolicyLoadFailureRecoversCurrentAccountButNotPrevious`, and
`TestNetworkPolicyUnsupportedSDKWorkIsDeniedAndCancelled` — the four Rust
`network_policy_tests.rs` cases.

Also re-verified in this pass: #46331 (defer environment network policy
validation until after composition) is already Go's shape — Go captures the
portable policy in `EnvironmentNetworkPolicyFromConfig` and validates only the
composed policy in `NewSpec`/`NewSpecForEnvironment`, so nothing validated the
selected-but-replaceable values eagerly.

## sync59: Code Mode tool metadata in compaction prompts (#46044)

The sync49 triage queued this item. Go implemented the *old* Rust behaviour
(`StripDirectMetadataWhenDisabled`, Rust's `strip_disabled_direct_metadata`) but
never called it from the compaction path, and compaction prompts carried no code
Mode inventory at all.

- `turn.ExecutedToolCallRecorder.AttachToCompactionPrompt(items, enabled)`
  mirrors Rust `attach_to_compaction_prompt`: with capture disabled the direct
  records already in history are removed so compaction cannot replay them;
  otherwise the pending Code Mode observations are attached **without**
  consuming them (a compaction request is not the sampling window that commits
  an attachment). Go's recorder state is per-thread and created on first record,
  so the caller passes the session's `executed_tool_call_metadata` enablement
  explicitly where Rust reads the recorder's own state existence.
- `agentCompactRunner` now carries the thread's recorder and that enablement
  flag (`compactRunnerForRecord` resolves the feature for the compaction turn's
  effective config) and applies the attach to the compaction prompt items.

Evidence: `turn.TestExecutedToolCallRecorderAttachesCodeModeMetadataToCompactionPromptLikeRust`
(the pending cell inventory lands on the prompt, a later sampling attach still
carries it, and disabling capture strips direct records while leaving Code Mode
cells intact) and
`appserver.TestAgentCompactRunnerAttachesCodeModeMetadataLikeRust` (the remote
compaction request's `InputItems` carry `executed_tool_calls` / `mcp__echo`).

Remaining nuances from Rust's version: the independent `MetadataBudgetScope`
accounting (with `CodeModeOnly`, direct records captured in history must not
compete with the Code Mode recorder's budget) and the retained-observation
handling across a shortened compaction retry. Go bounds metadata in the model
request builder for every request; implementing the scope needs a per-request
budget-scope flag.

## sync60: shared platform identity and executor-OS socket path validation (#46302, #46334)

Upstream was re-checked (`git fetch origin main`) and is unchanged at
`78245b47af`, so this pass lands the two queued network/permissions-lane items
whose Go surface was scoped but not built: the shared `Platform` identity and
the executor-OS-aware unix-socket policy validation. Both are portable, and the
Rust test matrices translate directly.

### Landed: the shared `Platform` identity (`utils/platform.go`)

Rust moved the platform identity into `codex-utils-path-uri`
(`src/platform.rs`): an explicit `Platform` value with metadata parsing
(`from_platform_os`), native detection (`native`), and platform-derived path
conventions (`path_convention`, returning `None` for unknown metadata). Go now
mirrors it:

- `utils.Platform` (`PlatformLinux`/`PlatformMacos`/`PlatformWindows`/
  `PlatformUnknown`), with `String()` using Rust's Debug spelling because it is
  embedded in validation error messages.
- `utils.PlatformFromOS` preserves missing and unrecognized metadata as
  `PlatformUnknown` (never substituting the current host), and
  `utils.NativePlatform` maps `runtime.GOOS` (`darwin` -> `Macos`).
- `utils.Platform.PathConvention` derives `ConventionPosix` / `ConventionWindows`
  or reports absence for unknown metadata, reusing the existing
  `utils.PathConvention` type (`PathUri::infer_path_convention`'s counterpart).

Evidence: `utils.TestPlatformFromOSPreservesMissingAndUnknownLikeRust`,
`utils.TestPlatformPathConventionLikeRust`, and
`utils.TestNativePlatformMatchesProcessMetadataLikeRust` — the three Rust
`platform_tests.rs` cases.

### Landed: executor-OS unix-socket policy validation (#46302)

Rust threaded an executor OS through network policy validation so a controller
and its executor can run different operating systems: allowed socket paths must
be NUL-free and absolute **for the executor OS**, deny entries are preserved
unchanged, and unknown metadata accepts either absolute syntax
(`network-proxy/src/socket_path.rs`: `socket_path_is_absolute` plus the
host-independent `windows_path_is_absolute`). Go previously rejected anything
that did not start with `/`, and skipped validation entirely on a Windows
controller, so a Windows path valid on the executor was rejected by a Linux
controller (and a relative path was accepted on Windows).

- `network.SocketPathIsAbsolute(platform, path)` mirrors
  `socket_path_is_absolute`: Unix-style `/` prefixes stay valid on every
  platform, the Windows grammar is consulted only for Windows or unknown
  metadata, and unknown metadata accepts either syntax.
- `windowsPathIsAbsolute` ports Rust's byte-level std-parity check exactly
  (drive-letter roots, `\\?\` verbatim, `\\.\` device roots, and UNC shares with
  non-empty server and share segments).
- `network.ValidateProxyUnixSocketAllowlistPathsForPlatform` and
  `network.ResolveProxyRuntimeForPlatform` mirror
  `validate_unix_socket_allowlist_paths` / `resolve_runtime(cfg, executor_os)`,
  including Rust's message
  (`invalid network.allow_unix_sockets[i]: expected a NUL-free absolute path for
  <Platform>, got "<path>"`). `NewProxySpecForPlatform` mirrors
  `build_config_state(config, constraints, executor_os)`, validating the
  composed config. The existing `ValidateProxyUnixSocketAllowlistPaths` /
  `ResolveProxyRuntime` / `NewProxySpec` delegate with `utils.NativePlatform()`,
  which is the correct identity at Go's two production sites: the local
  app-server spec (`build_config_state_for_spec`) and the exec-server process
  proxy, which runs on the executor host itself.

Evidence: `network.TestSocketPathValidationUsesExecutorPlatformLikeRust` (the
full Rust `resolve_runtime_validates_allow_unix_sockets_for_executor_os` matrix:
relative, `~/`, Unix absolute, `C:\`, `C:/`, UNC, `\\?\`,
`\\.\pipe\...`, drive-relative, drive-root-relative, bare UNC, and the two NUL
cases across Linux/Macos/Windows/Unknown),
`network.TestUnixSocketAllowlistAcceptsUnixStyleAbsoluteAndLeavesDenyUnchangedLikeRust`
(the Rust `resolve_runtime_accepts_unix_style_absolute_allow_unix_sockets_entries`
case, which pins that deny entries are never validated),
`network.TestSocketPathIsAbsoluteLikeRust`, and
`network.TestNewProxySpecValidatesSocketAllowlistForExecutorOSLikeRust`
(`C:\example.sock` is accepted for a Windows or unknown executor but rejected
for Linux; relative paths are rejected everywhere; a managed requirements
entry joins the same validation).

### Recorded remainder in this lane

- The remote-executor carrier: Rust validates the per-command state on the
  controller with the environment's `executor_platform_os`
  (`tools/network_approval.rs`), while Go's remote path serializes the
  controller-built `RemoteNetworkProxyConfig` and the exec-server validates it
  with its own native OS (`execserver.prepareExecutorNetworkProxy`). Go's
  environment records carry `PlatformOS`, so wiring an explicit executor
  platform through `buildManagedNetworkProxyConfigForCWD` -> `NewProxySpecForPlatform`
  is the remaining step for a cross-OS remote executor; the `*ForPlatform`
  entry points landed here are the seam it needs.
- `effective_sandbox_mode` (the other portable half of #46334) has no Go
  consumer yet: Go's legacy resolver
  (`config.resolveLegacySandboxPermissionProfileWithWorkspaceRoots`) returns nil
  when `sandbox_mode` is unset and has no trusted-project default or
  Windows-disabled -> read-only fallback to attach the helper to.

### Verification

- `go build ./...` green; `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file.
- `go test ./network/ ./utils/ ./execserver/ ./config/ ./appserver/ ./tool/
  ./exec/ -count=1` green, and `go test ./... -count=1` -> 97 packages `ok` with
  no `FAIL` (one earlier run reported a transient failure that did not reproduce
  on two consecutive full runs, consistent with the recorded suite flakiness).

## sync61: keep remote workspace roots under server control (#46494)

Upstream was re-checked (`git fetch origin main`) and is unchanged at
`78245b47af`. This pass lands the queued #46494 item, which had two portable
halves: rejecting client-host workspace-root overrides with `--remote`, and
preserving the active session's server-resolved roots when a Session-sourced
fork happens.

### Re-checked: the remote lifecycle already omits client roots

Rust #46494 makes remote `thread/start`, `thread/resume`, and `thread/fork`
requests omit client-configured `runtimeWorkspaceRoots` (client config paths
belong to the client host) and adopt the roots the server reports instead. Go
already has that shape:

- `app.remoteThreadStartParams` / `app.remoteSideThreadForkParams` never send
  `RuntimeWorkspaceRoots` (the remote requests carry only the shared CLI
  settings), so the server resolves its defaults.
- `app.remoteTUISettingsFromResume` / `remoteTUISettingsFromStartedThread` adopt
  the response's `RuntimeWorkspaceRoots` into the TUI settings, and
  `app.dynamic_tools` seeds a resumed thread's start params from the resumed
  roots.

The picker fork (`app.remoteSessionActionHandler`'s `SessionSelectionFork`) also
omits the roots; Go's server restores the parent record's saved roots in that
case (`appserver.threadRecordRuntimeWorkspaceRoots(record, cwd, nil)`), which is
at least as strong as Rust's server default.

### Landed: `--add-dir` / `writable_roots` are rejected with `--remote`

Rust rejects client workspace-root overrides before connecting, with distinct
texts. Go now mirrors it in `app.interactiveRemoteWorkspaceRootError`, called
from `runInteractive` before the remote endpoint is resolved/connected:

- `--add-dir` + `--remote` ->
  `--add-dir is not supported with --remote. Configure additional workspace roots on the server.`
- an override for `sandbox_workspace_write.writable_roots` (either the dotted key
  or an inline `sandbox_workspace_write={writable_roots=...}` table) + `--remote`
  -> `sandbox_workspace_write.writable_roots overrides are not supported with --remote. Configure additional workspace roots on the server.`
- every other sandbox override (for example
  `sandbox_workspace_write.network_access`) still proceeds, matching Rust's
  `remote_start_allows_network_access_overrides_before_requiring_terminal`.
- Embedded launches keep explicit roots (the check only fires when `--remote` is
  set), matching Rust's `Embedded => Some(config.workspace_roots)`.
- The `codex resume|fork|archive|unarchive|delete|queue --remote` surface runs
  the same check (`sessionRemoteWorkspaceRootError`, called from
  `resolveSessionRemoteEndpoint`), since Rust validates the merged TUI CLI for
  every interactive command.

The override scan reuses `config.ParseOverrides`, the same parser the existing
legacy-sandbox-mode check uses, so it understands both `key=value` spellings.
A malformed override is left to the normal config load rather than reported here.

Evidence: `app.TestInteractiveRemoteWorkspaceRootRejectionsLikeRust` (all six
cases above, including the two network-access spellings), the fatal-exit surface
`app.TestInteractiveRemoteAddDirFailsFatalLikeRust`, and
`app.TestInteractiveRemoteNetworkAccessOverrideProceedsPastRootCheck` (the
ordering Rust pins), plus `app.TestSessionRemoteWorkspaceRootRejectionsLikeRust`
for the session commands.

### Landed: Session-sourced forks forward the active session's roots

Rust's `ForkConfigSource::Session` (the side-conversation and active-session
forks) explicitly sets `runtime_workspace_roots` because "fork does not restore
saved roots when omitted" and the fork's CWD override would otherwise drop the
server-resolved selection. Go now does the same:

- the TUI tracks the active thread's server-resolved roots
  (`tea.Model.sessionWorkspaceRoots`, populated in
  `applyThreadSettingsValues` from `appserver.Settings.RuntimeWorkspaceRoots`,
  the same settings snapshot the server sends for resume/switch and settings
  updates);
- `SideStartParams` gained `RuntimeWorkspaceRoots`, and
  `startSideConversation` forwards the tracked roots;
- `app.remoteSideThreadForkParams` and the local
  `interactiveLocalSideCoordinator.Start` set
  `ThreadForkParams.RuntimeWorkspaceRoots` from them; the local coordinator
  falls back to the parent record's saved `runtime_workspace_roots` when the TUI
  has not reported any, so an embedded side conversation with a CWD override no
  longer drops the parent's extra roots.

Evidence: `app.TestInteractiveRemoteStartSideForksAndInjectsBoundary` now asserts
the fork request carries the session's two roots alongside the CWD override;
`app.TestInteractiveLocalSideCoordinatorForksInjectsAndDeletes` asserts the
embedded side record keeps the parent's roots; and
`tea.TestSideCommandForwardsSessionWorkspaceRootsLikeRust` pins the TUI
forwarding.

### Recorded remainder

Seeding the TUI's tracked roots from the embedded `thread/start` response:
`app.interactiveStartLocalTUIThread` ignores the start response's
`RuntimeWorkspaceRoots`, so the embedded TUI only tracks roots once a settings
update arrives. The local side-coordinator fallback above already keeps the
behavior correct; seeding the model directly would remove the fallback's
dependency on stored record metadata.

### Verification

- `go build ./...` green; `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file; `go vet` clean
  on the touched packages.
- `go test ./app/ ./tui/... ./appserver/ ./cli/ ./session/ -count=1` green, and
  `go test ./... -count=1` -> 97 packages `ok` with no `FAIL`.

## sync62: triage re-audit and two core classification fixes (#45475, #45602)

Upstream stays at `78245b47af`. Rather than trusting the earlier theme-level
dispositions, this pass re-derived the triage from scratch: it enumerated every
commit in `5b1d656018..78245b47af`, extracted each PR number, and checked which
numbers the plan never mentions. 120 of 319 numbers were absent, most because
the plan grouped them by theme (Guardian crate moves, the Windows sandbox
service, analytics) - but the sweep exposed two genuinely untriaged,
Go-visible core fixes, which are landed here.

### Landed: fuzzy match scoring inside a lowercase expansion (#45475)

Rust fixed `codex-utils-fuzzy-match`: it reconstructed the first matched lowered
position by scanning the lowercased text for the original index, which lands on
the expansion's *first* character. A match beginning inside a lowercase
expansion (for example the combining dot of `İ` -> `i\u0307`) therefore received
an incorrect prefix bonus or gap penalty, so strings that lowercase identically
ranked differently.

Go's `filesearch.FuzzyMatch` is the direct port and carried the same
reconstruction:

```go
targetOriginal := result[0]
for index, originalIndex := range loweredToOriginal { ... }
```

It now records `firstLowerPos` at the matched lowered offset directly, which is
Rust's fix, and scores from it (the prefix bonus only applies when the first
*lowered* match is at index 0).

Evidence: `filesearch.TestFuzzyMatchScoresWithinLowercaseExpansionLikeRust`
ports Rust's new regression expectations at the function level: for the query
`"\u0307x"`, `İx`/`i\u0307x`/`aİx`/`ai\u0307x` return indices `[0,1]`/`[1,2]`/
`[1,2]`/`[2,3]` with score 0 each (the pre-fix code produced -100 for `İx` and 1
for `aİx`), and each lowercase-identical pair now scores identically. The
pre-existing `TestFuzzyMatchUnicodeLowercaseExpansionDedupe` still passes.

The user-visible site in Rust is the skill popup, which calls `fuzzy_match` for
ranking *and* highlighting. Go's skill popup ranks through its own
`fuzzySkillScore` (a position-based score that returns no highlight indices and
has no lowercasing expansion step), so it does not share the bug; adopting
`FuzzyMatch` there would be a separate TUI change and is recorded, not assumed.

### Landed: retry classification for throttling and quota errors (#45602)

Rust split `slow_down` out of the overload classification and broadened the
quota codes:

- HTTP 503 bodies: `server_is_overloaded` keeps the terminal overload kind,
  `slow_down` becomes a retryable rate limit carrying the server message, and
  any other code stays generic.
- SSE `response.failed`: `slow_down` is a retryable rate limit (not an
  overload), and retry timing is parsed from its message.
- SSE quota exhaustion now includes `credit_balance_exhausted`,
  `organization_spend_limit_exceeded`, and `project_spend_limit_exceeded`
  alongside `insufficient_quota`, so those terminate without retries.

Go already had the HTTP 429 quota set (including `organization_usage_limit_exceeded`)
from #44492, but the streaming path still mapped `slow_down` to
`ErrorServerOverloaded` and only recognized `insufficient_quota`:

- `model.responseFailedError`: `server_is_overloaded` alone maps to
  `ErrorServerOverloaded`; `rate_limit_exceeded`/`slow_down` map to
  `ErrorRateLimitExceeded` with the parsed delay; the four quota codes map to
  `ErrorQuotaExceeded`.
- `model.responseFailedRetryDelay` accepts `slow_down`, matching Rust's
  `try_parse_retry_after`.
- `model.responsesHTTPError` classified a 503 body: `server_is_overloaded` ->
  `ErrorServerOverloaded`, `slow_down` -> `ErrorRateLimitExceeded` preserving
  the server message, anything else -> the generic `ResponsesAPIError`.
  A 503 previously produced only the generic error, so Go never produced the
  overload kind from the HTTP path at all.
- `model.isRetryableResponsesStreamError` now treats `ErrorRateLimitExceeded`
  as retryable. Go's predicate is status-based for the unlisted kinds, and the
  streaming rate-limit error carries no HTTP status, so a rate limit was
  previously terminal despite Rust retrying it.

Evidence: `model.TestResponseFailedErrorDistinguishesCapacityFromSlowDownLikeRust`
(slow_down kind + preserved message + retryable + 3s parsed delay; overload
message preserved; each spend/credit code terminating as quota and not
retryable), `model.TestResponsesHTTPErrorDistinguishesCapacityFromSlowDownLikeRust`
(the three 503 bodies, mirroring Rust's
`map_api_error_distinguishes_capacity_from_slow_down`), and the existing
`TestResponseFailedErrorClassifiesRateLimitExceededLikeRust` unchanged.

### Recorded (re-checked, no Go delta)

- #45928 (bound model catalog decode errors; classify catalog refresh deadlines
  as `RequestTimeout`): Go's `decodeModelsEndpointResponse` discards the raw
  `encoding/json` error and returns its own bounded message
  (`models API response did not contain models`), so Go never surfaces an
  unbounded serde-style payload dump; and Go's catalog refresh is best-effort
  (`fetchAndUpdateModels` ignores the error, and `RefreshAfterAuthChange`
  bounds itself with a timer), so there is no `Timeout`-vs-`RequestTimeout`
  classification to change. Rust's delta is real but has no Go landing site.

### Newly identified queued lane

- #46112 (preserve filesystem sandbox policy context when the cwd disappears):
  a large, portable exec-server change - a required policy `cwd` in
  `FileSystemSandboxContext`, explicit `policyContext` in the filesystem RPCs
  with legacy fallback at executor ingress, executor-file-URI permission paths,
  and Windows relative denial globs bound before the helper's launch directory
  changes (`exec-server-protocol/src/protocol.rs` +405, `fs_sandbox.rs` +243,
  plus the capability-discovery and path-URI suites). Go has the
  `execserver` filesystem RPC surface, so this is a real lane, but it needs its
  own pass to mirror the wire additions without regressing the existing
  `validateWirePathURI` contract.

### Verification

- `go build ./...` green; `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file.
- `go test ./model/ ./appserver/ ./exec/ ./turn/ ./codexapi/ ./filesearch/
  ./parity/ -count=1` green, and `go test ./... -count=1` -> 97 packages `ok`
  with no `FAIL`.

## sync63: filter saved reasoning overrides when the feature is disabled (#46291)

The PR-number sweep in sync62 flagged another untriaged item, landed here.

### Landed: request input drops saved configuration updates when disabled

Rust's `ModelClient` carries the `reasoning_effort_override` feature flag and,
when it is off, retains-out `ResponseItem::ConfigurationUpdate` items from the
formatted request input - so resuming a thread after disabling the feature stops
re-sending saved updates until compaction, while the persisted history keeps
them.

Go had the detector (`isConfigurationUpdateInputItem`) and the trusted-item
plumbing, but `historyInputItemsForTurn` replayed every saved
`configuration_update` from the record unconditionally, so a resumed thread
still sent them with the feature off:

- `appserver.rejectConfigurationUpdateInputItems` drops those items from a
  request-input slice (other items untouched).
- `appTurnConfig` applies it to the history-derived items only, gated on
  `reasoningEffortFeatureEnabled(cfg)` - the same feature-only gate Rust uses
  (not the provider/model/exemption gate that decides whether a *new* override
  is recorded). The current turn's own trusted override items are appended
  later from `params.AdditionalInputItems`, so they are unaffected, and the
  persisted record is never rewritten.

Evidence: `appserver.TestAppTurnConfigFiltersSavedConfigurationUpdatesWhenDisabledLikeRust`
builds a store whose thread history holds a harness-authored
`configuration_update` plus user/assistant messages, then calls the real
`appTurnConfig`: with the feature off the run config's `InputItems` carry no
`configuration_update` while the saved user message survives; with the feature
on the saved update is preserved. The test also re-reads the store to confirm
the persisted history still contains the item in both cases.

### Newly identified queued lanes

- #46004 (preserve attachment Unix socket grants when the controller policy is
  omitted): Rust made `dangerously_allow_all_unix_sockets` an `Option<bool>` end
  to end and kept explicitly empty `unix_sockets` maps, so an omitted controller
  policy defers to the attachment instead of acting as a denial. Go's
  `network.ProxySettings.DangerouslyAllowAllUnixSockets` is a plain `bool`
  (`network/proxy_config.go`, set by `proxy_config_values.go`) and its
  `UnixSockets` map is dropped when empty, so omission and explicit `false`
  are indistinguishable and the attachment-grant deferral has no Go seam.
  Landing it means threading presence through `ProxySettings`,
  `ProxyConstraints`, `NewProxySpec`, and the remote launch config - the
  `*ForPlatform`-style entry points from sync60 are the precedent for how to do
  that without breaking the existing callers.
- (from sync62) #46112 (filesystem sandbox policy context when the cwd
  disappears) remains queued with its scope recorded above.

### Verification

- `go build ./...` green; `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file.
- `go test ./appserver/ ./model/ ./turn/ ./exec/ -count=1` green, and
  `go test ./... -count=1` -> 97 packages `ok` with no `FAIL`.

## sync64: attachment unix-socket grants honor an omitted controller policy (#46004)

The #46004 lane recorded in sync63 is landed here.

### The divergence

Rust made `dangerously_allow_all_unix_sockets` an `Option<bool>` end to end and
kept explicitly empty `unix_sockets` maps, so an **omitted** controller socket
policy defers to the attachment while an explicit `false` (or an explicitly
empty map) stays a restrictive ceiling. Go modelled the flag twice as a plain
`bool` and collapsed empty maps to nil in three places, so omission and explicit
`false` were indistinguishable and an execution environment could never supply
its own Unix socket grants.

### Landed

- `network.ProxySettings.DangerouslyAllowAllUnixSockets` and
  `network.Config.DangerouslyAllowAllUnixSockets` are now `*bool` (Rust's
  `Option<bool>`); `DefaultProxySettings` leaves it absent, and
  `applyRequirements`/`cloneConfig` carry the pointer.
- `network.boolPointerValue` is the single `unwrap_or(false)` helper, used at
  every "ordinary execution" reader: the bind clamp, the proxy's socket
  authorization, `ValidateAgainstConstraints`, and the remote launch config
  conversion (`execserver.RemoteNetworkProxyConfigFromProxyConfig` resolves
  omitted -> false and `ProxyConfig()` resolves the wire bool -> `Some(bool)`,
  exactly as Rust's `remote_config.rs`).
- `ProxySettings.SetAllowUnixSockets` and the values parser keep an explicitly
  empty list/table as a present-but-empty permission map instead of dropping it
  to nil.
- `EnvironmentNetworkPolicy.ApplyTo` now computes
  `inheritedAllowAll = flag.unwrap_or(sockets == nil)` **before** swapping in
  the attachment's socket map, which is the whole point of the Rust fix: an
  omitted controller policy leaves socket permissions to the attachment, while
  explicit denials, finite grants, explicitly empty maps, and managed
  requirements all narrow it. The inherited-denial and owner-deny precedence is
  unchanged.
- Also aligned in the same function: `allow_upstream_proxy` and
  `allow_local_binding` are ANDed with the attachment policy (Rust's
  `&=`/'&&'), where Go previously ORed them and could only widen.
- `NewSpecForEnvironment` logs the composition (Rust's `tracing::debug!`
  "resolved environment network policy").

### Evidence

- `network.TestAttachmentSocketGrantsRespectConfiguredRestrictionsLikeRust`
  ports Rust's `attachment_socket_grants_respect_configured_restrictions_at_remote_launch`
  matrix byte for byte: omitted -> granted, explicit deny -> denied, explicit
  allow -> granted, finite grants -> denied-but-preserved, `unix_sockets = {}` ->
  denied, managed `Some(false)` -> denied.
- `network.TestEnvironmentNetworkPolicyFromConfigResolvesOmittedFlagLikeRust`
  pins `from_config`'s `unwrap_or(false)` (controller-only commands never
  inherit grants).
- `network.TestProxySettingsPreserveOmittedAndEmptyUnixSocketPolicyLikeRust` and
  `network.TestSetAllowUnixSocketsRetainsExplicitlyEmptyCeilingLikeRust` pin the
  parsed-config presence semantics.
- `execserver.TestRemoteNetworkProxyConfigRoundTripsSupportedRustFields` now
  round-trips through the optional field.

### Recorded nuance

The composition surface Go now matches (`network.Config`,
`NewSpecForEnvironment`, `EnvironmentNetworkPolicy`) still has no production
caller - the app-server managed network resolves through `ProxyConfig` /
`ProxySettings`. The presence semantics are landed on `ProxySettings` too, so
they are live wherever the proxy actually authorizes a Unix socket; wiring the
attachment-policy composition into the app-server remains part of the broader
"Unix socket grants in Seatbelt/Linux managed sandbox" lane (#45548/#45534).

### Verification

- `go build ./...` green; `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file; `go vet`
  clean on the touched packages.
- `go test ./network/ ./execserver/ -count=1` green, and
  `go test ./... -count=1` -> 97 packages `ok` with no `FAIL`.

## sync65: shortened skill descriptions no longer warn (#46070)

### Landed

Rust removed the description-shortening warning entirely: fitting the skills
catalog by shortening descriptions is expected behavior, and only omitted
skills warn. Go's `prompt.skillRenderWarning` still emitted
`SkillDescriptionTruncatedWarning` (or the `2%` variant) whenever the average
truncated description length exceeded 100 characters, so a bounded catalog
raised a warning Rust no longer produces.

- The truncation branch is gone (`skillRenderWarning` now returns nil unless
  skills were actually omitted), and the three constants it used
  (`SkillDescriptionWarningThreshold`, `SkillDescriptionTruncatedWarning`,
  `SkillDescriptionTruncatedWarningWithPercent`) are removed.
- The omitted-skills warning, including its `2%` phrasing, is unchanged.

Evidence: `prompt.TestRenderAvailableSkillsShortenedDescriptionsDoNotWarnLikeRust`
(a bounded catalog that still shortens every description now reports
`TruncatedDescriptionChars > 0` with a nil warning) and the updated
`prompt.TestRenderExtensionAvailableSkillsPreservesEntriesBeforeOmittingLikeRust`
(all ten entries included with shortened descriptions, no warning). The
omitted-skills warning stays pinned by
`prompt.TestRenderAvailableSkillsTokenBudgetWarningMentionsPercentLikeRust`.

### Newly identified queued lane

- #46081 (mark finished empty Code Mode tool inventories as complete): Rust
  retains a completed, losslessly recorded cell even when it recorded no tool
  calls, and always emits `executed_tool_calls` alongside `tool_calls_complete`
  (using `[]` for an empty inventory). Go's
  `turn.ExecutedToolCallRecorder.AttachPendingToPrompt` only attaches metadata
  `if len(calls) > 0`, so an empty finished cell still omits completeness - the
  same observable gap. Landing it means reworking the cell-completion
  bookkeeping (`startedCells`/`invalidCells`/`groups`) to distinguish a closed
  lossless-but-empty inventory from an unobserved one; that recorder carries a
  dense invariant suite, so it needs its own pass.

### Verification

- `go build ./...` green; `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file.
- `go test ./prompt/ ./appserver/ ./turn/ ./tool/ ./app/ -count=1` green, and
  `go test ./... -count=1` -> 97 packages `ok` with no `FAIL`.

## sync66: nonfatal clock read errors (#46006 and the inert feature)

### The divergence

Rust's `Session::read_clock_for_context` is shared by the reminder and the
environment-date reads. On a clock-provider failure it either fails fatally
(feature off) or, with `nonfatal_clock_read_errors` enabled, records one
`CurrentTimeUnavailable` developer fragment per turn and context window and
continues without a clock value. #46006 then clears `last_clock_failure` on a
successful read so a *new* outage after recovery reports again.

Go had the feature key in the registry ([sync39] section 1) but no behavior:
the reminder and environment-date reads both propagated the failure (failing the
turn), the clock tools returned a wrapped error, and there was no
`CurrentTimeUnavailable` fragment, notice, or dedupe.

### Landed

- `context.CurrentTimeUnavailable` (role developer, kind
  `current_time.unavailable`, markers `<current_time_unavailable>`,
  body `failed to read current time`) plus the shared
  `CurrentTimeUnavailableMessage` constant, mirroring Rust's fragment and its
  `MESSAGE`.
- `RuntimeRouter.readClockForContext` is the shared read: success clears the
  recorded failure (#46006); failure with the feature off returns Rust's fatal
  `failed to read current time: ...`; failure with the feature on logs and
  returns `emitNotice` from `noteClockFailure`, which dedupes on the
  `(turnID, contextWindowID)` key. `clearClockFailure` deletes the record.
- The reminder read (`currentTimeReminderInputItems`) uses it: a handled failure
  inserts the notice and skips the reminder, and a deduplicated read inserts
  nothing.
- The environment-date reads use it too (both the per-turn input item and the
  compaction environment context): `environmentClockForTurn` returns a nil time
  on a handled failure, `turnEnvironmentContextTextAtDate` omits
  `<current_date>`, and the notice is recorded before the environment section,
  as Rust does. `turnEnvironmentContextInputItemForTurn` now returns the extra
  items/session items so the notice reaches history.
- `isForkExcludedDeveloperText` excludes the unavailable marker alongside the
  reminder marker, matching Rust's `CurrentTimeUnavailable::matches_text` in the
  subagent fork filter.
- The clock tools gate their failures the same way: with the feature off they
  are `Fatal("failed to read current time: ...")` / `Fatal("failed to sleep:
  ...")`, and with it on they respond to the model with the Rust message. The
  flag threads through `turn.ToolOptions` into `tool.CoreHandlerOptions`.

### Evidence

- `context.TestCurrentTimeUnavailable` pins the fragment's role, kind, markers,
  body, and rendered text.
- `tool.TestClockHandlersGateFailuresOnNonfatalClockReadErrorsLikeRust` covers
  both tools in both feature states (model-visible notice vs fatal error text).
- `appserver.TestRuntimeRouterNonfatalClockFailureRecordsNoticeLikeRust` drives a
  real turn against a failing `currentTime/read` sink: the turn completes, the
  model request carries exactly one `<current_time_unavailable>` notice, no
  reminder is injected, and the environment context renders without
  `<current_date>`.
- `appserver.TestCurrentTimeReminderClockFailureDedupeLikeRust` pins the dedupe
  rule: same turn+window shares one notice, a new turn reports again, a new
  context window reports again, and `clearClockFailure` (the #46006 fix) lets a
  post-recovery outage report again.
- The pre-existing fatal-path test
  (`TestRuntimeRouterEnvironmentClockFailureStopsBeforeInferenceLikeRust`) still
  passes unchanged, proving the feature-off behavior is preserved.

### Recorded nuance

The post-tool reminder path swallows a *fatal* (feature-off) clock failure
because `turn.ToolPostExecutionInputItems` has no error return, where Rust's
`maybe_record_current_time_reminder` propagates it. With the feature on the
behavior matches (the notice is recorded); only the feature-off post-tool
failure differs, and the turn-start read still fails the turn there.

### Verification

- `go build ./...` green; `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file; `go vet`
  clean on the touched packages.
- `go test ./context/ ./tool/ ./appserver/ ./turn/ ./model/ ./exec/ ./app/
  ./rollout/ ./prompt/ -count=1` green, and `go test ./... -count=1` -> 97
  packages `ok` with no `FAIL`.

## sync67: preserve configured Flex tiers and omit service tiers on Bedrock (#46230)

Found by the sync62 PR-number sweep and landed here.

### Landed (core half)

Rust's `get_service_tier(configured, fast_mode_enabled, model_info)` now returns
a configured `flex` tier even when fast mode is disabled or the catalog does not
advertise it; every other tier still requires fast mode and catalog support. The
same commit makes the request builder omit `service_tier` for Amazon Bedrock
("Bedrock only supports the implicit default tier, including with custom
catalogs").

Go gated every configured tier on `fast_mode` and then ran
`ServiceTierForRequest` (which requires catalog support), so a configured `flex`
was silently dropped whenever fast mode was off or the catalog omitted it:

- `model.ServiceTierForConfiguredRequest` mirrors Rust's helper (flex survives
  unconditionally, other tiers keep the fast-mode + support gate), and
  `model.IsFlexServiceTier` exposes the exact-match `flex` test.
- The four configured-tier sites use it: `appserver.appServiceTierForTurn`,
  `appserver.threadStartServiceTier`, the subagent root-tier override
  (`runtime after #41308`), and `exec.effectiveServiceTier`.
- `model.ResponsesAgentRunner.serviceTierForRequest` omits `service_tier` for an
  Amazon Bedrock provider, applied at both request builders (the SSE/HTTP one
  and the websocket one).

Evidence: `model.TestServiceTierForConfiguredRequestLikeRust` (flex with and
without fast mode, flex without catalog support, ordinary tiers still gated,
default dropped, exact-match normalization) and
`model.TestResponsesAgentRunnerOmitsServiceTierForBedrockLikeRust` (a body-level
check: an OpenAI provider keeps `service_tier: "flex"` while a Bedrock provider
omits the field entirely).

### Recorded remainders for this commit

- TUI: Rust's `tui/src/service_tier_resolution.rs` and `app_server_session.rs`
  changes (connected-thread startup and turn submission) are the TUI half, kept
  for the TUI lane per the core-first ordering.
- `unsupported_service_tier_warning` is unchanged by #46230 and has no Go
  counterpart; recorded rather than invented.

### Newly identified lane: bundled/executor plugin cleanup hooks

The same sweep exposed an area the plan never triaged: Rust's temporary
cleanup-hook allowlist (`plugin/src/bundled_hooks.rs`) plus the executor
admission that resolves trusted `codex_apps` routing
(`core-plugins/src/executor_hooks.rs`), consumed by both local and executor hook
discovery (`hooks/src/engine/discovery.rs`). It admits only known cleanup
handlers from unsigned bundled plugins - `browser@openai-bundled`,
`chrome*@openai-bundled`, `computer-use@openai-bundled` ->
`node_repl.turn_ended`, `unified-computer-use@openai-bundled` ->
`cua_repl.turn_ended`, and `browser@openai-curated-remote` ->
`codex_apps.browser.turn_ended` **only** when the handler has no matcher, empty
input, and the caller's enabled-tool catalog registers that connector.
#46029 is only the `Interrupt` addition to that list.

Go's `appserver.hooks_discovery` admits plugin hook sources with no allowlist and
models no app-connector requirement, so the lane is the allowlist plus its
exact-match admission rules first, then the executor admission/routing metadata.

### Verification

- `go build ./...` green; `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file. (`go vet`
  reports the pre-existing copy-lock warnings on `model/responses_agent.go` and
  `exec/agent_controller.go`, unchanged at HEAD.)
- `go test ./model/ ./exec/ -count=1` green, and `go test ./... -count=1` -> 97
  packages `ok` with no `FAIL`.

## sync68: bundled plugin cleanup-hook allowlist (#46029 and Rust's bundled_hooks)

The lane identified in sync67 is started here.

### The divergence

Rust keeps a temporary allowlist for cleanup hooks declared by unsigned bundled
plugins (`plugin/src/bundled_hooks.rs`), consumed by local hook discovery
(`hooks/src/engine/discovery.rs`) and by the executor plugin-hook admission
(`core-plugins/src/executor_hooks.rs`). A matching handler becomes `builtin`,
which (a) enables it and marks it trusted without a recorded hash
(`hook_enabled` / `hook_trust_status`) and (b) hides it from the public
`hooks/list` projection (`app-server/src/request_processors/catalog_processor.rs`).
#46029 is only the `Interrupt` addition to that list.

Go admitted every plugin hook with no allowlist and had no builtin trust
concept at all, so a bundled cleanup hook was untrusted (and therefore not
executed without an explicit trust record) and would have appeared in the public
list.

### Landed

- `appserver/bundled_hooks.go`: the seven-entry allowlist plus
  `isAllowlistedBundledCleanupHook` with Rust's exact rules - the handler must be
  `mcp_tool`, the matcher absent, and the plugin id, event, server, and tool must
  match; the Apps target additionally requires an empty input template and the
  registered connector id.
- `HookMetadata.Builtin` is internal (`json:"-"`), matching Rust's note that
  builtin hooks stay available internally but are omitted from the public list;
  `hookEnabled` and `hookTrustStatus` gained Rust's `isBuiltin` parameter
  (builtin -> enabled and trusted without a hash, before the managed branch).
- Plugin discovery computes `builtin` per handler from the source plugin id and
  passes it to both the command and MCP-tool metadata.
- `handleHooksList` hides builtin entries from the public response, while
  `hooksForCWD` (execution) and `compact_hooks` keep using the unfiltered
  discovery so the engine still runs them.

### Evidence

- `appserver.TestAllowlistedBundledCleanupHooksLikeRust`: thirteen cases - the
  bundled `node_repl` cleanup, #46029's `Interrupt`, `cua_repl` via
  `unified-computer-use`, an event outside the list, colliding tool and server
  names, an unknown plugin, a present matcher, a command handler, the Apps
  target with no connector catalog / the registered connector / a colliding
  connector / manifest-provided arguments.
- `appserver.TestBundledCleanupHookIsBuiltinAndHiddenFromThePublicListLikeRust`:
  discovery marks the allowlisted handler builtin + enabled + trusted while the
  non-allowlisted handler in the same plugin stays untrusted; the public list
  contains only the non-allowlisted handler, and the discovered response (which
  the engine consumes) is unchanged by the projection.

### Recorded remainder for this lane

- The executor half: Rust's `executor_plugin_hook_sources` filters
  executor-discovered manifests through the same allowlist using the
  enabled-tool catalog for `app_connector_id`, then resolves the trusted
  `codex_apps` routing metadata (`MCP_TOOL_CODEX_APPS_META_KEY` /
  `resource_uri`) onto the hook source. Go's exec-server capability discovery
  does not yet admit plugin hook sources, so the connector-aware admission has
  no Go seam yet; the allowlist function already takes `appConnectorID` so the
  admission can call it once that path exists.

### Verification

- `go build ./...` green; `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file; `go vet`
  clean on `./appserver/`.
- `go test ./appserver/ -count=1` green, and `go test ./... -count=1` -> 97
  packages `ok` with no `FAIL`.

## sync69: mark finished empty Code Mode tool inventories complete (#46081)

The lane recorded in sync63/67 is landed here.

### The divergence

Rust's recorder distinguishes a *Recording* cell (dispatch gate open) from a
*Complete* one (gate closed), and #46081 makes completion final even for an
empty inventory: `attach_to_prompt` attaches metadata when `calls` is non-empty
**or** the cell is complete, so a verified empty inventory reaches the model as
`executed_tool_calls: []` + `tool_calls_complete: true` instead of being
indistinguishable from an unverified one.

Go had no cell-finish concept at all: `RegisterCell` only opened a group, the
attach gate was `if len(calls) > 0`, the completion marker required an implicit
"the cell is done" that was never established, and both `marshal*` helpers
returned early when the call list was empty - so even a marker-carrying empty
inventory serialized as nothing.

### Landed

- `ExecutedToolCallRecorder.FinishCell` mirrors Rust's
  `finish_cell_recording`: it closes the cell's group
  (`recordedToolCallGroup.finished`).
- `toolDispatcher` calls it for a Code Mode exec/wait output that is not
  running (`codeModeOutputIsRunning` reads the `running` flag the code-mode
  tooling already emits), so a yielded cell keeps its inventory open and a
  finished/terminated one closes it.
- `AttachPendingToPrompt` now requires the closed gate for completeness
  (`complete = complete && groupFinished`) and attaches when
  `len(calls) > 0 || groupFinished`, so a finished empty cell still produces an
  attachment.
- `marshalToolResponseItem` and `marshalAgentItemWithExecutedToolCalls` emit the
  metadata document whenever a cell or completeness marker is present, using an
  explicit `[]` for an empty inventory (Rust #46081's "always include
  `executed_tool_calls` when setting `tool_calls_complete`").
- `ClearExecutedToolCalls` clears the cell and completeness marker alongside the
  calls, so the strip paths cannot leave a marker behind after the inventory is
  removed.

### Evidence

- `turn.TestExecutedToolCallRecorderAttachesEmptyCompleteInventoryLikeRust`: a
  cell finished with no nested calls attaches `cell_id`, `tool_calls_complete:
  true`, and an explicit empty `executed_tool_calls` list.
- `turn.TestExecutedToolCallRecorderOmitsCompletenessWhileCellRunsLikeRust`: a
  running cell with a recorded call attaches the call without the completeness
  marker, and an empty running cell attaches nothing.
- `turn.TestToolDispatcherClosesFinishedCodeModeCellsLikeRust`: drives the real
  dispatcher with a stub code-mode exec executor - a non-running output closes
  the cell (empty complete inventory) while a `running: true` output leaves it
  open (no attachment).
- The pre-existing recorder completeness tests were updated to call `FinishCell`
  for the cells they treat as finished, mirroring Rust's test change (its
  "unfinished" scenario is the new running-cell case above).

### Verification

- `go build ./...` green; `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file.
- `go test ./turn/ ./model/ ./appserver/ -count=1` green, and
  `go test ./... -count=1` -> 97 packages `ok` with no `FAIL`.

## sync70: caller-owned per-environment executor skill disablement (#46015)

Found by the sync62 PR-number sweep; landed here.

### The divergence

Rust's `ExecutorSkillProvider::with_disabled_skill_paths` lets a caller disable
specific executor skills per environment: the map is keyed by environment id and
holds SKILL.md documents in that executor's filesystem, and a matching catalog
entry is marked disabled in both discovery routes so it leaves the model-visible
catalog. Paths configured for another environment have no effect, and every
discovered skill stays enabled by default. Go's `skillprovider.CatalogEntry`
already had an `Enabled` field, but nothing could ever set it from a caller.

### Landed

- `RuntimeServices.DisabledExecutorSkillPaths map[string][]string` is the
  caller-owned surface (environment id -> SKILL.md paths), mirroring the Rust
  provider option.
- `appserver.executorSkillProviderCatalog` applies it: a matching entry is left
  out of the catalog (the same effect as its `Enabled` field).
- `executorSkillRootEnvironments` resolves each catalog entry's environment from
  the thread's selected capability roots. Go's executor catalog authority is the
  capability *root* id rather than the environment, so this mapping is what lets
  an environment-keyed disablement reach the right entry.
- `executorSkillPathEquals` compares the configured path and the entry's
  SKILL.md path (`SourcePath`, then the exposed `Path` locator) as path URIs -
  host-native paths are converted with `utils.FromHostNativePath` and locators
  with `utils.Parse`, so alias/format differences still match - with an exact
  string comparison as the fallback.

### Evidence

`appserver.TestRuntimeRouterDisabledExecutorSkillPathsLikeRust` drives a real
thread with a selected executor capability root: the skill is listed by default;
configuring its SKILL.md path for that environment removes it; the same path for
another environment leaves it in place; and the forward-slash and `file://`
spellings of the same document match too (Rust compares path URIs, not
spelling).

### Recorded nuance

Rust's provider option has no production caller either (the extension installer
and its tests are the only users), so this lands the capability at the same
level: a router-level option that defaults to nothing.

### Verification

- `go build ./...` green; `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file; `go vet`
  clean on `./appserver/`.
- `go test ./appserver/ -count=1` green, and `go test ./... -count=1` -> 97
  packages `ok` with no `FAIL`.

## sync71: preserve plugin caches across display metadata refreshes (#46309)

Found by the sync62/70 PR-number sweep; landed here.

### The divergence

Rust added `core-plugins/src/remote_metadata.rs` (commit `0c9be8a836`): a
display-only installed-plugin metadata refresh (a renewed image URL, an updated
store badge) must keep live MCP sessions and cached skill resources. The
cache-write snapshot now compares behavior-determining metadata before
invalidating:

- `installed_plugin_metadata_eq` compares marketplace name, id, name,
  `canonical_app_id`, version, `installed_at`, enablement, install policy and its
  source, the installation interstitial, authentication policy, availability,
  disabled reason, and eligible plan types. Display metadata (interface imagery,
  badges, keywords, descriptions) is excluded.
- The snapshot write returns whether the effective-plugin cache must refresh and
  the caller (`build_and_cache_remote_installed_plugin_marketplaces`) fires the
  change callback only when it did.
- `remote_catalog_metadata_eq` is exported to compare catalogs independently of
  display metadata and plugin display order while keeping marketplace order
  significant.

Go had no such distinction: `PluginService.ReplaceInstalledRemotePlugins`
replaced a marketplace's storage and the router always called
`effectivePluginsChanged`, so any pixel-level metadata change tore down live MCP
runtimes and cached skills.

### Landed

- `plugin/remote_metadata.go` (rewritten): `InstalledPluginMetadataEqual`
  compares exactly the Rust field set mapped onto `plugin.PluginSummary`
  (`RemotePluginID` carries the core remote plugin `id`; order stays
  significant). `RemoteCatalogMetadataEqual` mirrors the exported Rust catalog
  comparison over `[]PluginMarketplaceEntry`, sorting a copy of each
  marketplace's plugins by remote plugin id and ignoring display order.
- `PluginService.ReplaceInstalledRemotePlugins` now reports whether
  behavior-determining metadata changed. The published payload is still stored
  unmodified for display consumers, and the comparison reads back the *stored*
  (normalized) form on both sides - `addPluginLocked` returns that form - so the
  store's policy/trim/empty-slice normalization can never look like a behavioral
  change.
- `SortInstalledPluginDetailsForCompare` orders both snapshots by remote plugin
  id, the closest stable stand-in for Rust's positionally-compared published
  list (Go's store is a map).
- `appserver.reconcileInstalledRemotePlugins` clears the derived caches only
  when the aggregate comparison reports a change (it previously always did).

### Evidence

- `plugin.TestInstalledPluginMetadataBehavioralChangesStillInvalidateLikeRust`
  (thirteen cases, mirroring `remote_metadata_tests.rs`) and
  `...DisplayChangesDoNotInvalidateLikeRust` (renewed imagery, badges, display
  name, description, keywords, skills, hooks, apps, templates stay
  non-invalidating).
- `plugin.TestInstalledPluginMetadataOrderingRemainsSignificantLikeRust`,
  `...ReplaceInstalledRemotePluginsOnlyReportsBehavioralChangesLikeRust` (a
  display-only refresh reports no change *but still publishes the renewed
  payload*; a version change and full removal do report one), and
  `...RemoteCatalogMetadataReorderingAndBehaviorLikeRust` (display reordering
  and duplicate substitution / length / marketplace-name changes).
- `appserver.TestRemoteInstalledPluginDisplayRefreshPreservesDerivedCachesLikeRust`
  drives the real `reconcileInstalledRemotePlugins` against an installed-plugins
  HTTP fixture: a `display_name` refresh republishes the summary while the
  seeded skill cache and the MCP runtime epoch are untouched, and a `status`
  (availability) change clears them - the app-server reach of Rust's
  `mcp_resource/plugin_metadata_refresh.rs`.

### Recorded remainders

- Go has no `canonical_app_id` field on the plugin summary, so that one Rust
  field is absent from both sides and never causes a mismatch.
- Go's installed-plugins HTTP projection still does not parse `release.interface`
  (`logo_url`, `capabilities`), `authentication_policy`, `installation_policy`,
  or `release.version` into the summary, so those display/behavioral inputs
  cannot vary through the real fetch yet; the comparison handles them as soon as
  they are projected. Same for Rust's reconcile-only extra clear when
  `changed_plugins` is non-empty but metadata did not change
  (`handlePluginReconcile` reports changes but does not clear on them).

### Verification

- `go build ./...` green; `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file; `go vet`
  clean on `./plugin/` and `./appserver/`.
- `go test ./plugin/ ./appserver/ -count=1` green, and `go test ./... -count=1`
  -> 97 packages `ok` with no `FAIL`.

## sync71 addendum: upstream stayed current; PR-number sweep of the last 100 commits

`git fetch` on the Rust checkout shows `origin/main` still at `78245b47af`, so
there is no new upstream range to triage. To make sure nothing in the pinned
range was missed, this pass re-ran the sync62 PR-number sweep over the last 100
upstream commits and diffed the extracted numbers against this plan. Five
numbers were absent; each is dispositioned below.

| commit | PR | disposition |
|---|---|---|
| `7498521d28` | #46335 Keep MCP policy evaluation consistent with turn environments | **newly identified lane** (below) |
| `c775dd3c33` | #46310 Defer environment selection changes until the next turn | **partially present** (below) |
| `3e581ebca8` | #46297 Support catalog descriptions for all multi-agent V2 tools | **partially present** -> landed in sync72 |
| `ff73d63e64` | #46330 Move retry backoff into `codex-async-utils` | **N/A** |
| `1f631def3f` | #46322 Set the Windows sandbox type in the pending environment test | **N/A** |

- **#46297 partially present.** Go's `turn.MultiAgentToolOverridesFromCatalog`
  does resolve `model.ModelMessages.MultiAgentToolDescriptionOverride` for every
  entry in `agent.MultiAgentV2ToolNames`, and
  `agent.applyMultiAgentToolOverride` replaces the bundled text for the five
  non-spawn V2 tools. The `spawn_agent` half was missing: its override was
  carried but never applied, so the tool kept its bundled static text and
  silently dropped the catalog description. Landed in sync72.
- **#46330 N/A.** A Rust crate move for the exponential-backoff helper
  (`core/src/util.rs` -> `async-utils/src/backoff.rs`) with the delays and jitter
  preserved; Go's `model` retry backoff is untouched by it.
- **#46322 N/A.** A one-line Windows-sandbox test change.
- **#46310 partially present.** Its core observable - turn contexts and MCP
  workspace roots staying tied to the active environment snapshot while a later
  selection is saved for the next turn - is already pinned by
  `appserver.TestActiveTurnEnvironmentSnapshotRemainsStableAcrossLaterUpdatesLikeRust`
  over `registerActiveRuntimeTurn`/`activeTurnParams`, plus
  `persistTurnEnvironmentSelections` vs `inheritTurnEnvironmentSelections`. The
  remaining halves ("stop the running task before manual compaction adopts the
  saved environments", and callback routing to matching active vs future
  selections) still need a dedicated pass against the manual-compaction path.
- **#46335 partially present -> landed in sync73.** Go already evaluates MCP
  availability from the captured active-turn snapshot
  (`selectedEnvironmentIDs(r.activeTurnParams(threadID))`), so a selection saved
  for the next turn does not disturb the running turn. The missing half was the
  *refresh* decision: the MCP runtime was keyed by a fingerprint that ignored
  the captured selections, so a new turn with different environments reused the
  previous turn's availability. Landed in sync73.

### Verification

- `git -C D:\qax\reagent\dev\git\codex rev-parse HEAD origin/main` equal at
  `78245b47af` (behind count 0).
- `git show --stat` reviewed for each of the five commits above.

## sync72: catalog descriptions reach spawn_agent (#46297)

Flagged by the sync71 PR-number sweep; landed here.

### The divergence

Rust #46297 extended `model_messages.tools.multi_agent` description overrides
from `spawn_agent` to all six Multi-Agent V2 tools and moved the wrapper into
`core/src/tools/multi_agent_tool.rs`. In `spec_plan.rs` the five non-spawn tools
pass `multi_agent_tool_description_override(<name>)` to `multi_agent_v2_handler`
(which overwrites the bundled description), while `spawn_agent` passes
`/*description_override*/ None` to that wrapper because its own handler already
receives the override and composes it with runtime guidance in
`create_spawn_agent_tool_v2` -> `spawn_agent_tool_description_v2`.

Go mirrored the wrapper half (`applyMultiAgentToolOverride` replaced the text for
every kind except spawn) but never applied spawn's override anywhere: the
`spawn_agent` Spec hard-coded its static text and the override was dropped.
Rust's expanded integration test (`multi_agent_tool_descriptions.rs`) pins the
opposite - the spawn description must contain the catalog text, must not contain
"Spawns an agent to work on the specified task.", and must still end with the
configured usage hint.

### Landed

- `agent.multiAgentV2ToolExecutor.Spec` (`agent/tools_v2.go`) now applies
  `toolOverrides["spawn_agent"].Description` to the spawn description before the
  usage hint is appended: a present override (including an empty string, which
  suppresses the static text) replaces the bundled text, a missing/null override
  keeps it, and the runtime usage hint is appended either way. The Code Mode
  declaration is still appended afterwards by `codemode`, so it stays the suffix.
- The wrapper comment now records that the `spawn_agent` description is applied
  by the executor, matching Rust's `description_override: None` on the wrapper.

### Evidence

- `agent.TestMultiAgentV2SpawnReplacesStaticDescriptionLikeRust` (the override
  replaces the bundled text, the usage hint still trails the description, and
  the parameter override still applies) and
  `agent.TestMultiAgentV2SpawnEmptyDescriptionSuppressesStaticTextLikeRust` (an
  explicit empty description suppresses the static text without disabling the
  tool). The previous `TestMultiAgentV2SpawnKeepsComposedDescriptionLikeRust`
  encoded the wrong expectation and was replaced.
- `turn.TestBuildToolRegistryAppliesSpawnCatalogDescriptionLikeRust` drives the
  real `BuildToolRegistry` wiring (`AgentToolOverrides` -> spawn Spec) so the
  end-to-end path is covered, not just the executor.
- `agent.TestMultiAgentV2CatalogOverridesApplyLikeRust` (non-spawn descriptions)
  and `turn.TestMultiAgentToolOverridesFromCatalogMatchesRust` (the resolver)
  are unchanged and still pass.

### Verification

- `go build ./...` green; `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file.
- `go test ./agent/ ./turn/ ./exec/ ./model/ -count=1` green, and
  `go test ./... -count=1` -> 97 packages `ok` with no `FAIL`.

## sync73: refresh the MCP runtime when the captured turn environments change (#46335)

Flagged by the sync71 PR-number sweep; landed here.

### The divergence

Rust #46335 keeps MCP policy evaluation and runtime publication on *one* captured
`TurnEnvironmentSnapshot` and makes the publication gate compare **both** the
captured selections (`all_selections()`, including selections that are still
starting or have failed) and the ready environment handles
(`ready_environment_handles()`) before reusing a runtime. The user-visible bug it
fixes: environment settings saved for the next turn must not change MCP tool
availability during the active turn, and a selection change must actually take
effect on the next turn.

Go already derives attachment-scoped server availability from the captured
active-turn snapshot (`selectedEnvironmentIDs(r.activeTurnParams(threadID))` ->
`RuntimeConfig.AvailableEnvironment`), so the "running turn is stable" half was
present. The refresh half was not: `mcpRuntimeCoordinator.serviceForThread` keyed
each thread's runtime on `mcpRuntimeConfigFingerprint(cfg)`, which reads only a
fixed set of `cfg.Values` keys (`mcp_servers`, `apps`, `features`, ...). The
captured environment selections are thread state rather than config values, so
the fingerprint never changed when the next turn selected a different
environment and the thread kept the previous turn's `AvailableEnvironment` -
deselected attachment servers stayed enabled and newly selected ones stayed
disabled.

### Landed

- `RuntimeRouter.managedMCPServiceForThread` captures the turn-environment
  snapshot once (`availableEnvironment := selectedEnvironmentIDs(r.activeTurnParams(threadID))`)
  and uses it for both the refresh decision and the applied
  `RuntimeConfig.AvailableEnvironment`, so the two can no longer disagree.
- `mcpRuntimeCoordinator.serviceForThread` takes an `environmentFingerprint` and
  folds it into the binding fingerprint:
  `mcpRuntimeConfigFingerprint(cfg) + "|environments=" + environmentFingerprint`.
  A selection change now routes through the coordinator's existing
  `updateService` path, which re-applies the runtime config (rebuilding server
  availability from `AvailableEnvironment` while preserving matching client
  caches). `mcpEnvironmentRuntimeFingerprint` renders the ids order-sensitively,
  matching Rust's slice comparison of the captured selections.

### Evidence

- `appserver.TestMCPRuntimeTracksActiveTurnEnvironmentSelectionsLikeRust` drives
  the real path: a first turn selecting `env-a` exposes only `env-a`'s
  attachment server; mutating the caller's registered params (a selection saved
  mid-turn) leaves the running turn's availability unchanged; after the turn is
  consumed, a second turn selecting `env-b` must expose `env-b`'s server and
  disable `env-a`'s, with the local server available throughout. The test fails
  before this change (`newly selected environment server should be available on
  the next turn`) and passes after.
- The existing coordinator tests (auth-revision replacement, config-change
  refresh, lifecycle invalidation, concurrency) were updated for the new
  parameter and still pass.

### Recorded remainder (with Rust source)

Rust's `McpEnvironmentScope::authority_for` has four outcomes - `Unrestricted`,
`SelectedPluginsOnly`, `Unavailable`, and `Restricted(policy)` - and derives
`Unavailable` for a selected environment whose (owner-supplied) configuration is
still `Pending` or has `Failed`, and `Restricted` from a ready environment's
`mcp_policy`. Go models this as binary membership in `AvailableEnvironment`
(`mcpServerEnvironmentAvailable`). **Closed in sync75** - see the section below;
the remaining nuance is the disabled-vs-omitted projection.

- a selected environment that is owner-pending or owner-failed is still treated
  as available. Expressing `Unavailable` needs a "a selection snapshot exists"
  flag so that an all-unavailable snapshot is not mistaken for "no selections"
  (the current `len(available) == 0 -> no filtering` branch). The app-server
  boundary resolves every request selection to `FromThread`
  (`DefaultEnvironmentConfigState`), so this path is only reachable through
  internal owner callbacks today.
- `Restricted(mcp_policy)` has no Go counterpart at all: `EnvironmentConfig` has
  no `McpPolicy` field and no environment-level `mcp_policy` is parsed, so an
  environment's managed server/plugin restrictions are not enforced. That is the
  unfinished half of #39335, which Go otherwise mirrors through
  `AvailableEnvironment` plus the thread-level `managedconfig.ConfigRequirements`.

### Verification

- `go build ./...` green; `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file; `go vet` clean
  on `./appserver/` and `./mcp/`.
- `go test ./appserver/ ./mcp/ -count=1` green, and `go test ./... -count=1` ->
  97 packages `ok` with no `FAIL`.

## sync74: reject path syntax in project-doc fallback filenames (#45865)

Newly identified by the wide PR-number sweep (see below); landed here.

### The divergence

Rust #45865 validates every `project_doc_fallback_filenames` entry before any
filesystem probe: probing a Windows network path can send ambient credentials
even during a metadata check. `candidate_filenames` now ignores entries that are
`.`, `..`, or contain `/` or NUL on any executor, and additionally ignores
entries containing `\` or `:` when the *executor's* path convention (inferred
from the cwd's `PathUri`) is Windows; a warning is logged and the remaining
entries are still considered.

Go's `prompt.InstructionsCandidateFilenames` appended every non-empty,
non-duplicate entry unfiltered, so `.`/`..`, `nested/CLAUDE.md`,
`..\AGENTS.md`, `C:\AGENTS.md`, `\\server\share\AGENTS.md`, and
`AGENTS.md:stream` all reached `os.Stat`/`os.ReadFile`.

### Landed

- `prompt.InstructionsCandidateFilenames(fallbackNames, convention)` now applies
  Rust's rule verbatim, warning through `slog` and continuing with the valid
  entries.
- `prompt.instructionsPathConvention(cwd)` mirrors
  `cwd.infer_path_convention()`: a `file://` path URI carries its own convention
  (a `file:///C:/...` cwd reports Windows even from a POSIX host), a
  Windows-native path (drive-letter or UNC) is recognized by shape, and anything
  else uses the host convention. `InstructionsAgentsMDPaths` infers it from the
  cwd it already receives.

### Evidence

- `prompt.TestInstructionsCandidateFilenamesRejectPathSyntaxLikeRust` ports
  Rust's `fallback_paths_are_rejected_before_filesystem_probes` list: under
  POSIX `.`, `..`, `/`, `\0` are rejected while backslashes and colons remain
  ordinary filename characters; under Windows the nine Windows path shapes are
  rejected too; `WORKFLOW.md` dedupes and `.instructions.md` survives.
- `prompt.TestInstructionsPathConventionInfersExecutorConventionLikeRust` pins
  `file:///repo` -> POSIX, `file:///C:/repo` -> Windows, and the Windows-native
  shapes, independently of the host OS.
- `appserver.TestProjectDocFallbackFilenamesRejectPathSyntaxLikeRust` drives the
  real discovery boundary: `sub/CLAUDE.md`, `..`, `.`, and `nested/CLAUDE.md`
  never load the nested document, while a valid `CLAUDE.md` beside them still
  loads.

### Wide sweep result (for the record)

Re-running the sync62 PR-number sweep across the whole pinned range
(`5b1d656018..78245b47af`, 319 commits) leaves 102 unmentioned numbers. Most are
the theme-grouped families the earlier triage already dispositions by theme
(Guardian crate extraction #45413-#45693, the Windows sandbox service
#45455-#46241, analytics reports #45739-#45762/#46058, TUI polish, SDK/Bazel
CI). The Go-visible items found this pass were #46335 (landed in sync73),
#46297 (landed in sync72), and #45865 (this section). Tracked remainders:
#46264 (the v2 `spawn_agent` bundled description is a Go one-liner rather than
Rust's long text, so its removed "concrete, bounded subtask" line has no Go
counterpart to remove) and #46123 (the model-catalog spawn description override
half is covered by sync72; the parameter half by #46505).

### Verification

- `go build ./...` green; `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file; `go vet` clean
  on `./prompt/` and `./appserver/`.
- `go test ./prompt/ ./appserver/ -count=1` green, and `go test ./... -count=1`
  -> 97 packages `ok` with no `FAIL`.

## sync75: owner-supplied environment MCP policies (#39335)

Closes the remainder recorded in sync73. The owner half of #39335 was never
modelled: Go only tracked which environments were selected, so an owner-supplied
`mcp_policy` was silently ignored.

### The divergence

Rust's `McpEnvironmentScope::authority_for` has four outcomes and
`McpCatalogBuilder::build_with_environment_authority` filters every registration
by the authority of its environment:

- `Unrestricted`: thread-supplied configuration, or a ready owner config without
  a policy.
- `Restricted(policy)`: a ready owner config with `mcp_policy`. Configured,
  compatibility, and extension servers are admitted when the policy declares no
  configured-server allowlist or when their identity matches the entry for their
  name; plugin registrations are denied outright by an explicitly empty
  configured allowlist, and otherwise follow the per-plugin allowlist when one
  exists.
- `Unavailable`: a selected environment whose owner configuration is still
  pending or has failed.
- `SelectedPluginsOnly`: an unselected non-default environment, where only
  explicitly selected plugin servers survive.

The controller-owned (compatibility) Codex Apps registration is exempt. Go
approximated all of this with binary membership in `AvailableEnvironment` and
never parsed `mcp_policy`, so an owner restriction was a silent no-op.

### Landed

- `config.EnvironmentMCPPolicy` (mirroring `protocol::EnvironmentMcpPolicy`) with
  `EnvironmentMCPPolicyFromMap` (reusing the managed-requirement matchers, with
  nil vs explicitly-empty `servers` preserved) and `ToMap` for storage
  round-trips.
- `appserver.EnvironmentConfig.McpPolicy`: parsed from `mcp_policy`/`mcpPolicy`,
  cloned by `cloneEnvironmentConfig`, and serialized by `environmentConfigToAny`
  so `validateEnvironmentSelectionConfig` keeps the policy in the normalized
  selection that later turns re-read.
- `mcp.EnvironmentAuthority` (`Scoped`/`Unlimited`/`Restricted`/`Unavailable`)
  plus `AllowsRegistration` and `environmentPolicyAllowsRegistration`, mirroring
  the Rust filter and its host-owned-Apps exemption. It replaces
  `mcpServerEnvironmentAvailable` at the three call sites (catalog build,
  `SetServerPermissionProfiles`, `ForThreadlessOperations`) while keeping that
  helper as the fallback for value-only configs that carry no captured snapshot.
- `appserver.mcpEnvironmentAuthorityForTurn` builds the authority from the
  captured turn selections (thread-origin selections unrestricted; owner ready
