  plus `run.duration_ms`, and the `failed` duration with a run failure label);
  scan tolerance and per-file metrics (`check_root`/`read_directory`/
  `read_metadata` scan failures, `scanned`, `skipped_unreadable_meta`, the
  per-outcome counter/duration and the byte/ratio histograms); `time_budget`
  reporting; and the stale temp sweep with its `removed`/failure metrics.
- `rollout/rollout.go`: `MaterializeRolloutForReference` now records the
  `plain_exists` / `missing` / `decompressed` outcomes with their durations and
  the staged failure labels (`read_metadata`, `prepare_directory`, `create_temp`,
  `open_source`, `decode_and_write`, `sync`, `flush`, `set_metadata`, `publish`,
  `remove_source`).

### Evidence

- `rollout.TestRolloutCompressionWorkerMetricsLikeRust` drives a real pass over a
  cold rollout and asserts the `started`/`completed` run counters with
  `completion_reason=scan_finished` and the three boolean tags, the run duration,
  the `scanned`/`compressed` file counters, and the source/compressed/ratio
  histograms plus the file duration; a second pass reports
  `skipped_already_running`.
- `rollout.TestRolloutCompressionTempCleanupMetricsLikeRust`: a 7-hour-old
  `rollout-compress-*.tmp` is removed and counted, a fresh temp survives, and the
  completed run reports `cleanup_errors=false`.
- `rollout.TestMaterializeRolloutReferenceMetricsLikeRust`: `decompressed` with
  its duration, then `plain_exists`, then `missing` for a path with neither
  representation.
- `rollout.TestRolloutCompressionErrorKindLikeRust` pins the fifteen error
  categories and the failure-counter tag shape (including that the
  materialize/run families carry no trigger tag).
- `metrics.TestGlobalCounterAndHistogramLikeRust` covers the new global helpers.
- `model/models_endpoint_metrics_test.go`'s fake recorder gained the two methods
  the widened interface requires.
- `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file; `go vet ./rollout/ ./metrics/` clean;
  `go test ./rollout/ ./metrics/ ./telemetry/ ./model/ -count=1` green;
  `go test ./... -count=1` green (97 packages, no `FAIL`).

### Recorded remainders for this lane

- Rust's `open_rollout_line_reader` (not-found retries across a representation
  transition plus the per-reader `codex.rollout_compression.read` counter and
  `read.io_duration_ms` duration with `format`/`outcome`(eof/partial/failed)/
  `stage`/`error_kind`) has no Go counterpart: Go's `Load` opens plain or zstd
  files directly, with no retry and no reader metrics.
- Go records a single `compress` stage for per-file failures rather than Rust's
  fine-grained `read_metadata`/`create_temp`/`encode`/`verify`/`recheck`/
  `set_metadata`/`publish`/`remove_source` stages, and the scan reports
  `read_directory` at the root level only (Go's walker surfaces a nested
  directory or file-type error as one failure rather than Rust's per-entry
  `read_file_type`). Rust's `skipped_no_runtime` status is N/A: Go's spawn has no
  tokio runtime to miss.

## sync94: the retrying rollout line reader and its read metrics (#45461 remainder)

The remainder sync93 recorded: Rust's `open_rollout_line_reader` and the
`codex.rollout_compression.read` observations had no Go counterpart.

### The divergence

Rust's reader:

- resolves the rollout's current representation (`existing_rollout_path`, plain
  preferred) and retries a not-found resolution `MAX_NOT_FOUND_RETRIES` (3) times
  with a 50 ms delay, so callers do not need to know which representation is on
  disk during a transition;
- accumulates the open/read time and reports one `codex.rollout_compression.read`
  counter plus a `read.io_duration_ms` duration per reader on drop, tagged
  `format` (plain/zstd/unknown), `outcome` (eof/partial/failed), `stage` and the
  bounded `error_kind`.

Go's `Load` opened the given path directly, with no representation resolution, no
retry, and no metrics.

### Landed

- `rollout/line_reader.go`: `OpenRolloutLineReader` (resolution + the retry
  budget + a failed-open metric carrying `format=unknown`, `stage=open` and the
  error kind), `RolloutLineReader.ReadLine` (tracks EOF and the first read
  failure, accumulating io time), and `Close` (releases the file/decoder and
  records the read metric once, mapping to eof/partial/failed exactly like Rust's
  `Drop for ReadMetrics`).
- `rollout.Load` reads through the reader, so every caller gains Rust's
  representation resolution, transition retry and read metric.

### The regression this exposed, and its fix

Wiring the retrying reader into `Load` first broke the appserver suite (five
failures and a 517 s package run versus ~130 s): Go used `Load` as an
*existence probe* in opportunistic paths, so every missing rollout paid the
3 × 50 ms retry budget. In the cold-resume path that delay surfaced a pre-existing
race - the record's stored `rollout_path` bakes in the timestamp of the
`thread/start` call, while the recorder derives the file name later - and
`TestRuntimeRouterThreadSettingsApprovalPolicySurvivesColdResume` failed two to
three times in five.

Rust never probes with a reader: it checks existence first (`try_exists`,
`existing_rollout_path`) and only then opens one. The probe-style Go call sites
now do the same with `rollout.ExistingRolloutPath`:
`latestPersistedApprovalPolicy`, `latestPersistedOwnedThreadCWD`,
`latestPersistedCollaborationMode`, `attachRolloutTurnSnapshots` and
`feedbackTurnMetadataFromRollout`. With those guards the flaky test is 0/8 and the
appserver package runs in 77 s.

### Evidence

- `rollout.TestOpenRolloutLineReaderRecordsReadMetricsLikeRust`: a complete plain
  read reports `plain/eof/none/none`, a reader released before EOF reports
  `partial`, the compressed representation reports `zstd`, a missing rollout
  reports `unknown/failed/open/not_found`, and each observation also carries the
  io duration with the same tags.
- `rollout.TestOpenRolloutLineReaderRetriesRepresentationTransitionLikeRust`: a
  rollout that appears ~10 ms after the open starts is still read (the retry
  works), and a path that never appears fails after at least one retry delay
  rather than blocking.
- `rollout.TestLoadReadsCompressedSiblingLikeRust`: `Load` on the plain path of a
  compressed-only rollout succeeds, with `ExistingRolloutPath` resolved to the
  `.zst` sibling.
- `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file; `go vet ./rollout/ ./appserver/` clean;
  `go test ./rollout/ -count=1` green; `go test ./appserver/ -count=1` green in
  77 s; `go test ./... -count=1` green (97 packages, no `FAIL`).

### Recorded lane: the stored rollout path can disagree with the created file

The race the guards sidestep is real and remains: `thread/start` stores
`extra["rollout_path"]` from `rollout.PathForThread(codexHome, threadID, now)`,
while `Router.newThreadRolloutRecorder` builds `rollout.CreateParams` without that
path and the recorder derives its own file name, so a call that straddles a second
boundary writes a different name than the record points at (and
`unmaterializedThread`, which stats the stored path, then reports "no rollout
found for thread id ..." even though the rollout exists). Rust computes the path
once and records it. Aligning this means threading the stored path (or a single
`now`) into `rollout.NewRecorder`; it is its own pass.

## sync95: the recorder honors the record's stored rollout path

Closes the lane sync94 recorded above ("the stored rollout path can disagree with
the created file").

### The divergence

Rust creates a rollout through `RolloutRecorderParams::Create` and the created
recorder's own `rollout_path` is the path the thread stores, so the record and the
file can never disagree; `precompute_new_rollout_path` renders the filename once
from the conversation id (and, for `thread/revert`, the `rollout_id_override`
suffix) and the writer opens exactly that path. Go instead precomputed the path
at `thread/start` (`extra["rollout_path"] = PathForThread(codexHome, threadID,
now)`) while `Router.newThreadRolloutRecorder` handed `rollout.CreateParams` no
path at all, so the recorder re-derived its own name from whatever `now` the
materialization call passed. When those two timestamps fall in different seconds
the file lands next to the record's path, and `unmaterializedThread` - which
stats the stored path - keeps reporting "no rollout found for thread id ..." for
a rollout that exists.

### Landed

- `rollout.CreateParams.Path` pins the created file: `NewRecorder` uses it
  verbatim when set and only falls back to `PathForThread` when it is empty.
- `Router.newThreadRolloutRecorder` reads `record.Metadata.Extra["rollout_path"]`
  and passes it as the pinned path, but only when the basename embeds this
  record's own thread id (`rollout.ThreadIDFromFilename`). The guard matters:
  `session.forkMetadata` clones the source record's `Extra`, so a fork inherits
  the source's `rollout_path`; without the id check the fork's recorder would
  overwrite the source rollout instead of deriving a name for its own id.

### Evidence

- `rollout.TestNewRecorderHonorsPinnedPathLikeRust`: a recorder given a pinned
  path writes exactly there even when its `Now` would derive a different name,
  the derived name is not created, and a recorder without a pinned path still
  derives the standard timestamped name.
- `appserver.TestRuntimeRouterThreadRolloutRecorderHonorsStoredPathLikeRust`:
  after `thread/start` records its rollout path, a `createThreadRollout` call
  carrying a timestamp five seconds later still writes at the recorded path, and
  a cold router then resumes the thread without the "no rollout found" error. The
  test fails before the change (`rollout was not written at the recorded path`).
- `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file; `go vet ./appserver/ ./rollout/` clean;
  `go test ./rollout/ ./appserver/ ./parity/ -count=1` green; `go test ./...
  -count=1` green (97 packages, no `FAIL`).

## sync96: the local stdio MCP server is contained as a process tree (#10710 / #46660)

### Upstream check (second pull of 2026-09-19)

`git pull origin main` in `D:\qax\reagent\dev\git\codex` fast-forwarded
`78245b47af..595cc91e8c` (3 commits):

| commit | PR | disposition |
|---|---|---|
| `d086e2752d` | #46659 Move local child-process spawning into `codex-utils-pty` | **N/A**: pure crate move (`rmcp-client/src/local_child.rs` and `macos_stdio*.rs` move into `utils/pty`) |
| `3801fc8dea` | #46660 Make local child process launch settings explicit | **landed here** (the Go-visible behaviour it preserves was missing) |
| `595cc91e8c` | #46661 Avoid fork when spawning macOS filesystem helpers | **N/A**: macOS-only spawn internals (`posix_spawn` + `POSIX_SPAWN_CLOEXEC_DEFAULT`, socket-backed stdin, `wait_with_output`). Go's fs helper spawns through `os/exec` with `fsHelperEnvironment()` and Go's own close-on-exec descriptors, and Go has no socket-fd `sandboxed_file_open` helper to convert. |

`#46660` itself is a Rust-internal refactor (a constrained `codex_utils_pty::Command`
shared by Tokio and the native macOS backend); its commit message states it is
"retaining its new process group and Windows suspended-spawn support" for local
MCP servers. That retained behaviour was never ported to Go.

### The divergence

Rust launches every local stdio MCP server with `ProcessMode::NewGroup`
(`setpgid(0, 0)` in the child) and, on Windows, into a Job Object created before
the suspended child runs (`JobObject::create_without_breakaway` +
`prepare_suspended_spawn` + `assign_and_resume_process`). Teardown is
tree-shaped: SIGTERM to the process group, then SIGKILL after
`PROCESS_GROUP_TERM_GRACE_PERIOD` (2 s) on Unix, and `TerminateJobObject` on
Windows. `82c981cafc` (#10710) introduced this precisely to prevent "orphan
process storms".

Go did neither: `newMCPStdioCommand` set no process group and
`stdioClient.closeLocked` / `failTransportFor` called `cmd.Process.Kill()`, which
terminates only the direct child. A server launched through a shim (the common
`npx <server>` -> `node` shape) therefore left the real server running after the
transport was gone.

### Landed

- `mcp/stdio_process.go` holds the shared contract and the no-containment
  fallback (kill the direct child, Rust's `LocalProcessTerminator::Process`
  arm); `mcp/stdio_process_unix.go`, `mcp/stdio_process_windows.go` and
  `mcp/stdio_process_other.go` implement `startMCPStdioProcess` plus the
  idempotent `terminate`/`release` pair per platform.
- Unix: the child becomes a process-group leader, teardown sends SIGTERM to the
  group and escalates to SIGKILL after the same 2 s grace, and skips the
  escalation when the group is already gone (matching Rust's `Ok(exists)` rule).
- Windows: the child is spawned `CREATE_SUSPENDED`, assigned to a
  `KILL_ON_JOB_CLOSE` job object, and resumed through `NtResumeProcess`; job
  creation/assignment failure degrades to the uncontained fallback exactly like
  Rust's warning arms, and closing the job handle on release finishes any
  survivor.
- `stdioClient` gained a `process` field; the start site uses
  `startMCPStdioProcess`, and both teardown sites (`closeLocked` and
  `failTransportFor`) terminate and release the containment instead of killing
  the direct child.

### Evidence

- `mcp.TestStdioProcessTerminationReachesTheWholeTree` re-executes the test
  binary as the direct child, which spawns a grandchild and records its pid; the
  test asserts the grandchild is alive, terminates the transport, and asserts the
  grandchild is gone within 10 s. Verified to be meaningful: forcing the
  uncontained path makes it fail (`grandchild ... survived teardown; the server
  tree was not contained`) and the grandchild is still visible to `tasklist`.
- `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file; `go vet ./mcp/` clean;
  `go test ./mcp/ -count=1` green (8.9 s); `go test ./appserver/ -count=1` green
  (73.9 s) and the full `go test ./... -count=1` had exactly one failure,
  `TestRuntimeAgentControllerSpawnUsesCapturedStepSettingsLikeRust`'s known
  load-sensitive `TempDir RemoveAll cleanup: ... The directory is not empty`
  flake (already recorded in sync44/sync92), which passed 3/3 in isolation and on
  the package re-run.

### Recorded nuances

- Rust's macOS `terminate_process_group`/`kill_process_group` retry a denied
  group signal against individual members through `proc_listpgrppids`. Go has no
  cgo-free binding for that, so the port keeps the group SIGTERM/SIGKILL contract
  without the per-member retry; on Linux (the orphan-storm case from #10710) the
  group signal is the whole mechanism.
- Rust terminates the contained tree from the process handle's `Drop`; Go
  terminates at its two explicit teardown sites (close and transport failure),
  which are the only paths that release a stdio transport.
- The failure paths still surface `waitMCPStdioCommand`'s pre-existing one-second
  bound; a server that ignores SIGTERM relies on the 2 s SIGKILL (Unix) or the
  job handle close (Windows) to finish, exactly as Rust escalates asynchronously.

## sync97: the permission selection no longer clobbers the `[permissions]` table (#45981)

The sync88 sweep listed #45981 ("Preserve session config when switching thread
permission profiles") under app-server permission routing. Reproducing Rust's
`thread_settings_update_preserves_session_profiles` found the same behaviour
already carried by Go, plus a latent Go-only bug in how the selection is written
back into the config.

### The divergence

Rust applies the requested profile through
`ConfigOverrides::default_permissions` and, after #45981,
`ConfigManager::load_permission_config_for_thread` merges the thread's enabled
`SessionFlags` layers before resolving, so a profile defined at thread start
stays available when `thread/settings/update` switches away and back.

Go instead wrote the selected profile id into the **`permissions` key** - the
config's named-profile *table* - at three sites:

| site | before |
|---|---|
| `RuntimeRouter` thread-start config snapshot | `sessionDefaults["permissions"] = *params.Permissions` |
| `applyThreadLifecycleMCPConfig` (start/resume config overrides) | `overrides["permissions"] = *permissions` |
| `persistThreadResumeConfigSnapshot` | `overrides["permissions"] = *params.Permissions` |

`config.permissionProfilesFromConfig` reads `values["permissions"]` as a table
and `values["default_permissions"]` as the selection, so overwriting the table
with a scalar both hid every session-defined profile and shadowed the disk
`[permissions]` table for the merged turn config. The concrete failure is
`default_permissions requires a '[permissions]' table` (or a silent fall back to
the default profile) on the next turn.

### Landed

- The three sites now write `default_permissions`, Rust's `ConfigOverrides`
  key, leaving the `[permissions]` table intact.

### Evidence

- `appserver.TestRuntimeRouterThreadSettingsUpdatePreservesSessionProfilesLikeRust`
  mirrors Rust's three cases (session-only profile, disk + session profile, and a
  top-level `permissions` selection): it switches `:workspace` then back to
  `audit`, asserts every `thread/settings/updated` notification reports the
  requested profile, and then resolves `audit` through the next turn's effective
  config (record session config merged). The third case failed before the change
  with `default_permissions requires a '[permissions]' table`, which is how the
  latent bug was found.
- `gofmt -l` clean; `go test ./appserver/ -count=1` green (74.2 s);
  `go test ./... -count=1` green (97 packages, no `FAIL`).

## sync98: the Guardian review reports the turn's latest response id (#45441)

Re-checking the sync88 sweep's remaining candidates at `595cc91e8c`: #46323
(active plugin inventory in turn analytics) and #45806 (plugin install restricted
to the root thread) turned out to be **already landed** in Go
(`RuntimeRouter.turnAnalyticsPluginInventory`/`activePluginTelemetryIDs` with
`appserver/turn_analytics_plugins_test.go`, and
`tool/plugin_install.go`'s `nonRootAgent` rejection). `#45441` was not.

### The divergence

Rust keeps the latest response id received in a turn in the turn context's
extension data and reads it as `GuardianReviewContext::parent_response_id`: the
guardian review request then carries it as the `parent_response_id` client
metadata field, so the reviewer is attributed to the response that was in flight
when review was requested. #45441 removed the pre-retry clearing of that value -
"Clearing the response ID before that response emits `response.created` leaves
those reviews without a `parent_response_id`" - and pinned three properties: the
id survives a sampling retry that never reaches `response.created`, a later
`response.created` replaces it, and a fresh turn does not inherit it.

Go never recorded a turn-scoped response id at all: `responsesStreamNotificationState.activeResponseID`
lives inside one sampling attempt's notification state (created per attempt), and
`modelGuardianReviewer.reviewClientMetadata` built the review metadata without any
`parent_response_id` key, so a review issued while the next response was in
flight had no attribution.

### Landed

- `activeRuntimeTurn` gained `LatestResponseID`, and the stream handler records
  it on `response.created` (`RuntimeRouter.recordRuntimeTurnResponseID`). The value
  is turn-scoped, so it outlives a sampling retry (each attempt builds a fresh
  notification state) and a new turn starts without it.
- `RuntimeRouter.guardianLatestResponseIDForTurn` resolves it for the reviewer,
  wired in `ensureGuardianReviewerWithPrewarm` beside the other per-turn
  resolvers.
- `modelGuardianReviewer.reviewClientMetadata` adds `parent_response_id` when the
  resolved id is non-empty, exactly like Rust's `set_guardian_metadata` arm.

### Evidence

- `appserver.TestRuntimeTurnResponseIDKeepsLatestAcrossRetriesLikeRust`: a turn
  without `response.created` reports nothing, the first `response.created`
  records `resp-attempt-1`, a retry that never reaches `response.created` keeps
  it, a second `response.created` replaces it with `resp-attempt-2`, and a fresh
  turn on the same thread (after `ConsumeTurn`) reports nothing.
- `appserver.TestGuardianReviewCarriesTurnParentResponseIDLikeRust`: through the
  router-wired reviewer, a review while `resp-in-flight` is recorded carries
  `parent_response_id`, and a review before any `response.created` omits the key.
- Both tests were verified to fail with the recording removed
  (`parent response id = "", want "resp-attempt-1"` and
  `parent_response_id present = false, want true`).
- `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file; `go vet ./appserver/` clean;
  `go test ./appserver/ -count=1` green (74.2 s); `go test ./... -count=1` green
  (97 packages, no `FAIL`).

### Recorded remainders in this lane

- Rust's reviewer requests also carry the `x-codex-guardian: reviewer` header
  (set when the reviewer session is created) and use it to drop the request's
  service tier and to suppress the routing-hint header. Go's reviewer already
  sends no service tier (the review request never sets one) and marks the request
  with `x-openai-subagent: guardian` and `turn_trigger=guardian_review`, but it
  has no `x-codex-guardian` header, so a backend that keys reviewer behavior off
  that header only sees Go's subagent marker. That header belongs with the
  reviewer request-assembly surface, which is a separate lane.

## sync99: a Guardian reviewer request never carries a routing hint (#45441 surroundings)

Closing the routing-hint half of the remainder sync98 recorded.

### The divergence

Rust wraps the routing-hint insertion in client.rs with a `!guardian_reviewer`
guard, so a Guardian approval-review request omits `x-codex-routing-hint` even
when it is sent on the standard `/responses` route (the dedicated `/guardian`
route is only chosen when every eligibility condition holds).

Go suppressed the hint only for the dedicated endpoint
(`if endpoint == ResponsesEndpointResponses`). Because
`ResponsesAgentRunner.responsesEndpoint` requires `FreeGuardianEnabled`,
codex-backend auth, supported routes, and `model == approvalReviewPreferredModel`
before it picks `/guardian`, a review with free_guardian disabled or a different
review model fell back to `/responses` and carried `x-codex-routing-hint`, where
Rust would have omitted it.

### Landed

- The routing-hint arm now requires `!isGuardianReviewRequest(request)` as well as
  the standard route. `isGuardianReviewRequest` is the runner's existing
  reviewer predicate (task kind `review` or originator `guardian`), the same one
  the endpoint selection already uses.

### Evidence

- `model.TestResponsesAgentRunnerGuardianEndpointRouting` gained the
  standard-route case: with `free_guardian` disabled a review request stays on
  `/responses` and carries no routing hint, while an ordinary request on the same
  runner still carries `model=gpt-test`. Verified to fail with the guard removed
  (`standard-route guardian routing hint = "model=gpt-test", want empty`).
- `gofmt -l` clean; `go test ./model/ -count=1` green (17.2 s);
  `go test ./... -count=1` green (97 packages, no `FAIL`).

### Remaining recorded gap in this lane

The `x-codex-guardian: reviewer` header itself. Go identifies reviewer traffic by
task kind/originator and routes it to the dedicated guardian endpoint, so it has
no header plumbing; the header's other Rust-side effects (service tier cleared,
routing hints suppressed) are already satisfied in Go, which is why the
behavioural half is landable without it. Sending the header would require a
request-level extra-header channel in the model package, which is a separate
change.

## Site checks: the rest of the sync88 sweep at `595cc91e8c`

Re-verified the sweep candidates that were still listed as queued. Two were
already landed, and two are blocked by Go-side prerequisites rather than by
missing analysis.

### Already landed (stale plan entries, no change needed)

- **#46323 (active plugin inventory in turn analytics)**: Go already composes it
  in `RuntimeRouter.turnAnalyticsPluginInventory` / `activePluginIDsForTurnAnalytics`
  / `activePluginTelemetryIDs` (remote-id preference, `plugin.IsValidRemotePluginID`,
  the 128-byte id bound, the 512-id cap, sort + dedupe, `nil` for unknown and `[]`
  for observed-empty) and pins it with `appserver/turn_analytics_plugins_test.go`
  and the `telemetry` turn-event shape.
- **#45806 (plugin install restricted to the root thread)**: landed as
  `bfed480a`, with `tool.PluginInstallSuggestionOptions.NonRootAgent` and the
  handler rejecting before argument parsing
  (`request_plugin_install can only be used by the root thread`).

### Blocked by a Go-side prerequisite

- **#46179 (sender user messages in Guardian delegation reviews)**: the producer
  is missing at every layer Go would need. Rust gates it on
  `GuardianContextMode::ThreadOwned` and stores a `SenderUserMessages` snapshot in
  the history's retained context; Go has **no `GuardianContextMode` at all**
  (`git grep` finds no equivalent), no retained-context/`CodexHarnessMetadata`
  envelope model, and its Guardian root evidence is the on-demand best-effort
  read in `RuntimeRouter.runtimeRootUserEvidence`. Go does have the pieces the
  render needs to sit next to (`context.ParseDelegatedToolOutput`, the
  `>>> ... START/END` section writer in `state.writeGuardianPromptSection`, the
  root-authorization evidence section), but the capture, the per-delivery
  retention across replay/compaction/rollback, and the fork stripping are all
  defined in terms of the missing context-mode/retained-context model. Porting it
  means introducing that model first, which is its own lane.
- **#45811 (bounded WSL terminal detection)**: the decision function exists as a
  parity stub (`tui/tui.KeyboardEnhancementDisabledFor` /
  `VscodeTerminalDetected`) but nothing consumes it, because Go's TUI never
  enables keyboard enhancements: `ResetKeyboardEnhancementFlagsANSI`,
  `EnableModifyOtherKeysANSI` and `DisableModifyOtherKeysANSI` have no production
  callers, and the startup probe helpers
  (`UpdateStartupProbe`/`StartupProbeComplete`/`FinishStartupProbe`,
  `StartupKeyboardEnhancementProbeSkip`) have no producer. The WSL probe bounding
  and the three-way VS Code/other/unknown decision only matter once Go pushes the
  enhancement flags, so this belongs with a keyboard-enhancement enable-path lane
  (Go's composer is `bubbles/textarea`, which handles paste/key events
  differently).

## sync100: previous-model inline compaction and its current-model fallback (#46324)

The compaction lane the sweep recorded as "queued (fallback selection)". Rust
compacts before sampling with the model the thread used *before* when the model's
compaction compatibility hash changed or when the thread downshifts to a smaller
context window, and retries with the selected model when that attempt fails.
Go had none of it: no per-turn context record, no previous-turn settings, no way
to point a compaction at a specific model, and a compaction path that always
fell back to a local summary (which would have masked any model fallback).

### The divergence

- Rust persists one `TurnContextItem` per real user turn ("so resume/lazy replay
  can recover the latest durable baseline"), carrying `model` and
  `comp_hash`, and rebuilds `PreviousTurnSettings` from the last record during
  rollout reconstruction. **Go wrote no `turn_context` record at all** - its
  recorders only wrote `event_msg`/item lines, so a Go rollout had no per-turn
  context and a thread had no recorded previous model.
- `maybe_run_previous_model_inline_compact` runs before the token-status
  auto-compaction: a changed compaction hash triggers it outright (even for the
  same model), and a model switch triggers it when the previous model's window is
  larger, the models differ, and the downsized model's own limit is already
  reached. Rust passes a *fallback step context* for the selected model, and
  `run_remote_compact_task_inner_impl` retries the V2 attempt with it when
  `should_retry_with_current_model` holds (#46324: everything except
  `TurnAborted`, `Interrupted`, `SessionBudgetExceeded`), recording
  `codex.compaction.model_fallback` and returning the *original* error when the
  fallback also fails.

### Landed

- `rollout.TurnContextRecord` + `Recorder.AppendTurnContext` write the record
  (turn id, cwd, approval policy, sandbox policy, effort, personality, model,
  `comp_hash`), and `rollout.TurnContextSettings` parses the previous-turn
  settings back out (a record without a model is not usable, matching Rust's
  `Option` being absent).
- `RuntimeRouter.recordRuntimeTurnContext` persists one record per turn after the
  pre-turn compaction decision (so the next turn still sees the previous one) and
  keeps an in-memory payload per thread;
  `RuntimeRouter.runtimePreviousTurnSettings` answers from that payload, seeded
  from the rollout-reconstructed record for a resumed thread.
- `previousModelCompactionForTurn` implements both triggers, using
  `runtimeModelContextWindow` (explicit `model_context_window` override, then the
  catalog value - Rust's `TurnContext::model_context_window`) and the existing
  `compactTokenStatusForTurn` for the limit; the hash trigger is ignored when
  either side is missing (`comp_hash_changed`).
- `runPreviousModelInlineCompact` runs the pre-turn compaction with the previous
  model (`runtimeCompactRequest.Model`, threaded into
  `compactRunnerForRecord`), and on failure retries with the selected model when
  `shouldRetryCompactionWithCurrentModel` holds (context cancellation and
  `ErrSessionBudgetExceeded` stop it), records
  `codex.compaction.model_fallback` with reason/implementation/outcome tags, logs
  Rust's warning, and returns the original error if the fallback also fails.
- `runtimeCompactRequest.RemoteOnly` disables the local-summary fallback for
  these attempts when the provider supports remote compaction, because Rust's V2
  auto-compaction path retries with another model rather than summarizing
  locally.
- `compact.ReasonCompHashChanged` + the analytics mapping for
  `comp_hash_change`/`model_downshift` complete the reason surface (the
  `ReasonModelSwitch` producer existed without a caller).

### Evidence

- `rollout.TestAppendTurnContextRecordsPreviousTurnSettingsLikeRust` (record
  round-trip through `RecordFromPath`, including the loader applying the model and
  cwd to the thread metadata) and
  `TestTurnContextSettingsRejectsRecordsWithoutAModel`.
- `appserver.TestRuntimePreviousTurnSettingsFollowRecordedTurnContextLikeRust`
  (no record -> no settings; after recording -> the model and hash; a cold router
  reading the same rollout sees the same values, and the rollout file contains
  the `turn_context` record).
- `appserver.TestPreviousModelCompactionDecisionLikeRust` (six cases: hash change,
  missing hash with a downshift, same model/hash, previous window not larger,
  downsized model under its limit, downshift at the limit) and
  `TestPreviousModelCompactionSkipsThreadsWithoutRecordedContext`.
- `appserver.TestRunPreviousModelInlineCompactFallsBackLikeRust` (a retryable
  failure produces exactly two attempts; a canceled turn and a budget stop
  produce one attempt and surface the error) and
  `TestShouldRetryCompactionWithCurrentModelLikeRust`.
- `appserver.TestRuntimeTurnStartRunsPreviousModelCompactionLikeRust` drives real
  turns: the first turn's record is recovered as previous settings, and switching
  to a model with a different compaction hash compacts before the second turn
  samples. Verified to fail with the turn-path call removed
  (`previous-model compaction did not run for a changed compaction hash`).
- `appserver.TestRuntimeModelContextWindowPrefersConfigOverrideLikeRust`.
- `recordreplay`'s persisted-rollout cross-check now walks the `turn_context`
  record (Rust gives every paginated record an ordinal, so the record is part of
  the page) instead of failing the contiguity check.
- `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file; `go vet ./appserver/ ./rollout/
  ./compact/` clean; `go test ./appserver/ -count=1` green (74.5 s); `go test
  ./... -count=1` green (97 packages, no `FAIL`).

### Recorded nuances

- Rust captures a *speculative step context* for the fallback
  (`capture_current_model_fallback_step_context`, gated on Codex-backend auth and
  an OpenAI provider) and re-points the session's last-known step context before
  the retry; Go's compaction is not step-context-scoped, so the retry resolves
  the selected model from the record instead. The gate itself is therefore not
  modelled.
- The `implementation` tag on `codex.compaction.model_fallback` uses Go's
  existing `compactionAnalyticsImplementation` mapping ("responses"); a failed
  attempt yields no result, so the V2 split Rust reads from the attempt metadata
  is not available at that point.
- Rust's previous-model attempt takes its service tier from the previous step
  context; Go's `remoteCompactServiceTierForRecord` still derives the tier from
  the thread record, so a tier change in the same turn as a model switch keeps
  the record's tier for the previous-model attempt.
- The window comparison uses the thread's config override and the catalog value;
  Rust additionally consults the per-environment resolved profile, which Go's
  thread-level config does not carry.

## sync101: the Guardian prompt keeps the stable evidence before the action (#46279)

The Guardian-lane item the sync39 table recorded as "queued (behaviour lane)".

### The divergence

Rust composes the Guardian review input from ordered `ContextSection`s and
`#46279` moved the *volatile* sections (previous reviews 1->6, trusted tool 2->7,
trusted skills 3->8) **after** the stable ones (root conversation / sender
messages 1, retained instructions 2, trusted answers 3, transcript 4,
permissions 5), explicitly "so they do not invalidate the reusable history
prefix when previous decisions or tool evidence change". The planned action was
already last: `ContextSection::PlannedAction` renders at position 11, with the
invoked tool's descriptions as an optional item inside that same section, and
node-repl evidence at position 10.

Go's `state.BuildPromptWithOptions` emitted the sections in its own order - the
planned-action framing **first**, then tool descriptions, then node-repl
evidence, then the root-conversation evidence, then the transcript. Because the
prompt is the reviewer request's user message, the action (which changes on every
approval request) led the message, so no evidence prefix was ever reusable and
the section order matched neither Rust's current nor its pre-#46279 order.

### Landed

- `BuildPromptWithOptions` now emits the sections Go models in Rust's composed
  order: root-conversation evidence (position 1), transcript (4), node-repl
  evidence (10), then the planned action with its tool descriptions (11).

### Evidence

- `state.TestBuildPromptKeepsEvidenceBeforeTheActionLikeRust`: with root evidence
  and a transcript present, the section indices satisfy
  root < transcript < action, a changed action keeps the evidence prefix byte
  identical (`strings.HasPrefix(second, first[:actionIndex])`), and the action end
  marker follows the action framing. The ordering assertions fail with the
  previous section order.
- `state.TestBuildPromptFramingMatchesRust` still holds (with no evidence the
  action alone is the prompt, so the framing remains the prefix) and
  `TestBuildPromptAppendsGuardianToolDescriptionsLikeRust` still holds (the
  descriptions stay adjacent to the action they describe).
- `gofmt -l` clean on both touched files; `go test ./state/ -count=1` green
  (4.2 s); `go test ./appserver/ -count=1` green (75.9 s); `go test ./...
  -count=1` green (97 packages, no `FAIL`).

### Recorded remainders in this lane

- Rust's remaining section positions have no Go prompt counterpart: retained user
  instructions (2) and trusted user answers (3) need the retained-context model
  #46179 is blocked on, the permission context (5) reaches Go as the request's
  permission profile plus environment input items rather than a composed section,
  and previous reviews (6) plus trusted tool/skills (7/8) are absent because Go's
  reviewer reuses its own session response chain instead of replaying per-review
  evidence sections. Each would need its own producer before an ordering can be
  pinned.

## sync102: the F8 voice-conversation shortcut (#46071)

With the core lanes either landed or blocked on a missing Go-side model (see the
site checks above), this pass moves to the TUI lane, which the workflow orders
last. #46071 is the first item taken from it.

### The divergence

Rust binds `F8` to start or stop a voice conversation through the existing voice
toggle handler, exposes `tui.keymap.chat.toggle_voice` for remapping/unbinding,
lets the default yield to an existing `F8` binding or an overlapping chord
prefix, validates explicit bindings against the shortcut and reserved-text-input
conflicts, and dispatches the shortcut only on key presses with no modal or popup
active. Go's keymap had only `chat.toggle_voice_mute` (ctrl-x): there was no way
to start or stop a voice conversation from the keyboard at all, even though the
`/voice` command, the `OnVoiceConversationStart` hook, the voice strip, and the
keymap/config/validation machinery all exist.

### Landed

- `tui` keymap catalog: a `chat.toggle_voice` action ("Start or stop a voice
  conversation.", default `f8`), ordered next to `toggle_voice_mute` like Rust's
  chat keymap struct. Because the config's known contexts/actions are derived
  from the catalog, `tui.keymap.chat.toggle_voice` is now accepted, remappable,
  and unbindable.
- `tui/mainSurfaceBindingExists` generalizes the existing ctrl-x shadowing check;
  the new default is suppressed when a main-surface context already binds `f8`,
  so it never shadows a shortcut the user configured (Rust's
  `configured_main_surface_alias_is_used` arm for `f8`; Go has no chord model, so
  the chord-prefix half has no counterpart).
- `tui/tea`: an F8 branch in the main key handler dispatches to
  `toggleVoiceConversationFromKey`, which runs the same `applyVoiceCommand("")`
  handler as `/voice` and refreshes the status controls. The branch sits after
  the modal early-return and the skill/slash popup handlers, so an open modal or
  popup consumes the key first (Rust's "no modal or popup" rule), and it never
  touches the composer, preserving the draft.

### Evidence

- `tui.TestVoiceToggleDefaultBinding` (default `f8`, custom remap, unbind),
  `TestVoiceToggleDefaultYieldsToMainSurfaceF8` (all seven main-surface contexts
  shadow the default), and `TestVoiceToggleBindingConflictsAreRejected`
  (`Validate()` names `toggle_voice` when it shares a key with another chat
  action).
- `tui/tea.TestModelVoiceToggleKeyRunsTheVoiceHandlerLikeRust` (the F8 notice and
  phase equal the `/voice` command's, and the composer draft survives) and
  `TestModelVoiceToggleKeyFollowsKeymapLikeRust` (a remap to `f9` makes F8 inert
  and `f9` work; unbinding makes the key inert; a `global.copy` F8 binding wins so
  the voice handler does not run). Both were verified to fail with the dispatch
  branch removed (`F8 notice = "", /voice notice = "Voice runtime is not installed
  ..."`).
- `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file; `go test ./tui/... -count=1` green;
  `go test ./... -count=1` green (97 packages, no `FAIL`).

### Recorded nuances

- The default's shadowing check reads configured bindings only, matching Go's
  existing ctrl-x helper; Rust additionally considers chord prefixes, which Go's
  keymap config does not model.
- Go's voice start path depends on `voicehost.IsSupported()` (packaged helper), so
  in a build without the helper both `/voice` and F8 report the bounded
  "Voice runtime is not installed" message; the tests assert the shortcut drives
  exactly that shared handler rather than a helper-specific success path.

## sync103: Guardian inference routes through `/responses` with identifying headers (#45736)

### The sweep that found it

Recomputing the plan's coverage over `5b1d656018..595cc91e8c` (322 commits) still
leaves **50 untriaged PRs**. Most are Rust-internal crate moves (Guardian
reviewer/context extraction `#45418`-`#45985`, analytics dashboards, SDK and
Windows sandbox service work), but `#45736` is Go-visible: Go still implemented
the pre-#45736 routing. `#45684` ("Pass `ReviewModel` through guardian review
sessions") was checked in the same sweep and is a pure refactor of
`GuardianReviewSessionConfig`/`Params` - the selection inputs, the seed model,
and every observable request field are unchanged, so it has no Go delta.

### The divergence

Rust replaced the dedicated Guardian endpoints: `codex_api::ResponsesEndpoint`
lost its Guardian variants, and a review now goes to the standard `/responses`
route carrying `x-codex-guardian: reviewer` (the asynchronous classifier uses
`classifier`). The header is seeded on the reviewer session for
`provider.approval_review_preferred_model()` and applied only while the request's
model still equals that seed, the auth uses the Codex backend, and the provider
supports the backend routes; the client rechecks auth per attempt, sends the
header on HTTP and on the WebSocket handshake, and reconnects a cached socket
when the applicable headers change. `features.guardianv2.free_guardian` is
retained for configuration compatibility but no longer gates routing (the
backend controls billing), the reviewer request drops its service tier, and
`guardian_credits_requested` is attached to non-reviewer Codex-backend traffic.

Go implemented the old design end to end: `ResponsesEndpointGuardian` /
`ResponsesEndpointGuardianClassifier` with `/guardian` and `/guardian-classifier`
paths, a `FreeGuardianEnabled` routing gate for `/guardian`, and a
`Config.FreeGuardianEnabled()` accessor.

### Landed

- `model.ResponsesEndpoint` collapsed to the standard route (the guardian and
  classifier variants and their paths are gone), so every inference - including a
  review - posts to `/responses`.
- `ResponsesAgentRunner.isGuardianReviewerInference` implements Rust's
  `responses_headers` predicate: Guardian review task/originator, request model
  equal to the provider's preferred review model, Codex backend auth, and
  provider routes supported. `GuardianHeaderName`/`GuardianHeaderReviewer`
  ("x-codex-guardian"/"reviewer") are the new constants.
- The HTTP path sets the header, clears the service tier **before** the body is
  serialized (the previous endpoint-based clear ran after the marshal, so it
  never reached the body), and keeps the routing-hint suppression on the same
  predicate. The WebSocket path inherits the handshake header from the same
  request, and the cached-socket key gained a reviewer dimension so a review
  cannot reuse the reviewed thread's ordinary connection.
- Non-reviewer Codex-backend requests attach `guardian_credits_requested=true`
  (`withGuardianCreditsRequested`), mirroring Rust's `set_guardian_metadata` arm.
- `ResponsesAgentOptions.FreeGuardianEnabled`, its app-server wiring, and
  `Config.FreeGuardianEnabled()` are removed (Rust deleted the accessor too); the
  `features.guardianv2.free_guardian` key still parses, so existing config keeps
  loading.

### Evidence

- `model.TestResponsesAgentRunnerGuardianRoutingLikeRust`: a preferred-model
  review over Codex backend auth stays on `/responses`, carries
  `x-codex-guardian: reviewer`, drops the routing hint and the serialized
  `service_tier`, and does **not** ask for Guardian credits; a review whose model
  was overridden to something else carries no header and keeps its routing hint;
  an ordinary request has no header, keeps `model=gpt-test`, and does ask for
  Guardian credits; API-key auth and a non-backend base URL produce unmarked
  review requests. Verified to fail with the predicate forced false
  (`guardian header = "", want "reviewer"`).
- `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file; `go vet ./model/` unchanged
  (the two `copylocks` findings recorded in sync92 are pre-existing);
  `go test ./model/ -count=1` green (17.3 s); `go test ./appserver/ -count=1`
  green (75.0 s); `go test ./... -count=1` green (97 packages, no `FAIL`).

### Recorded observations

- Rust derives `guardian_reviewer` from the header, so a review whose *selected*
  model is an override (the seed is always the preferred review model) loses both
  the marking and `parent_response_id`, and regains a routing hint. Go's
  app-server attaches `parent_response_id` for every review, which is the
  behaviour a review should have; reproducing Rust's coupling exactly would mean
  making the reviewer metadata depend on the preferred-model comparison in the
  app-server as well. Recorded rather than hard-coded, because the mismatch looks
  incidental to #45736 rather than stated intent.
- Rust's credits arm also requires `!is_basic_session_source`, which is true only
  for Guardian reviewer sessions; Go has no session-source concept in the model
  layer, so the predicate uses the review check directly - equivalent for the
  request kinds Go models.
- Go has no asynchronous Guardian classifier, so `x-codex-guardian: classifier`
  has no producer, and the request-metric endpoint label is now always
  `/responses` (Rust deleted the endpoint variants).

## sync104: file images are charged in context estimates and survive compaction (#46072)

The second item taken from the untriaged sweep recorded in sync103.

### The divergence

Rust's `estimate_image_reference_bytes` distinguishes the two image reference
forms: an inline data URL keeps the existing estimate, while a **file-backed**
reference costs `RESIZED_IMAGE_BYTES_ESTIMATE` or - because the reference exposes
no dimensions - `approx_bytes_for_tokens(ORIGINAL_IMAGE_MAX_PATCHES)`
(10,000 patch tokens) when the request asks for `original` detail. Before
#46072 Rust charged file images **zero** in both the message and the
function-output arms; that was the bug the commit fixed.

Go had the same shape plus one more: `compact.EstimateItemTokens` estimated a
`message` item from its *text* only (`EstimateTextTokens(ItemText(item))`), so an
image in a user message - inline or file-backed - contributed **zero**, and
`compact.ContentPart` had no file reference at all, so `compactItemsFromSessionItems`
/ `sessionItemsFromCompactItems` silently dropped a part's `file_id` and a
compaction could not retain the reference.

### Landed

- `compact.ContentPart` gained `FileID`, carried in both directions by the
  session <-> compaction converters in `appserver/router.go`.
- `compactImageReferenceBytes` implements Rust's rule: inline and
  non-original-detail file references cost the resized estimate (7373 bytes),
  while a file reference with `original` detail costs
  `compactOriginalImageMaxPatches * 4` (40,000 bytes = 10,000 tokens, Rust's
  `approx_bytes_for_tokens`).
- `EstimateItemTokens` gained a `message` arm that charges the message's content
  blocks through the same helper, so a message image is counted for history and
  compaction estimates (the tool-call and tool-output arms already funnelled
  through `compactItemContentBytes`).

### Evidence

- `compact.TestEstimateItemTokensChargesMessageImagesLikeRust`: inline image,
  file image, file image with `auto` detail and file image with `original` detail
  (10,000 tokens); text+image bytes add up in one message; a text-only message
  keeps the plain text estimate; a tool output charges a file image through the
  same rule. Verified to fail with the message arm disabled (`EstimateItemTokens()
  = 0, want 1844` and `= 0, want 10000`).
- `appserver.TestCompactItemsPreserveFileImageReferencesLikeRust`: a session item
  with a `file_id` image survives the conversion both ways and the converted item
  estimates 10,000 tokens.
- `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file; `go test ./compact/ -count=1` green;
  `go test ./appserver/ -count=1` green (75.0 s); `go test ./... -count=1` green
  (97 packages, no `FAIL`).

### Recorded remainders in this lane

- #46072's Guardian half - "preserve file images from user messages and tool
  outputs through Guardian selection and delivery, deduplicate review images by
  file ID, and charge them against the Guardian request budget" - needs the
  `guardian-context` image-selection/budget model (`images.rs`, `budget.rs`,
  `enforcement.rs`). Go's reviewer only admits node-repl evidence images
  (`MultimodalInputItems`) and has no transcript image selection, count/byte
  limits, or per-request Guardian budget, so this is the same class of blocker as
  the retained-context lane recorded for #46179.
- Rust also computes an *exact* estimate for inline `original`-detail images by
  decoding the data URL and counting patches against a cache
  (`estimate_original_image_bytes`). Go approximates every inline image with the
  resized estimate; that predates #46072 (which only added the file case) and
  needs an image decoder plus a cache to match.

## sync105: one shared three-row tool-output preview (#46492, superseding #46073)

The TUI item the sync39 table listed as "queued (TUI)". The sweep in sync103
surfaced `#46073` ("Bound code-mode output previews across result blocks") first,
but `#46492` ("Unify TUI tool output previews with a three-row limit") is the
later commit in the same range and replaces that ad-hoc budget with a shared
renderer, so Go was aligned to the current upstream shape.

### The divergence

Rust renders compact tool-output previews through `tui::tool_output`:
`PREVIEW_LINES = 3`, a 16 KiB input bound per logical line, hard-wrapping (so a
long URL cannot exceed the budget), and a `+N lines (ctrl + t to view transcript)`
marker; a partially displayed logical line counts as hidden and every later line
is hidden with it. Both the exec cell (agent commands) and the MCP tool-call cell
use it, and the transcript keeps the complete output.

Go had three different previews:

- `tui/exec_cell` agent commands went through `OutputLinesFor` (a head/tail split
  with a `ToolCallMaxLines` limit and a different marker) plus
  `truncateOutputLinesMiddle`;
- the MCP tool-call cell applied a **per result block** 50 logical-line cap and
  appended a bare `"... truncated"` line, so several blocks could each keep 50
  lines and a trailing failure diagnostic could be dropped;
- nothing bounded *rendered* rows, so wrapped text and long URLs exceeded any
  budget.

### Landed

- `tui/tool_output_preview.go`: `ToolOutputPreview` / `ToolOutputPreviewLines`
  mirror `ToolOutputPreview` / `tool_output_preview` - the shared three-row
  budget, the 16 KiB per-line input bound applied on a rune boundary, grapheme
  aware column bounding, hard-wrapped rows, and the hidden-line marker truncated
  to the preview width (`PreviewLines`, `MaxPreviewLineBytes`, `TranscriptHint`).
- `tui/exec_cell`: agent command output uses the shared preview (three wrapped
  rows plus the marker, with the four-column tree prefix reserved), while user
  shell commands keep their 50-line path.
- `tui/history_cell/mcp.go`: the compact preview collects the rows of every result
  block and applies one shared budget (paired with the tree-prefix width), the
  error detail is bounded the same way, and `truncateToolResultLines` (the
  per-block cap with its own marker) is gone. `TranscriptLines`/`RawLines` still
  render every block in full.

### Evidence

- `tui.TestToolOutputPreview*`: leading rows with the hidden count (singular and
  plural), wrapped rows consuming budget and a partially displayed line being
  reported hidden while its leading rows stay visible, the 16 KiB/column input
  bound on a long line, and the marker truncated to a narrow width.
- `tui/history_cell.TestMcpToolCallPreviewSharesOneBudgetLikeRust` (three rows
  shown, `+3 lines` reported, rows past the budget absent, and both transcript and
  raw output keeping all six lines) and
  `TestMcpToolCallTranscriptKeepsTrailingFailureLikeRust`.
- `tui/exec_cell.TestExecCellDisplayUsesSharedThreeRowPreviewLikeRust` (three
  preview rows plus the marker, `+3 lines`, and a transcript without the hint).
- Both wirings were verified to fail with the old code restored: the exec path
  produced `… +2 lines` with a head/tail layout, and the MCP path printed all six
  rows with no marker at all.
- `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file; `go test ./tui/... -count=1` green;
  `go test ./... -count=1` green (97 packages, no `FAIL`).

### Recorded remainders in this lane

- #46492 also deleted `tui/src/text_formatting.rs` and adjusted
  `history_cell/mcp_result.rs`; Go has no `text_formatting` module and its MCP
  result rendering lives in the same `history_cell/mcp.go`, so nothing else needed
  rewiring.
- Go keeps its own markers where Rust kept them: the user-shell exec path still
  uses `OutputLinesFor` with `OutputEllipsisText`, and `tui/history_cell/patches.go`
  keeps its `… +N lines (ctrl + t to view transcript)` line for the patch cell,
  which #46492 did not touch.

## sync106: server version notices cover prerelease and local clients (#46673)

### Upstream check (third pull of 2026-09-19)

`git pull origin main` in `D:\qax\reagent\dev\git\codex` fast-forwarded
`595cc91e8c..a5290028a2` (1 commit: #46673 "Extend server version notices to
prerelease and local clients"). The parity layer's snapshot manifest needed a
re-pin for the two new history-cell snapshots (done below).

### The divergence

Rust's notice policy required **both** versions to be strict stable triplets, so
a prerelease or local client never heard about a differing background service.
`#46673` replaced `is_official_server_older` with
`server_version_notice_kind(client, server) -> Option<{Older, Different}>`:

- both versions must parse as semantic versions (malformed versions produce no
  notice at all);
- a local client (`0.0.0`, or any version with build metadata) compares identity,
  so a differing build reads as a mismatch rather than an ordering;
- a prerelease client only orders within its own release line; another release
  line is a mismatch;
- otherwise the server must be released (no build metadata, not `0.0.0`) and the
  client must have higher semantic precedence, which is what makes a stable
  client order correctly against a prerelease server.

Go mirrored only the old shape: `IsOfficialServerOlder` compared strict stable
triplets, and `tui/status.ServerVersionNoticeMessage` had a single "older than"
wording, so local and prerelease clients were silent.

### Landed

- `tui.ServerVersionNoticeKind` (`Older`/`Different`) and
  `ServerVersionNoticeKindFor` implement the policy above.
  `parseSemanticVersion` validates through `golang.org/x/mod/semver` (already a
  repository dependency) and extracts the release tuple, prerelease, build
  metadata, and the full version text used for identity comparison;
  `compareSemanticPrecedence` uses the same package's precedence comparison.
  `IsOfficialServerOlder` is gone, matching Rust.
- `tui/status`: `ServerVersionNoticeMessage` reads "older than" or "different
  from" from the kind, and `ServerVersionNoticeKey`/`PendingServerVersionNotice`
  now exist whenever a kind exists, so a mismatch notice deduplicates through the
  same endpoint/server-home identity.
- `parity`: the TUI snapshot manifest is re-pinned to `a5290028` (total
  1175 -> 1177, `tui/src/history_cell/snapshots` 77 -> 79) and requires the two
  new `local_daemon_alpha_mismatch` / `local_daemon_source_mismatch` snapshots.

### Evidence

- `tui.TestStableClientsOnlyWarnForOlderReleasesLikeRust`,
  `TestPrereleaseClientsCompareWithinTheSameReleaseLineLikeRust`,
  `TestPrereleaseAndLocalClientsWarnForMismatchesLikeRust`, and
  `TestUnknownOrMalformedVersionsDoNotProduceNoticesLikeRust` reproduce Rust's
  three policy tables plus its malformed-version list (`dev`, `0.0.0.0`,
  `0.153`, `0.153.0.1`, `" 0.153.0"`, `+0.153.0`, `0.0153.0`,
  `0.153.0-alpha.01`, `0.153.0-alpha..1`).
- `tui/status.TestServerVersionNoticeUsesClientReleasePolicyLikeRust` covers the
  older wording, the local-client mismatch wording, the prerelease same-line and
  other-line cases, and the nil/bulid-metadata silences; the dedup test gained a
  local-client mismatch case (notice retained, key suppressed on repeat).
- Verified to be meaningful: forcing the local/prerelease branch off fails both
  suites (`notice("0.155.0-alpha.23", "0.156.0") = "", want different` and the
  status notice test).
- `gofmt -l` clean; `go vet ./tui/ ./tui/status/` clean; `go test ./tui/
  ./tui/status/ ./parity/ -count=1` green; `go test ./... -count=1` green
  (97 packages, no `FAIL`).

### Recorded remainders in this lane

- Rust also updates the agents-overview notice and its `<`/`≠` indicator
  (`app/server_version_notice.rs`, `Status v… < Codex CLI v… · /daemon`). Go has
  no agents-overview server-version notice at all - no such view-state field, no
  `Service v… < Codex CLI v…` string, and no `/daemon` slash command or menu - so
  that half has no landing site until the overview notice feature itself (and the
  `/daemon` surface) is ported.
- `#46673` only rewrote the description of `tui.show_server_version_notice`; the
  setting itself is not modelled in Go, which is part of the same overview-notice
  lane rather than this policy change.

## Site checks: #46010 and #46051 (the websocket / tool-result-metadata lane)

Two sweep candidates were traced to their Go seams so the next pass starts from
evidence. Both are real gaps, and they share one missing Go concept.

### #46010 "Enable app tool result metadata with analytics controls"

- **Capture gate.** Rust captures the raw MCP `_meta` onto the executed-tool-call
  record when the analytics client is enabled *and* the prepared call is a
  host-owned apps call (`PreparedMcpCall::is_host_owned_apps`), where it used to
  be hardcoded `false`. Go has the whole metadata model -
  `model.ToolResultMetadata` with the bounded snapshot and `NewToolResultMetadata`
  / `SetToolResultMetadata` / `HasToolResultMetadata` - and
  `turn.ExecutedToolCallRecorder.RecordToolResultMetadata` (its comment says "MCP
  capture is disabled today"), but **no production caller**: `git grep
  RecordToolResultMetadata` matches only the definition and its tests, so an
  analytics-enabled Go session never records the internal `tool_result_metadata`.
  Landing it needs `turn.ToolRegistryOptions` to carry the recorder plus the
  thread's analytics enablement (`appserver.analyticsEnabledOptionForThread`),
  `turn.registerMCPToolSet` to gate on `mcp.IsCodexAppsMCPServerName(info.ServerName)`
  and that enablement, `mcp.ToolExecutorOptions` to take the flag (and a recorder
  callback), and `mcp.ToolExecutor` to invoke it where it still holds the raw
  `MCPToolCallResponse.Meta` (next to `mcpToolResponseData`). Model visibility is
  a separate, already-landed surface: Go's `data["_meta"]` is stripped per
  destination by #44336's `filterToolResultMetadataForDestination` /
  `clearToolResultMetadataInPromptItem`, so this lane is about the internal record
  only.
- **Websocket reuse guard.** Rust disables *incremental* request reuse when the
  raw result metadata or its call binding changed (`has_same_tool_result_metadata`
  inside `response_items_equal_ignoring_internal_metadata`). Go has no
  incremental reuse at all: `runWebSocket` reuses only the socket from
  `websocketSession(request).conn` and always writes the full `apiRequest.Input`
  (`websocketResponseCreatePayload`), with `connectionReused` reporting dial vs
  reuse. The guard therefore has no Go landing site, and adding one would first
  require porting the delta/incremental continuation path.

### #46051 "Track WebSocket continuation modes and full-input send reasons"

The commit adds the `codex.websocket.continuation` counter over every
`response.create` attempt, tagged with `mode` (incremental continuation, fresh
request, restored history, closed connection, other full-input send), `reason`
and `phase`, keeping the first reset reason until the next send. Go's websocket
telemetry records `connectionReused` plus the recovery mode/phase
(`recordWebsocketRequestRecord`), which is the same distinction it can currently
observe: because Go never sends an incremental continuation, it has no
continuation *mode* or reset *reason* to classify. This is the same missing piece
as the #46010 guard, so the two belong to one lane: port the reuse/delta decision
(request-property comparison, reset reasons, warmup and generation tracking), then
expose the counter tags.


## sync107: hosted apps tool-result metadata is captured on analytics-enabled sessions (#46010)

The first landing from the site checks above: the capture gate that the
app-server already had the model and recorder for, but no production caller of.

### The divergence

Rust's `McpHandler::handle_call` computed `result_metadata_capture_allowed` as
`analytics_events_client.is_enabled() && prepared_mcp_call.is_host_owned_apps()`,
and `McpToolOutput::tool_result_metadata()` returned the raw `CallToolResult.meta`
only under that flag. The recorded call then received it in one of two places:
`tools/parallel.rs` attached it to the prepared direct call before
`attach_direct_call_to_output`, and `ExecutedToolCalls::record_accepted_result`
did the same for a CodeMode result. Before #46010 the flag was hardcoded `false`,
so nothing was ever captured.

Go already had every piece except the wiring: `model.ToolResultMetadata` with the
bounded snapshot, `turn.ExecutedToolCallRecorder.RecordToolResultMetadata` (whose
comment said "MCP capture is disabled today"), and the dispatcher's direct-call
attachment. No production caller existed - `git grep RecordToolResultMetadata`
matched only the definition and its tests.

### Landed

- `mcp.ToolExecutorOptions.CaptureResultMetadata` carries the gate, and
  `mcp.ToolExecutor.Execute` reports the raw `MCPToolCallResponse.Meta` on
  `tool.Output.ToolResultMetadata` only when it is set. The new field is never
  serialized (`json:"-"`): the model-visible `_meta` is a separate, already-landed
  surface (#44336 strips it per destination), and this one exists only for the
  internal executed-tool-call record.
- `turn.mcpResultMetadataCaptureAllowed` is Rust's `result_metadata_capture_allowed`:
  the thread's effective analytics state (`options.AnalyticsEnabled`, nil meaning
  no session analytics context) plus `mcp.IsCodexAppsMCPServerName`. Recording the
  exposed snapshot stays a second check at the dispatch sites, exactly like Rust's
  own recorder-state check, so the gate function has no third condition.
- `turn.registerMCPToolSet` resolves the flag per tool from its server name, and
  `appserver.toolRouterForTurnContext` supplies the thread's analytics state
  (`analyticsEnabledOptionForThread`), which is the same value the turn metadata
  already reports.
- `turn.ToolDispatcher.completeDirectCall` attaches the metadata its invocation
  exposed to that invocation's own recorded call before the record reaches the
  output, and the nested Code Mode completion hook records it through
  `RecordToolResultMetadata` before the lifecycle reporting/early return - the two
  Rust sites above.

### Evidence

- `appserver.TestRuntimeRouterMCPResultMetadataFollowsAnalyticsLikeRust` runs a
  real turn against an in-process HTTP MCP server that answers `tools/call` with
  `_meta`, three ways: a hosted-apps server with analytics enabled (the follow-up
  request's tool output carries `tool_result_metadata` with the provider payload),
  the same server with a disabled analytics client (no record), and an external
  `sdk` server with analytics enabled (no record). It also checks the
  `item/completed` notification for the call, which must not leak the raw
  metadata. Verified to fail with the app-server wiring removed
  (`recorded result metadata present = false, want true`) and with the analytics
  conjunct removed (`true, want false`).
- `turn.TestMCPResultMetadataCaptureAllowedLikeRust` pins the predicate matrix
  (analytics enabled/disabled/absent x apps/other/empty server).
- `turn.TestToolDispatcherRecordsResultMetadataLikeRust` covers both dispatch
  halves: a direct call attaches the metadata its handler exposed, and a nested
  Code Mode result lands on the pending nested call.
- `mcp.TestToolExecutorResultMetadataCaptureLikeRust` covers the executor seam
  (allowed, disallowed, nil response, response without `_meta`).
- `gofmt -l` clean on every touched file; `go vet ./turn/ ./appserver/` clean;
  `go test ./... -count=1` green (97 packages, no `FAIL`).

### Recorded observations in this lane

- Rust evaluates ownership at call time from the prepared call's binding ("a later
  catalog refresh must not change eligibility"). Go resolves it once per turn from
  the registered tool's server name, which is the same value the turn's MCP
  executor already uses for its other apps-only behaviour; a mid-turn catalog
  refresh cannot retarget a registered spec, so the two agree.
- Rust's `PreparedMcpCall::is_host_owned_apps` additionally requires a local MCP
  environment and a Compatibility/Extension (`host_owned_apps: true`) source. Go's
  `mcp.IsCodexAppsMCPServerName` matches the three spellings of the hosted apps
  server and does not model that registration source, which is the same
  simplification every other Go apps-only site already makes.
- Rust's second half of #46010 - `has_same_tool_result_metadata` disabling
  *incremental* WebSocket request reuse when the raw metadata or its call binding
  changed - has no Go landing site, re-confirmed here: `runWebSocket` reuses only
  the dialed socket and always writes the full `apiRequest.Input`
  (`websocketResponseCreatePayload` never takes a delta). The guard is a no-op
  until the incremental-continuation path itself is ported, which is the same
  missing piece the #46051 counter needs (see the site-check lane above).

### Newly recorded lane: the exec surface has no executed-tool-call recorder

Rust builds the recorder in the core session (`core/src/session/session.rs:1621`),
so `codex exec` records executed tool calls - and, under #46010, the apps result
metadata - whenever the feature and analytics are on. Go wires the recorder only
in the app-server (`appserver/turn_runtime.go` passes
`ExecutedToolCallMetadataEnabled` from the feature and
`RuntimeOptions.ExecutedToolCalls` from the router). `exec/exec.go` builds its
runtime at `runAgentTurn` without either: `git grep -n "ExecutedToolCallMetadataEnabled\|ExecutedToolCalls" exec/` matches nothing, and the exec tool registry (`exec/exec.go:1215`) sets neither the recorder nor `AnalyticsEnabled`. So an `codex exec` run with `executed_tool_call_metadata = true` records nothing, and its apps calls can never satisfy the #46010 gate. Landing it needs the exec run to own a per-thread recorder, pass the feature state through `AgentLoopRequest`, and expose an analytics state the registry gate can read (exec has no session analytics client in Go today - `git grep -n Analytics exec/` matches only the OTEL default).

## Upstream check: #46680 (TUI contrast, keyboard hints, pickers) - parity re-pin, behaviour queued

A fourth `git pull origin main` in the Rust checkout fast-forwarded
`a5290028a2..132c2be239` (1 commit, 199 files, +3423/-1149): #46680 "Improve TUI
contrast, keyboard hints, and picker layouts". It is a TUI-only change, and the
skills put TUI after the core surfaces, so this pass re-pins the static layer and
records the behaviour as the next TUI lane instead of half-porting it.

### Landed (static layer)

- `parity.TestRustTUISnapshotManifestCoversPrioritySurfaces` is re-pinned to
  `132c2be2`: the TUI snapshot total is 1177 -> 1184, with `tui/src/bottom_pane/
  snapshots` 239 -> 244 (the two picker-browser layouts and the two filled-tab
  windows), `tui/src/history_cell/snapshots` 79 -> 80 (the user-image prompt

  the user-image labels, and the diff syntax snapshot). Directory inventory and
  every other per-directory count are unchanged, and no other `parity`,

### Queued: the TUI lane this commit opens

almost one to one, so the lane has concrete sites:

- `style.rs` (`readable_color_on`, `secondary_text_style`) plus the new
  `style/contrast.rs` and `color.rs` (`is_light`, `blend`) - contrast-aware
  foregrounds that account for a painted background colour and the terminal's
  colour depth, with terminal-default fallbacks - land in `tui/style.go`,
  `tui/color.go`, `tui/terminal_palette.go` and `tui/terminal_default_colors*.go`,
  which today have `AccentStyleFor` / `UserMessageBackground` but no contrast
  correction.
- `key_hint.rs` (compact `ctrl+c`-style labels, one styled span per complete
  shortcut, `key_hint_label_tests.rs`) lands in `tui/tea/key_hint.go`, where

- `bottom_pane/picker_style.rs`, `selection_picker_layout.rs`,

  picker presentation (full-width selection, filled tabs, separate overflow

- `diff_render.rs` (354 lines) and `render/highlight.rs` (no dimming of
  syntax-highlighted diff text) land in `tui/diff_render.go` and

  `tui/status_indicator_widget.go`, `tui/terminal_palette.go`,
